1 Fundamentals of Letter Writing

1.1 What is a letter?

DefinitionA letter is a written message that one person or office sends to another person, office or organisation - by post, by hand or by email. It conveys information, a request, a feeling or a decision in an accepted format. It also remains as a permanent record of what was said.
In one lineA letter is a written, addressed, purposeful and permanent message that follows a fixed format.

Key features

FeatureWhat it meansExam point
WrittenIt is written or typed, not spoken.Spelling, grammar and a neat layout matter as much as the content.
Addressed to someoneIt has a definite receiver - a person, an office or an organisation.The receiver decides the register, the salutation and the close.
PurposefulIt is written for a clear reason - to request, complain, apply, inform, thank or share news.In a formal letter, state the purpose in the first paragraph.
Permanent recordIt can be kept, filed, quoted later and used as proof.Give exact facts - dates, numbers, amounts - and list the enclosures.
Follows a formatEach type has a fixed layout and a fixed order of parts.The format is part of the answer - learn the order of parts (see 1.7).

1.2 Purpose of letter writing

PurposeExample letter
To share news and feelingsLetter to a friend describing your first week in the hostel
To applyApplication for a job, admission, scholarship or leave
To requestRequest to the Principal for a bonafide certificate
To complain and seek actionComplaint to the Municipal Commissioner about irregular garbage collection
To ask for informationEnquiry to a coaching centre about course fees and batch timings
To buy or sell goodsOrder letter to a supplier for 500 notebooks
To informLetter to a bank about a change of address
To invite, thank, congratulate or consoleInvitation to a friend for a wedding; condolence letter to a friend
To express an opinion on a public issueLetter to the editor about traffic congestion in Chennai
To keep an official recordAppointment letter; letter confirming a telephone conversation

1.3 Types of letters - overview

CategoryWritten toRegisterExamples
Personal / informal (see 2.1)Family, relatives, close friendsInformalLetter to parents; invitation to a friend; congratulations; condolence
Semi-formal (see 2.2)People you know, but not closely - teacher, landlord, neighbour, a known officialSemi-formalRequest to a landlord for repairs; thank-you letter to a former teacher
Formal / official (see 2.3)Officials, offices, institutions, editors, unknown personsFormalComplaint to the Municipal Commissioner; leave letter; letter to the editor
Business (Section 3)Firms, suppliers, customersFormalInquiry, quotation, order, complaint, claim, adjustment, credit and collection letters
Banking / financial (Section 4)Banks and financial institutions (customer ↔ bank)FormalNew cheque book; stop-payment request; loan application; change of address; closing an account
Transactional (Section 5)The other party in one deal (buyer ↔ seller, customer ↔ service provider)FormalThe chain of one transaction: inquiry → quotation → order → dispatch → invoice → payment
Government / administrative (Section 6)Government offices, officials, other departments, the publicFormalOfficial letter; D.O. letter; Office Memorandum; Circular; Notification; G.O.

EXAM TIP: First ask "WHO will read this letter?" The receiver decides the category, the register, the salutation and the close.

1.4 Personal vs official letters

BasisPersonal letterOfficial letter
Written toFamily, relatives, friendsOfficials, offices, organisations, unknown persons
PurposeShare news, feelings and personal mattersApply, request, complain or inform - an official or business purpose
Tone and languageWarm and casual; contractions allowedPolite, impersonal and precise; no contractions, no slang
LayoutShort address, date, salutation, body, close, first nameFixed order: sender's address, date, receiver's address, subject, salutation, body, close, name, Encl.
Receiver's address and subjectNot writtenBoth written
Salutation → Close"Dear Priya," → "Your loving friend,""Dear Sir/Madam," → "Yours faithfully,"
Length and contentFree and flexible; personal details welcomeBrief and to the point; facts only
Name at the endFirst name onlyFull name, with designation if any

MUST REMEMBER: NO CONTRACTIONS in formal letters. Write "I am", "do not", "cannot", "will not", "I would" - never "I'm", "don't", "can't", "won't", "I'd". Also no slang, no exclamation marks, no SMS language ("pls", "u", "thx") and no emoji. Contractions are allowed only in informal letters.

1.5 Formal vs semi-formal vs informal - at a glance

PointFormalSemi-formalInformal
Written toOfficials, offices, unknown personsKnown persons, not close - teacher, landlord, neighbourFamily and close friends
Receiver's addressYes - begins with "To"Yes - name and addressNo
Subject lineYes - always in exam lettersOptionalNo
Salutation → Close"Dear Sir/Madam," → "Yours faithfully,""Dear Mr. Raman," → "Yours sincerely,""Dear Priya," → "Your loving friend,"
Name at the endFull name (with designation, if any)Full nameFirst name only

This is only a summary. The detailed high-yield comparison table is in Section 12.

MUST REMEMBER: The salutation and the close are a PAIR. "Dear Sir/Madam," / "Sir," / "Respected Sir," → "Yours faithfully," (official or unknown receiver). "Dear Mr. Raman," (a named person, in a formal or semi-formal letter) → "Yours sincerely,". "Dear Priya," / "Dear Appa," → "Your loving friend," / "Your loving son,". Write "Yours" with a capital Y and the next word with a small letter, with no apostrophe and with a comma after it.

MEMORY TRICK: "Sir" and "sincerely" never sit together. No name (Sir/Madam) → faithfully; a name (Mr. Raman) → sincerely.

1.6 Anatomy of a formal letter HIGH-FREQUENCY

Model: a complaint to the Municipal Commissioner in full-block, exam style. Each number marks one part; the legend below explains it.

1 12, Gandhi Road, K.K. Nagar, Madurai - 625 020 2 12 October 2026 3 To The Commissioner, Madurai City Municipal Corporation, Madurai - 625 001 4 Subject: Complaint regarding non-functioning street lights in K.K. Nagar 5 Reference: My complaint No. 4521 dated 14 September 2026 6 Dear Sir/Madam, 7 I am a resident of Gandhi Road, K.K. Nagar. I am writing to bring to your notice that the street lights in our area have still not been repaired, although I reported the problem in my complaint cited above. 8 For the past four weeks, most of the street lights on Gandhi Road and Nehru Street have not worked after sunset. The dark streets are unsafe for women, students and elderly people, and there is a risk of accidents and theft. I enclose a copy of my earlier complaint and photographs of the affected streets. 9 I request you to have the street lights repaired at the earliest and to arrange regular checks in future. Thank you for your time. 10 Yours faithfully, 11 R. Karthik 12 Encl.: 1. Copy of my complaint No. 4521 dated 14 September 2026 2. Photographs of the affected streets (4 nos.) Copy to: The Assistant Engineer (Electrical), Madurai City Municipal Corporation

Legend - the 12 parts

No.PartWhere it goesRule to remember
1Sender's addressTop, at the left margin - the first blockAddress only, NO name. No address in the question? Write "Examination Hall".
2DateBelow the sender's address, after one blank line"12 October 2026" - month in words; never all-numeric.
3Receiver's addressBelow the date; "To" on its own line, then the addressDesignation first ("The Commissioner,"). A comma at the end of each line except the last; no full stop after the PIN code.
4SubjectBelow the receiver's address, before the salutation"Subject:" + a short noun phrase; no full stop. "Sub:" is also accepted.
5ReferenceBelow the subject - only when neededOnly when you refer to an earlier letter, notice or complaint; give its number and date.
6SalutationBelow the subject / reference"Dear Sir/Madam," with a comma. It decides the close.
7Opening paragraphFirst paragraph of the bodySay WHY you are writing, in one or two sentences.
8Main paragraph (body)Middle of the letterFacts: what, where, since when, what effect. One idea per paragraph.
9Closing paragraphLast paragraph of the bodyThe action you want + a polite line of thanks. No separate "Thanking you," line in our model letters.
10Complimentary closeBelow the body, at the left margin"Yours faithfully," pairs with "Dear Sir/Madam,". Capital Y, small f, no apostrophe, comma after.
11Signature and nameDirectly below the closeSign above the name in a real letter. In the exam, use the given name or "ABC" / "XYZ".
12Enclosure and copyLast lines, at the left margin"Encl.:" only if something is attached (number the items); "Copy to:" only if copies are sent.

MUST REMEMBER: The sender's NAME is never written at the top of a formal letter - only the address. The name goes below the complimentary close. (Only the "From-To" variant in 1.7 writes the name at the top.)

MUST REMEMBER: EXAM IDENTITY RULE - if the question does not give a name or an address, write "Examination Hall" as your address and sign as "ABC" or "XYZ". Never reveal your real name, roll number or address in an exam answer. If the question gives a name and an address, use exactly those.

1.7 Correct order of components HIGH-FREQUENCY

Read each column from top to bottom, one step per row. The Formal column uses the same numbers as the anatomy in 1.6.

FormalSemi-formalInformalBusiness
1. Sender's address (no name)1. Sender's address1. Sender's address (short)1. Letterhead - FIRM NAME in capitals, address, phone, email, GSTIN
2. Date2. Date2. Date2. Ref. No.
3. "To" + receiver's designation and address3. Receiver's name and address3. Salutation - "Dear Priya,"3. Date
4. Subject4. Subject (optional)4. Body (contractions allowed)4. Inside address (receiver)
5. Reference (only when referring to earlier correspondence)5. Salutation - "Dear Mr. Raman,"5. Close - "Your loving friend,"5. "Kind Attention: ..." line (optional)
6. Salutation - "Dear Sir/Madam,"6. Body6. First name6. Subject
7. Opening paragraph7. Close - "Yours sincerely,"–7. Salutation
8. Main paragraph(s)8. Full name–8. Body
9. Closing paragraph––9. Complimentary close
10. Complimentary close - "Yours faithfully,"––10. "For FIRM NAME" (optional)
11. Name (signature above it)––11. Name (signature above it)
12. Encl. and Copy to (only if needed)––12. Designation
–––13. Encl.
–––14. CC

MEMORY TRICK: S-D-R-S-S-B-C-S = "Sunday Dinner Remains Special - Sambar, Biryani, Curd, Sweet": Sender's address, Date, Receiver's address, Subject, Salutation, Body, Close, Signature. (A Reference line, when needed, sits between Subject and Salutation; Encl. comes after the Signature.)

DO NOT CONFUSE: The position of the subject line. In the exam-style formal letter used in this book, the Subject comes BEFORE the salutation (receiver's address → Subject → "Dear Sir/Madam,"). In the traditional British / commerce-textbook business layout, it comes AFTER the salutation ("Dear Sir/Madam," → Subject → body). That is an accepted variant; choose one layout and use it consistently.

MUST REMEMBER: The subject line begins "Subject:" (or "Sub:"), is a short noun phrase and has NO full stop - e.g. "Subject: Request for a bonafide certificate". Write it in every formal and business letter. It is optional in semi-formal letters and never used in informal letters.

Accepted variant: the "From-To" format

This format is widely taught in Tamil Nadu schools. How it differs from our full-block layout:

  • "From" + the sender's NAME and address at the top (our layout writes only the address).
  • "To" + the receiver's address.
  • The salutation comes first, then the subject ("Sub:").
  • "Thanking you," is written on a separate line before the close (accepted Indian usage).
  • "Place:" and "Date:" are written at the bottom-left instead of the date at the top.
From R. Karthik, 12, Gandhi Road, K.K. Nagar, Madurai - 625 020 To The Headmaster, Government Higher Secondary School, K.K. Nagar, Madurai - 625 020 Respected Sir, Sub: Request for a bonafide certificate I am studying in Class XII-A in your school. I need a bonafide certificate to apply for a scholarship. I request you to issue the certificate at the earliest. Thanking you, Yours faithfully, R. Karthik Place: Madurai Date: 12 October 2026

Recognise and accept this variant. The model letters in this book use the full-block layout of 1.6 (see also Sections 9 and 13).

Address on the envelope

School examinations often ask for the "Address on the envelope" after the letter. Write "To" and then the receiver's name (or designation) and full address exactly as in the letter, ending with the PIN code (Postal Index Number - six digits). The sender's name and address go under "From" at the top-left of the front or on the back. The stamp goes at the top-right corner. See the example in 1.9.

1.8 Terminology of letter parts

This table covers the parts of a letter. The full glossary of letter-writing terms is in Section 7.

TermSimple definitionWhen it is usedImportant exam pointExample
Sender's addressThe writer's own address, written first.All letters (a short form in informal letters)Address only - no name above it. No address given? Write "Examination Hall".12, Gandhi Road, K.K. Nagar, Madurai - 625 020
DateThe day on which the letter is written.All letters, below the sender's addressMonth in words; no all-numeric dates (see 1.10).12 October 2026
Receiver's (inside) addressThe designation or name and the address of the receiver, written inside the letter.Formal, semi-formal and business letters; never in informal lettersFormal: "To" on its own line, then the designation. It must match the envelope address.The Principal, Government Higher Secondary School, Vellore - 632 001 (after a "To" line)
LetterheadThe printed heading of an organisation - name, logo, address, phone, email, GSTIN.Business and office letters on printed stationeryWith a letterhead, the sender's address is not written again. Firm name in capitals.KAVERI PAPER MILLS, Erode - 638 011; GSTIN: 33XXXXXXXXXX1Z5
Reference numberA file or letter number used to trace correspondence."Ref. No." at the top = the sender's own file number (business, official). "Reference:" below the subject = the receiver's earlier letter or notice.When replying, always quote the number and date of the earlier letter.Ref. No. KPM/SAL/2026/118; Reference: Your letter No. 45/2026 dated 2 October 2026
Attention lineA line that directs a letter addressed to a firm to a particular person or department.Business lettersAfter the inside address, before the subject. The salutation still suits the firm: "Dear Sir/Madam,".Kind Attention: The Purchase Manager
Subject lineA one-line summary of the purpose of the letter.Formal, business and official letters; optional in semi-formal; never in informal"Subject:" (or "Sub:") + short noun phrase; no full stop; before the salutation.Subject: Request for a bonafide certificate
SalutationThe greeting at the start of the letter.All letters; not used in office memoranda, office orders, notifications, government circulars and noticesComma after it; it must match the close. "Respected Sir," is Indian usage for superiors. Do not combine "Dear" and "Respected".Dear Sir/Madam,
Opening paragraphThe first paragraph; it says why you are writing.All lettersCome to the point in one or two sentences. No "I am fine here" in a formal letter.I am writing to bring to your notice the poor condition of the road in our area.
BodyThe main part; it gives details, facts and reasons.All lettersOne idea per paragraph; exact facts (place, date, amount); polite even in a complaint.The road has had deep potholes for three months, and two accidents have taken place.
Closing paragraphThe last paragraph; it states the action wanted and ends politely.All lettersSay clearly what you want done. "Thanking you," on a separate line is accepted Indian usage; better: "Thank you for your time." inside this paragraph.I request you to repair the road at the earliest. Thank you for your time.
Complimentary closeThe polite phrase that ends the letter, just before the name.All letters; not used in office memoranda, office orders, notifications, government circulars and noticesMust match the salutation. Capital Y, small second word, no apostrophe, comma after. Variant: "Yours truly," (business).Yours faithfully, / Yours sincerely,
SignatureThe writer's handwritten sign.Formal, business and official letters - just above the written nameIn an exam, never sign your real name; use the given name or "ABC" / "XYZ".(signature) above "R. Karthik"
Name and designationThe writer's full name, and the post when writing for an office or firm.Formal and business letters; designation in office lettersNo title (Mr./Dr.) before your own name. The designation goes on the line below the name.S. Senthil, Purchase Manager
Enclosures (Encl.)Documents sent along with the letter.When certificates, bills, photographs or forms are attachedBelow the name, numbered. Mention them in the body too.Encl.: 1. Copy of the bill 2. Photographs (4 nos.)
Copies (CC / Copy to)A note that copies of the letter have been sent to others.When other persons or offices must also be informedCC = carbon copy (today also read as "courtesy copy"). Official letters write "Copy to:". Placed after Encl.Copy to: The Assistant Engineer (Electrical)
Postscript (P.S.)A short afterthought added below the signature (Latin post scriptum = written after).Mainly personal lettersAvoid in formal exam letters - plan the letter so that nothing is left out.P.S. Do bring the photos of the trip.
Through proper channelAn employee's letter to a higher authority, sent through the immediate superior.Employees' applications and representations - transfer, promotion, permissionWrite a "Through: ..." line above or below the addressee.Through: The Headmaster, Government High School, Vellore
Addressing conventionsThe accepted titles and forms used for the receiver.Receiver's address, salutation and envelopeDesignation when the name is unknown; never two titles together (see 1.9).Tmt. K. Lakshmi; M/s. Kaveri Traders; The Branch Manager
Envelope addressThe receiver's address (and the sender's) written on the envelope.Posted letters; school exams ask for the "Address on the envelope"Same as the inside address; PIN code on the last line; stamp at the top-right.See the example in 1.9

1.9 Addressing conventions

Titles and forms of address used in the receiver's address, in the salutation and on the envelope.

FormUseExample
Mr.Any man, married or unmarriedMr. R. Karthik
Mrs.A married womanMrs. S. Meena
Ms.A woman, when her marital status is unknown or not relevant; the safe choice in businessMs. K. Divya
MissAn unmarried woman or a girl. No full stop - it is not an abbreviation.Miss S. Anitha
Dr.A medical doctor or the holder of a doctorate (Ph.D.)Dr. P. Suresh
Prof.A professorProf. V. Arun
ShriMr. - Central Government and Hindi usageShri Ramesh Kumar
Smt.Shrimati = Mrs. - Central Government usageSmt. Lakshmi Devi
KumariKumari (Km.) = Miss - Central Government usageKumari Priya Sharma
ThiruMr. - Tamil Nadu Government usageThiru K. Senthil
Thirumathi / Tmt.Mrs. - Tamil Nadu Government usageTmt. R. Kavya
SelviMiss - Tamil Nadu Government usageSelvi M. Divya
Hon'bleHonourable - used for ministers and judgesThe Hon'ble Chief Minister of Tamil Nadu; the Hon'ble Chief Justice, Madras High Court
M/s. (Messrs)Plural of Mr.; written before the name of a firm, never for one personM/s. Kaveri Traders, Erode
"The" + designationWriting to a post or an office, especially when the name is unknownThe Principal; The Branch Manager; The Editor; The District Collector
c/o (care of)The receiver is staying at another person's addressMs. K. Priya, c/o Mr. S. Arun, 14, Anna Street, Salem - 636 007

Rules

  • Name not known? Use the designation: "The Branch Manager, Southern Commercial Bank, ..." with "Dear Sir/Madam,". Official letters are addressed to the POST, so the designation is used even when you know the officer's name. (Exception: a D.O. letter between officers uses the name - "Dear Thiru Kumar," - see Section 6.)
  • Through proper channel: an employee writing to an authority above the immediate superior sends the letter through that superior. Write a "Through: ..." line (e.g. "Through: The Headmaster, Government High School, Vellore") above or below the addressee.
  • Never two titles together: "Mr. Dr. Suresh" is wrong; write "Dr. P. Suresh". Do not write "Dr." before a name and "Ph.D." after it.
  • No title for yourself: sign as "R. Karthik", not "Mr. R. Karthik".
  • The salutation follows the address: a named person → "Dear Mr. Raman," + "Yours sincerely,"; a designation → "Dear Sir/Madam," + "Yours faithfully,".
  • Each form in its place: M/s. only for firms; Hon'ble for ministers and judges; Thiru / Tmt. / Selvi in Tamil Nadu Government letters; Shri / Smt. / Kumari in Central Government letters.
  • Full stops: Indian usage writes Mr., Mrs., Ms., Dr. and Prof. with a full stop; British English often drops it (Mr, Dr) - accepted variant. "Miss" never takes a full stop.

Example: address on the envelope

To Ms. K. Priya, c/o Mr. S. Arun, 14, Anna Street, Salem - 636 007 From R. Karthik, 12, Gandhi Road, K.K. Nagar, Madurai - 625 020

Place the "To" block in the centre or on the right half of the front, the "From" block at the top-left of the front (or on the back), and the stamp at the top-right corner. For a formal letter, copy the inside address exactly - e.g. "The Commissioner, Madurai City Municipal Corporation, Madurai - 625 001".

1.10 Date formats

StyleExampleVerdict
Day, month in words, year (British / modern Indian)12 October 2026Recommended - used in every model letter in this book
Traditional Indian (ordinal + comma)12th October, 2026Acceptable
American (month, day, year)October 12, 2026Acceptable - American style; do not mix styles in one letter
All-numeric with slashes12/10/2026Avoid - ambiguous: 12 October (Indian / British reading) or 10 December (American reading)
All-numeric with a short year12.10.26 / 12-10-26Avoid - ambiguous and incomplete
Abbreviated month or no year12 Oct. 2026 / 12 OctoberAvoid - write the month in full and always give the year

MUST REMEMBER: In every letter write the date as "12 October 2026" - day, month in words, year. It goes below the sender's address, at the left margin. "12th October, 2026" is also accepted, but never use an all-numeric date such as 12/10/2026.

2 Types of Letters

Letters fall into three registers - informal, semi-formal and formal. This section lists the common types in each register. Business, banking, transactional and government letters have their own sections (Sections 3 to 6). Full model letters are in Section 10.

2.1 Personal / informal letters Informal

Features

  • Written to family members, relatives and close friends.
  • Warm, friendly, conversational tone; contractions ("I'm", "don't") are allowed.
  • Layout: short sender's address → date → salutation → body → close → first name.
  • NO receiver's address and NO subject line.
  • The salutation uses the first name or the relationship ("Dear Priya,", "Dear Appa,"); the close shows the relationship ("Your loving son,", "Your loving friend,").
  • The body moves from a friendly opening, to the news or purpose, to regards for the family and a request to reply.
TypePurposeSample opening lineSample closing lineSalutation → Close
Letter to parentsShare news of studies or hostel life; ask for money or permission; reassure themI reached the hostel safely on Sunday, and I've settled in well.Give my love to Thambi, and please take care of your health."Dear Appa," → "Your loving son,"
Letter to a friendKeep in touch; share news, plans and feelingsIt was lovely to get your letter after such a long time.Do write back soon and tell me all your news."Dear Priya," → "Your loving friend,"
Letter to a relativeShare family news; enquire about health; thank or inviteI was happy to hear from Amma that you have fully recovered.Please convey my regards to Aunty and my love to the children."Dear Uncle," → "Your loving nephew,"
Invitation letterInvite a friend or relative to a function - wedding, birthday, house-warmingI'm happy to invite you to my sister's wedding at Thanjavur on 15 November 2026.Do come a day early. We're all looking forward to seeing you."Dear Kavin," → "Your loving friend,"
Thank-you letterThank someone for a gift, help or hospitalityThank you so much for the lovely watch you sent for my birthday.Thanks once again for remembering my birthday."Dear Meena," → "With love,"
Congratulations letterCongratulate someone on a success - exam result, new job, prize, marriageI was delighted to hear that you have cleared the Group 4 examination.Once again, my heartiest congratulations and best wishes for your new job."Dear Arun," → "Your loving friend,"
Condolence letterExpress sympathy and offer support after a death or a great lossI was deeply saddened to hear about the sudden passing of your grandfather.Please take care of yourself and your family. I'll visit you this weekend."Dear Vignesh," → "Your loving friend,"
Apology letterSay sorry for a mistake - missing a function, breaking a promise, a harsh wordI'm really sorry that I couldn't attend your birthday party last Sunday.I hope you'll forgive me. Let's meet soon."Dear Divya," → "Your loving friend,"
Advice letterGuide a younger brother, sister or friend - studies, health, habits, careerAmma tells me that you're spending too much time on your mobile phone these days.Follow a timetable, and I'm sure you'll do well. My best wishes are always with you."My dear Kavin," → "Yours affectionately,"
Letter describing an experienceShare a memorable event - a festival, a match, an exhibition, the first day at collegeI must tell you about the wonderful day I spent at the science exhibition in our college.I wish you and Appa had been there with me."Dear Amma," → "Your loving daughter,"
Letter about a travel/visitDescribe a trip or a visit to a place - what you saw, did and feltLast week our family visited Kanyakumari, and I'm still thinking about the sunrise we saw there.Let's plan a trip there together next summer."Dear Priya," → "With love,"
Letter requesting personal helpAsk a friend or relative for a favour - books, notes, a place to stay, guidanceI need a small favour from you, and I'm sure you won't mind.I'll be very grateful for your help. Please reply soon."Dear Suresh," → "Your loving friend,"

EXAM TIP: Plan an informal body in three parts: (1) a greeting or a reference to the last letter, (2) the main news or purpose, (3) regards to the family and a request to reply. Even an informal letter needs the sender's address and the date.

2.2 Semi-formal letters Semi-formal

What it isA letter to a person you know, but not closely, or to whom you must show respect. It is polite and respectful, yet more personal than a formal letter.
When it is usedWhen you know the receiver by name and the matter is partly personal and partly official - a request, thanks, a polite complaint or some information.
LayoutSender's address → date → receiver's name and address → Subject (optional) → "Dear Mr. Raman," → body → "Yours sincerely," → full name.

Features

  • The receiver's name and address are written; the subject line is optional.
  • Salutation with title and name - "Dear Mr. Raman," / "Dear Mrs. Lakshmi,"; close - "Yours sincerely,".
  • Polite and respectful, but warm; one friendly line of greeting or thanks is allowed.
  • No slang; contractions are best avoided.
  • Short, clear paragraphs that come to the point.
  • Signed with the full name (e.g. "K. Divya"), not the first name alone.
LetterTypical situationSalutation → CloseSample opening line
Letter to a teacherThank a former teacher; ask for guidance or a recommendation"Dear Mrs. Lakshmi," → "Yours sincerely,"I am writing to thank you for guiding me through my Class 12 examinations.
Letter to a landlordAsk for repairs; explain a delay in paying rent; give notice of vacating"Dear Mr. Senthil," → "Yours sincerely,"I am writing about the leaking water tank in the house I rent from you.
Letter to a neighbourRaise a problem politely (noise, parking, an overhanging tree); thank for help"Dear Mr. Suresh," → "Yours sincerely,"I hope you and your family are well. I am writing about the parking space in front of our houses.
Letter to a known authorityA request or suggestion to an official who knows you - ward councillor, Panchayat President, head of the residents' association"Dear Mr. Kumar," → "Yours sincerely,"Thank you for meeting our residents last Sunday. As discussed, I am sending our suggestions for the new park.
Letter to an acquaintanceSomeone you met briefly - thank for help, or ask about a course or a job"Dear Mr. Vignesh," → "Yours sincerely,"We met at the career fair in Coimbatore last month, where you kindly told me about your company's internship scheme.
Letter to a former employerAsk for an experience certificate or a reference; inform about a new job"Dear Mr. Arun," → "Yours sincerely,"I worked in your accounts department from 2022 to 2025, and I am writing to request an experience certificate.
Letter to a known professional (doctor / lawyer / auditor)Ask for an appointment, a report or advice; thank for services"Dear Dr. Meena," → "Yours sincerely,"Thank you for treating my father last month. I am writing to request a copy of his medical report.

EXAM TIP: The same person can receive a formal or a semi-formal letter. A request to the Principal for a Transfer Certificate is FORMAL ("The Principal, ...", "Dear Sir/Madam," → "Yours faithfully,"). A thank-you letter to a teacher who knows you well is SEMI-FORMAL ("Dear Mrs. Lakshmi," → "Yours sincerely,").

Formal vs semi-formal vs informal - compact comparison

PointFormalSemi-formalInformal
RelationshipNo personal relationship - an official, an office or an unknown personKnown, but not close; respect neededClose - family and friends
TonePolite, impersonal, precisePolite, respectful, warmFriendly, casual
Salutation"Dear Sir/Madam," / "Sir,""Dear Mr. Raman,""Dear Priya," / "Dear Appa,"
Close"Yours faithfully,""Yours sincerely,""Your loving friend," / "With love,"
LanguageNo contractions, no slang, no exclamation marksNo slang; contractions best avoidedContractions and everyday words allowed
ExampleComplaint to the Municipal CommissionerLetter to a landlord about repairsLetter to a friend about a trip

For the full high-yield comparison, see Section 12.

2.3 Formal / official letters Formal

Formal (official) letters are written to officials, offices, organisations, editors and unknown persons for an official or business purpose. Default pair: "Dear Sir/Madam," → "Yours faithfully,". To a named person: "Dear Mr. Raman," → "Yours sincerely,". "Respected Sir," / "Respected Madam," (Indian usage for superiors) also take "Yours faithfully,"; a school student writing to the Principal may use the traditional "Yours obediently,". Layout: see 1.6 and 1.7. Templates: Section 9. Worked examples: Section 10.

TypePurposeAddressed toKey opening phrase
A. Requests and complaints
Complaint letter HIGH-FREQUENCYReport a problem or grievance and ask for actionThe officer or office responsible - Commissioner, Manager, Inspector of Police"I am writing to complain about ..." / "I wish to lodge a complaint regarding ..."
Application letter HIGH-FREQUENCYApply for a post, admission, scholarship, licence or certificateEmployer, Principal, Registrar, licensing officer"I wish to apply for the post of ... advertised in The Hindu dated ..."
Request letterAsk for something to be done or given - information, a facility, a documentThe office or officer concerned"I am writing to request ..." / "I would be grateful if you could ..."
Permission letterA student or citizen asks an AUTHORITY for permission - to use a hall, hold a function, visit a factoryPrincipal, Commissioner, Inspector of Police, factory manager"I request your permission to use the school auditorium on ... for ..."
Enquiry / Inquiry letterAsk for information - courses, fees, prices, services. Both spellings are accepted (British: enquiry = asking for information, inquiry = formal investigation; American: inquiry for both).An institution, firm or office"I would be grateful if you could send me details of ..."
Reply to inquiryGive the information asked for, often with a brochure or a price listThe person or firm that made the inquiry"Thank you for your letter dated ... enquiring about ..."
B. Commercial
Order letterPlace an order for goods - items, quantity, price, delivery and payment termsSupplier / seller (Sales Manager)"Please supply the following items at the prices given in your quotation No. ... dated ..."
Cancellation letterCancel an order, a booking, a subscription or a membership, with the reasonSupplier or service provider"We regret that we have to cancel our order No. ... dated ..., as ..."
Adjustment letterThe SELLER's reply that settles a complaint or claim - full grant, partial grant or polite refusal with reasonsThe buyer / customer who complained"Thank you for your letter dated ... We regret the inconvenience caused and are pleased to ..."
Claim letterThe BUYER demands a specific remedy or compensation - refund, replacement, price reductionSeller / supplier"We request a full refund (or a replacement) for the defective goods supplied under your invoice No. ... dated ..."
Apology letter (formal)Apologise for an error, a delay or poor service, and state the corrective stepThe customer, client or authority affected"Please accept our sincere apologies for ..."
C. Employment
Recommendation letterRecommend a person for a SPECIFIC job, course or award, stating his or her strengthsA named selection committee, employer or university"It gives me great pleasure to recommend Ms. K. Divya for ..."
Reference letterGive GENERAL information about a person's character, conduct and work"To Whom It May Concern" or a prospective employer"I have known Mr. S. Vignesh for the past four years as ..."
Appointment letterThe employer appoints a selected candidate - post, pay, date of joining, conditionsThe selected candidate"With reference to your application and the interview held on ..., we are pleased to appoint you as ..."
Resignation letterThe employee formally informs the employer that he or she is leaving the jobImmediate superior / appointing authority"I wish to resign from the post of ... with effect from ..."
Joining letterThe employee reports that he or she has joined duty (joining report)Appointing authority / Head of Office"With reference to your appointment order No. ... dated ..., I report for duty as ... on the forenoon of ..."
Leave letter HIGH-FREQUENCYAsk for leave - casual, medical or earned - giving the dates and the reasonPrincipal, Head of Office or Manager"I request you to grant me leave for two days, from ... to ..., as ..."
Transfer requestThe employee asks to be moved to another place, branch or section, with reasonsAppointing authority, through proper channel"I request you to transfer me from ... to ... on the following grounds: ..."
Promotion requestThe employee asks to be considered for promotion, giving service details and qualificationsAppointing / competent authority, through proper channel"I request you to consider my case for promotion to the post of ..."
Permission request (employee to employer)The EMPLOYEE seeks the employer's permission - to join a course, appear for an examination, apply for another postHead of Office / employer, through proper channel"I request permission to appear for the ... examination to be held on ..."
D. Public authorities and media
Letter to a government authorityRepresentation, request or grievance on a matter handled by a department - education, health, transportSecretary, Director or Head of Department"I wish to draw your attention to ..."
Letter to the District CollectorDistrict-level problems - drinking water, roads, flood relief, land records, public grievancesThe District Collector, ... District"I am writing on behalf of the residents of ... village to request ..."
Letter to a municipal authorityCivic problems - garbage, drainage, street lights, roads, stray dogs, mosquitoesThe Commissioner, ... Corporation / Municipality; the Executive Officer, ... Town Panchayat"I wish to bring to your notice the poor condition of ..."
Letter to a police authorityReport a theft or a loss; complain about traffic, noise or nuisance; request permission or protectionThe Inspector of Police, ... Police Station; the Superintendent / Commissioner of Police"I wish to report the theft of my motorcycle (Reg. No. ...) from ..."
Letter to the editor HIGH-FREQUENCYExpress an opinion on a public issue FOR PUBLICATION, to draw the attention of the public and the authoritiesThe Editor, (newspaper), city - e.g. The Editor, The Hindu, Chennai"Through the columns of your esteemed newspaper, I wish to draw the attention of the authorities concerned to ..."
Letter to a newspaperA business matter with the newspaper office - subscription, delivery, advertisement, correction of an advertisement - NOT for publicationThe Circulation Manager / Advertisement Manager, (newspaper), city"I wish to subscribe to ..." / "I wish to complain about the irregular delivery of ..."
Letter to an institutionAdmission, fees, certificates (Transfer Certificate, bonafide), course details, library membershipThe Principal, Registrar, Director or Secretary"I wish to seek admission to ..." / "I request you to issue my Transfer Certificate ..."
Letter to a public officerRequest a service or a certificate, or raise a grievance within the officer's powersThe Tahsildar, Block Development Officer, Village Administrative Officer, Postmaster"I request you to issue me an income certificate, which I need for ..."

DO NOT CONFUSE: Letter to the editor vs letter to a newspaper. A letter to the editor is written FOR PUBLICATION: it raises a public issue (bad roads, pollution, water shortage) so that readers and the authorities notice it. It is addressed to "The Editor, The Hindu, Chennai" and often begins "Through the columns of your esteemed newspaper ...". A letter to a newspaper is a BUSINESS matter with the newspaper office - subscription, delivery, advertisement, correction of an advertisement. It is NOT for publication and goes to the Circulation Manager or the Advertisement Manager.

DO NOT CONFUSE: Permission letter vs permission request. A permission letter (group A): a student or citizen asks an AUTHORITY - e.g. a student asks the Principal to use the school hall for a quiz. A permission request (group C): an EMPLOYEE asks his or her EMPLOYER - e.g. to appear for an examination or join an evening course - usually through proper channel.

EXAM TIP: Decide the receiver first. Officials and offices are addressed by DESIGNATION ("The Inspector of Police, ..."), with "Dear Sir/Madam," and "Yours faithfully,". Use "Dear Mr. Raman," and "Yours sincerely," only when the question names the receiver.

EXAM TIP: Let the first sentence state the purpose - use the "Key opening phrase" column. Never begin a formal letter with "I am fine here" or "Hope you are doing well".

EXAM TIP: An employee writing to an authority above the immediate superior (transfer, promotion, permission) adds a "Through proper channel" line - see 1.8 and 1.9. Government formats are in Section 6.

3 Business Letters

3.1 What is a Business Letter?

Definition: A business letter is a formal written communication between firms, or between a firm and its customers, suppliers, banks or government offices, on matters of trade. It is written in the firm's name and signed by an authorised person.

Purposes of a business letter

PurposeWhat it means
To communicateAsk for or give information on goods, prices, terms and payments
To get actionPlace orders, settle complaints, collect dues
To keep a recordThe office copy is a permanent record and evidence in a dispute
To build goodwillPrompt, courteous letters create lasting relations
To expand businessSales letters and circulars reach distant customers cheaply
To save time and moneyReplaces personal visits

Features of a good business letter

Inner qualities (content and language)Outer qualities (appearance)
Clear, simple and concise languageGood paper with a printed letterhead
Complete, correct facts, figures and datesNeat full-block layout with proper margins
Courteous, even when complaining or refusingTyped or printed; no overwriting
"You-attitude" - the reader's point of viewReference number and date on every letter
One subject per letter; prompt replyCorrect folding and envelope (posted letters)

The 7 Cs of business communication

CMeaning (one line)WeakBetter
1. CompletenessGive every fact the reader needs; answer all questions."Please send the paper.""Please send 100 reams of A4 paper (70 GSM) by 25 October 2026."
2. ConcisenessFewest words that keep the full meaning; no repetition."Please find enclosed herewith our price list for your kind perusal.""We enclose our price list."
3. ConsiderationReader's side ("you-attitude"); show what the reader gains."We allow 5% discount for early payment.""You save 5% when you pay within 10 days."
4. ConcretenessSpecific facts, figures and dates, not vague words."The goods will reach you soon.""The goods will reach you on 22 October 2026."
5. ClarityFamiliar words and short sentences; only one meaning."Please do the needful.""Please replace the damaged carton."
6. CourtesyPolite, tactful and respectful, even when refusing."You have failed to pay us.""Our invoice No. 512 is still unpaid; perhaps it has escaped your notice."
7. CorrectnessCorrect facts, figures, grammar, spelling and level of language."Your's faithfully""Yours faithfully,"

MEMORY TRICK: 3 CON + 3 CO + 1 CL = 7 Cs. CONciseness, CONsideration, CONcreteness; COmpleteness, COurtesy, COrrectness; CLarity.

EXAM TIP: "You-attitude" = Consideration - a favourite MCQ. Some books list Coherence instead of Consideration (accepted variant).

Layout of a business letter (full block)

Every line starts at the left margin. The parts, in order:

  1. Letterhead - firm name in capitals, address, phone, email, GSTIN (e.g. 33XXXXXXXXXX1Z5; Tamil Nadu numbers begin with 33)
  2. Ref. No. - the firm's reference, e.g. Ref. No. KPM/SAL/2026/145 (in a reply, also quote "Your Ref.")
  3. Date - e.g. 12 October 2026
  4. Inside address - receiver's designation or name, firm and address; "M/s." before a firm's name
  5. "Kind Attention: ..." line (optional) - e.g. Kind Attention: The Purchase Manager
  6. Subject - short noun phrase, no full stop
  7. Salutation - "Dear Sir/Madam," or "Dear Mr. Raman,"
  8. Body - opening (reference, purpose), main (details), closing (action wanted)
  9. Complimentary close - "Yours faithfully," or "Yours sincerely," to match the salutation
  10. "For FIRM NAME" (optional) - the person signs on behalf of the firm
  11. Name - of the signatory (signature above it)
  12. Designation - e.g. Proprietor, Sales Manager
  13. Encl. - enclosures, e.g. Encl.: Price list
  14. CC (carbon copy) - others who receive a copy

DO NOT CONFUSE: Position of the subject line. Our model letters put the Subject BEFORE the salutation (modern full block). In the traditional British / commerce-textbook layout it comes AFTER the salutation - an accepted variant.

How the cards are arranged: Before the sale (3.2–3.3) → Order and supply (3.4–3.7) → Problems (3.8–3.10) → Money (3.11–3.16) → Cancellation (3.17) → Promotion and relations (3.18–3.21) → Courtesy (3.22). Most examples (addresses left out) follow one deal: Sri Murugan Stationers, Madurai (buyer) and Kaveri Paper Mills (seller). Full transaction chain and delivery terms: Section 5.

3.2 Business Inquiry Letter HIGH-FREQUENCY

DefinitionA letter in which a prospective buyer asks a seller for information about goods or services - prices, quality, discounts, payment and delivery terms. "Inquiry letter" and "enquiry letter" are both accepted (British tradition: enquiry = asking for information, inquiry = formal investigation; American English uses "inquiry" for both).
PurposeTo collect prices, samples and terms from suppliers and compare them before ordering.
SenderProspective buyer (a firm or an individual)
ReceiverSeller - manufacturer, wholesaler or supplier
Key phrases"We saw your advertisement in ..."; "Please send us your latest price list and catalogue."; "Kindly quote your lowest prices for ..."; "Please let us know your terms of payment and delivery."
StructureIntroduce your firm and say where you found the supplier → State exactly what you need (item, quality, quantity) → Ask about prices, discounts, payment and delivery → Ask for samples or a catalogue → Request an early reply
Exam vocabularyprice list - list of current prices; catalogue - illustrated list of goods; trade discount - reduction allowed to traders; cash discount - reduction for prompt payment; solicited inquiry - prompted by seller's advertisement; unsolicited inquiry - made on buyer's initiative
Subject: Inquiry for A4 copier paper Dear Sir/Madam, We are a stationery shop in Madurai and wish to stock your A4 copier paper (70 GSM and 75 GSM), which we saw advertised in a trade journal. Please send us your latest price list, with your trade discount, terms of payment and delivery time for about 100 reams a month, and samples of both varieties. We look forward to your early reply. Yours faithfully, For SRI MURUGAN STATIONERS S. Senthil Proprietor

3.3 Quotation Letter HIGH-FREQUENCY

DefinitionThe seller's statement of prices, terms and conditions, sent in reply to an inquiry (before the order). It has a validity period (firm offer).
PurposeTo give the prices and terms asked for and win the order.
SenderSeller - manufacturer or supplier
ReceiverThe prospective buyer who sent the inquiry
Key phrases"Thank you for your inquiry dated ..."; "We are pleased to quote as follows:"; "This quotation is valid until ..."; "We are sending samples under separate cover."
StructureThank the buyer and refer to the inquiry → Give prices, discounts and taxes → State terms of payment and delivery → State validity; mention samples or enclosures → Invite the order
Exam vocabularyfirm offer - offer open for fixed period; estimate - approximate cost; tender - formal (often sealed) offer; proforma invoice - preliminary bill before supply; E&OE - Errors and Omissions Excepted; under separate cover - in a separate packet
Subject: Quotation for A4 copier paper Dear Mr. Senthil, Thank you for your inquiry dated 5 October 2026. We are pleased to quote as follows: A4 copier paper, 70 GSM - Rs. 240 per ream A4 copier paper, 75 GSM - Rs. 265 per ream Prices include GST and delivery to your shop. Trade discount: 10% on orders of 100 reams or more. Payment: within 30 days of delivery. This quotation is valid until 31 October 2026. Samples follow under separate cover. We look forward to your order. Yours sincerely, For KAVERI PAPER MILLS R. Karthik Sales Manager

3.4 Order Letter HIGH-FREQUENCY

DefinitionA letter placing an order: the buyer asks the seller to supply stated goods at stated prices and terms. A Purchase Order (PO) is the formal numbered document issued by the buyer; it is binding once accepted by the seller.
PurposeTo buy goods, stating quantity, quality, price, delivery and payment terms exactly, so that no dispute arises later.
SenderBuyer
ReceiverSeller / supplier
Key phrases"With reference to your quotation dated ..."; "Please supply the following goods:"; "Payment will be made within 30 days of delivery, as agreed."; "Kindly confirm this order."
StructureRefer to the quotation, catalogue or sample → List the goods (description, quantity, rate) → State delivery date, place and transport → State the payment terms → Ask for confirmation of the order
Exam vocabularypurchase order (PO) - buyer's formal numbered order; trial order - small first order; repeat order - same order placed again; consignee - person receiving the goods; execute an order - supply the goods ordered; back order - order awaiting fresh stock
Subject: Order for 100 reams of A4 copier paper Dear Sir/Madam, Thank you for your quotation dated 8 October 2026. Please supply the following under our Order No. SMS/145: A4 copier paper, 70 GSM - 60 reams at Rs. 240 per ream A4 copier paper, 75 GSM - 40 reams at Rs. 265 per ream The 10% trade discount in your quotation will apply. Please deliver the goods to our shop by 25 October 2026. Payment will be made within 30 days of delivery, as agreed. Kindly confirm this order. Yours faithfully, For SRI MURUGAN STATIONERS S. Senthil Proprietor

3.5 Confirmation Letter (order confirmation / acceptance of order)

DefinitionThe seller's letter that confirms ACCEPTANCE of the buyer's order on the stated terms (goods, price, delivery date); once accepted, the order becomes a binding contract. It goes further than an acknowledgement, which confirms only RECEIPT of the order (see 3.22). A confirmation letter may also put an oral or telephone agreement on record.
PurposeTo assure the buyer that the order will be executed (not merely that it has been received), restate the details to avoid misunderstanding, and give the dispatch date.
SenderSeller
ReceiverBuyer
Key phrases"Thank you for your Order No. ... dated ..."; "We are pleased to confirm your order for ..."; "We confirm the prices and terms stated in your order."; "The goods will be dispatched on ... and should reach you by ..."
StructureThank the buyer for the order (No. and date) → Confirm acceptance and restate the key details → Give the dispatch or delivery date and mode → Close with thanks
Exam vocabularyorder acknowledgement - seller's note that the order is received (receipt only, not acceptance); acceptance - seller's consent to supply; binding contract - legally enforceable agreement; counter-offer - reply that changes the terms; lead time - time from order to delivery
Subject: Confirmation of your Order No. SMS/145 Dear Mr. Senthil, Thank you for your Order No. SMS/145 dated 12 October 2026 for 100 reams of A4 copier paper. We are pleased to confirm the order at the prices and terms of our quotation dated 8 October 2026, including the 10% trade discount. The goods will be dispatched by road on 20 October 2026 and should reach you by 22 October 2026. We look forward to serving you regularly. Yours sincerely, For KAVERI PAPER MILLS R. Karthik Sales Manager

3.6 Dispatch Letter (dispatch advice)

DefinitionA letter (advice) from the seller telling the buyer that the ordered goods have been sent, with details of the consignment, the carrier and the documents. Also spelt "despatch".
PurposeTo let the buyer prepare to receive the goods, send the invoice and the transport receipt needed to take delivery, and record the dispatch date.
SenderSeller (consignor)
ReceiverBuyer (consignee)
Key phrases"We are pleased to inform you that your order was dispatched today."; "The goods have been sent by road under LR No. ..."; "We enclose our invoice No. ... and the lorry receipt."; "The goods are insured against transit risks."
StructureRefer to the order → Give the dispatch date, mode, carrier, receipt No. and number of packages → List the enclosures (invoice, LR / RR / B/L / AWB) → Mention insurance and the payment due → Ask the buyer to confirm receipt
Exam vocabularyconsignment - goods sent in one lot; invoice - seller's bill for goods; Lorry Receipt (LR) - road transporter's receipt; Railway Receipt (RR) - railway's receipt for goods; Bill of Lading (B/L) - receipt for sea shipment; Airway Bill (AWB) - document for air cargo
Subject: Dispatch of your Order No. SMS/145 Dear Mr. Senthil, We are pleased to inform you that the 100 reams of A4 copier paper against your Order No. SMS/145 were dispatched today by road, in 10 cartons, under Lorry Receipt No. 4471. We enclose our Invoice No. KPM/512 for Rs. 22,500 and the lorry receipt. The goods are insured against transit risks. Please confirm receipt when the consignment reaches you. Yours sincerely, For KAVERI PAPER MILLS R. Karthik Sales Manager Encl.: 1. Invoice No. KPM/512 2. Lorry Receipt No. 4471

3.7 Delivery Letter (delivery intimation / confirmation of receipt)

DefinitionA letter about the delivery of goods: the seller's (or transporter's) intimation that the goods have arrived or will be delivered on a stated date, or the buyer's confirmation that they have been received, and in what condition.
PurposeTo fix the date, time and place of delivery so that someone receives and checks the goods; the buyer's confirmation closes the order.
SenderSeller or transporter (intimation); buyer (confirmation of receipt)
ReceiverBuyer (intimation); seller (confirmation of receipt)
Key phrases"Your consignment will be delivered at your premises on ..."; "Please take delivery from the transporter's godown on presenting the LR."; "Kindly sign and return the delivery challan."; "We confirm receipt of the goods in good condition."
StructureRefer to the order and dispatch (LR No., invoice No.) → Give the delivery date, time and place, or confirm receipt and condition → Mention the documents to be signed → Ask for any damage or shortage to be reported at once
Exam vocabularydelivery challan - document travelling with goods; Goods Received Note (GRN) - buyer's record of receipt; proof of delivery (POD) - signed record of delivery; take delivery - collect goods from carrier; door delivery - goods brought to premises
Subject: Delivery of your Order No. SMS/145 Dear Mr. Senthil, The 10 cartons sent under LR No. 4471 have reached our transporter's Madurai godown. They will be delivered at your shop on 22 October 2026 between 10 a.m. and 1 p.m. Please arrange for someone to check the cartons and sign the delivery challan. If any carton is damaged, kindly note it on the challan and inform us at once. Yours sincerely, For KAVERI PAPER MILLS R. Karthik Sales Manager

3.8 Complaint Letter HIGH-FREQUENCY

DefinitionA letter in which the buyer reports a problem (defect, delay, shortage, wrong goods) to the seller and asks for it to be set right. Customers also write complaint letters about poor service.
PurposeTo draw the seller's attention to the problem, get it corrected and keep a written record. Tone: firm, factual and polite - never rude.
SenderBuyer / customer
ReceiverSeller, supplier or service provider
Key phrases"We regret to inform you that ..."; "On opening the cartons, we found that ..."; "The goods are not up to the sample."; "We shall be glad if you will look into this matter at once."
StructureRefer to the order and invoice (No. and date) → State the problem clearly, with facts → Explain the inconvenience or loss caused → Ask for corrective action → Close politely, expecting early action
Exam vocabularygrievance - cause for complaint; defective - having faults; shortage - less than invoiced; damaged in transit - harmed while being carried; not up to sample - poorer than sample shown
Subject: Paper not up to sample - Order No. SMS/145 Dear Sir/Madam, We received the goods against our Order No. SMS/145 on 22 October 2026. On opening the cartons, we found that 10 reams marked "75 GSM" contain thinner paper that is not up to the sample you sent us. The sheets jam in our customers' copiers. We shall be glad if you will look into this matter at once and let us know what you propose to do. Yours faithfully, For SRI MURUGAN STATIONERS S. Senthil Proprietor

3.9 Claim Letter HIGH-FREQUENCY

DefinitionA letter in which the buyer demands a specific remedy or compensation (refund, replacement, price reduction) for defective, short, damaged or late goods. A claim for loss or damage in transit is made on the carrier or the insurance company.
PurposeTo recover the loss by stating the exact remedy or amount claimed, supported by evidence.
SenderBuyer (the claimant)
ReceiverSeller; for transit loss, the carrier or the insurance company
Key phrases"We therefore claim ..."; "Please replace the defective goods free of cost."; "Please send us a credit note for Rs. ..."; "We are holding the goods at your disposal."
StructureRefer to the order, invoice and earlier complaint → State the loss with evidence (test report, photographs) → State the exact remedy or amount claimed → Say what you will do with the defective goods → Ask for early settlement
Exam vocabularyclaimant - person making the claim; compensation - payment for loss; refund - money paid back; allowance - price reduction for defect; at your disposal - held for your instructions
Subject: Claim for 10 reams of sub-standard paper - Invoice No. KPM/512 Dear Sir/Madam, Further to our letter dated 23 October 2026, we have tested the 10 reams of "75 GSM" paper supplied under your Invoice No. KPM/512. The paper is only 68 GSM. We therefore claim a credit of Rs. 2,385 (10 reams at Rs. 265, less 10% trade discount) and request you to take back these reams at your cost. We are holding them at your disposal and look forward to early settlement. Yours faithfully, For SRI MURUGAN STATIONERS S. Senthil Proprietor

3.10 Adjustment Letter HIGH-FREQUENCY

DefinitionThe seller's reply that settles a complaint or claim: full grant, partial grant, or polite refusal with reasons.
PurposeTo settle the matter fairly and keep the customer's goodwill. Tone: apologetic when at fault, tactful when refusing; never rude.
SenderSeller
ReceiverBuyer (the complainant or claimant)
Key phrases"We are sorry for the inconvenience caused to you."; "We accept your claim in full."; "As a special case, we are allowing you a discount of ..."; "We regret that we cannot accept responsibility, because ..."
StructureThank the customer and refer to the letter → Apologise, or explain what your inquiry found → State the decision: full grant, partial grant or refusal with reasons → Say what action is being taken (replacement, credit note, refund) → Rebuild goodwill for future business
Exam vocabularyfull adjustment - claim granted completely; partial adjustment - claim granted in part; credit note - seller's note reducing buyer's dues; ex gratia - as a favour, without obligation; goodwill - customers' favourable attitude
Subject: Your claim dated 26 October 2026 - Invoice No. KPM/512 Dear Mr. Senthil, Thank you for your letter dated 26 October 2026. We are sorry that 10 reams of lighter paper were packed in your order by mistake. We accept your claim in full. Please return the 10 reams through our transporter at our cost; we shall send you a credit note for Rs. 2,385 as soon as they reach us. We have tightened our packing checks. We value your continued business. Yours sincerely, For KAVERI PAPER MILLS R. Karthik Sales Manager

3.11 Credit Letter (credit request, credit status inquiry, grant or refusal of credit)

DefinitionA letter about CREDIT: a request to open a credit account, a credit (status) inquiry to referees, or a letter granting or refusing credit. Business (trade) credit means the supplier allows the buyer to pay later (e.g. 30 / 60 days).
PurposeBuyer: to buy now and pay later. Seller: to check the buyer's creditworthiness, then state the decision and terms (credit limit and period). A refusal must be tactful and invite cash orders.
SenderBuyer (request); seller (status inquiry, grant or refusal)
ReceiverSeller (request); referees - other suppliers or the buyer's bank (status inquiry); buyer (grant or refusal)
Key phrases"We shall be glad if you will open a credit account for us."; "We shall be grateful for your opinion, in confidence, on the standing of ..."; "We are pleased to allow you credit up to Rs. ... on 30 days' terms."; "We regret we cannot offer credit at present but welcome your cash orders."
StructureIntroduce the firm and its trading record → State the credit wanted (limit and period) → Give trade and bank references → Assure prompt payment and request a favourable reply
Exam vocabularycredit limit - maximum amount on credit; credit period - days allowed to pay; creditworthiness - ability to repay; trade reference - opinion from other suppliers; bank reference - bank's statement on customer; status inquiry - check on financial standing
Subject: Request to open a credit account Dear Sir/Madam, We have bought paper from you on cash terms for the past year. As our orders are now regular, we shall be glad if you will open a credit account for us with a limit of Rs. 1,00,000 on 30 days' terms. For our standing, you may refer to Southern Commercial Bank, Egmore, Chennai, and to Anbu Printers, Madurai. We assure you of prompt payment. Yours faithfully, For PAVAI BOOK HOUSE K. Meena Partner

DO NOT CONFUSE: A credit letter is about buying on credit. A Letter of Credit (L/C) is a BANK's written undertaking to pay the seller/exporter against documents; a credit note is the SELLER's document that reduces what the buyer owes; a credit advice is a bank's intimation of a credit - see Section 4.

3.12 Debit Letter (debit advice / debit-note covering letter)

DefinitionA letter about a DEBIT. (a) Debit advice: an intimation (usually from a bank) that an account has been debited. (b) Debit-note covering letter: sent with a debit note, which is issued by the BUYER to the seller when returning goods or claiming a deduction (it tells the seller that his account has been debited); a seller may also send a debit note to charge an extra amount (undercharge). Side note only: under GST law both notes are issued by the supplier.
PurposeTo tell the other party the amount and reason for a debit entry (goods returned, shortage, overcharge, undercharge, bank charges) so that both accounts agree.
SenderBuyer (returns, deductions); seller (undercharge); bank (debit advice)
ReceiverSeller (buyer's debit note); buyer (seller's debit note); account holder (bank's debit advice)
Key phrases"We enclose our Debit Note No. ... for Rs. ..."; "We have debited your account with Rs. ... for the goods returned."; "The freight of Rs. ... was left out of our invoice No. ... by mistake."; "Your account has been debited with Rs. ... towards ..."
StructureRefer to the invoice or transaction → State the reason for the debit → Give the amount and the debit note number, and enclose the note → Ask for a matching credit note or confirmation
Exam vocabularydebit note - buyer's note claiming a deduction; debit advice - bank's intimation of debit; undercharge - billed less than due; overcharge - billed more than due; purchase returns - goods returned to supplier; statement of account - summary of dealings and balance
Subject: Return of 10 reams - our Debit Note No. 12 Dear Sir/Madam, As agreed in your letter dated 28 October 2026, we have today returned, through your transporter, the 10 reams of sub-standard paper supplied under your Invoice No. KPM/512. We enclose our Debit Note No. 12 for Rs. 2,385 and have debited your account with this amount. Please send us your credit note for Rs. 2,385. Yours faithfully, For SRI MURUGAN STATIONERS S. Senthil Proprietor Encl.: Debit Note No. 12

DO NOT CONFUSE: A debit letter only explains or covers a debit. The debit note, the credit note and the Letter of Credit (a bank's undertaking to pay) are explained in Section 4. Quick check: debit note - mainly the BUYER's document; credit note - the SELLER's.

3.13 Collection Letter HIGH-FREQUENCY

DefinitionA letter written by a seller (creditor) to a customer whose payment is overdue, asking for payment. Collection letters are usually sent as a series, each firmer than the last (the collection series).
PurposeTo recover overdue money while keeping goodwill as long as possible, to learn the reason for delay and, at the last stage, to warn of legal action.
SenderSeller / creditor (accounts or credit department)
ReceiverBuyer / debtor who has not paid
Key phrases"May we remind you that our invoice No. ... for Rs. ... was due on ..."; "We are surprised that we have had no reply to our earlier letters."; "Unless we receive payment by ..., we shall be compelled to take legal action."; "If you have already sent the payment, please ignore this letter."
StructureState the amount, invoice No. and due date → Refer to earlier reminders (in later letters) → Ask for payment or the reason for delay → Fix a final date → State the consequence (final notice only)
Exam vocabularyoverdue - not paid by due date; outstanding - still unpaid; debtor - one who owes money; creditor - one owed money; final notice - last demand before action; legal notice - lawyer's formal demand
Subject: Final notice - Invoice No. JE/318 for Rs. 48,600 Dear Sir/Madam, Our Invoice No. JE/318 for Rs. 48,600 fell due on 31 July 2026. We have received neither payment nor a reply to our reminders dated 16 August 2026 and 10 September 2026. Unless the full amount reaches us by 15 October 2026, we shall be compelled to hand over the matter to our lawyers without further notice. We hope you will settle the account and spare us this unpleasant step. Yours faithfully, For JOTHI ELECTRICALS V. Divya Accounts Manager

The collection series - the tone rises step by step:

StageToneTypical line
1. First reminderPolite - assumes an oversight"Perhaps this has escaped your notice."
2. Second reminderFirmer - asks for payment or the reason for delay"May we know why the account is still unpaid?"
3. Final notice / demandFirm - warns of legal action"... we shall hand over the matter to our lawyers."

Some books show more stages (e.g. reminder, inquiry, appeal, ultimatum) - accepted variant; the tone still rises: polite → firm → final.

3.14 Reminder Letter

DefinitionA polite letter that reminds the receiver of something promised or due but not yet done - a payment, a reply, a delivery, a renewal or a document.
PurposeTo get the pending action without giving offence and to keep a written record of the follow-up. Tone: polite; assume it is an oversight.
SenderWhoever is waiting for action (seller, buyer or any contact)
ReceiverThe person or firm that has not yet acted
Key phrases"This is to remind you that ..."; "We would like to draw your attention to our letter dated ..."; "We have not yet received ..."; "We shall be glad to hear from you by ..."
StructureRefer to the earlier letter, order or due date → State what is still pending → Explain why it is urgent → Request action by a definite date
Exam vocabularypending - not yet done; follow-up - checking on progress; due date - last date for action; renewal - extension for another period; at the earliest - as soon as possible
Subject: Reminder - delivery against Order No. PBH/77 Dear Sir/Madam, We would like to draw your attention to our Order No. PBH/77 dated 1 September 2026 for 40 steel bookshelves, which you promised to deliver by 20 September 2026. We have not yet received the goods or any word from you. As we need the shelves for our new branch, please let us know the date of dispatch by 30 September 2026. Yours faithfully, For PAVAI BOOK HOUSE K. Meena Partner

3.15 Payment Request Letter

DefinitionA letter in which a seller or service provider asks the customer to pay an amount that is due (or an agreed advance or part payment), usually enclosing the bill.
PurposeTo get payment on time by stating the amount, due date and mode of payment clearly. Tone: routine and courteous - the money is due, not yet overdue.
SenderSeller / service provider (accounts department)
ReceiverBuyer / client
Key phrases"We enclose our invoice No. ... for Rs. ..."; "The amount is payable by ..."; "Please remit the amount by NEFT to our account ..."; "Kindly quote the invoice number with your payment."
StructureRefer to the goods or services supplied and the invoice → State the amount and the due date → Give payment details (mode, bank account) → Request payment by the due date and thank the customer
Exam vocabularyremit - send money; remittance - money sent; NEFT - National Electronic Funds Transfer; RTGS - Real Time Gross Settlement; part payment - paying only a portion
Subject: Payment for annual maintenance - Invoice No. BCS/2026/88 Dear Mr. Raman, We enclose our Invoice No. BCS/2026/88 dated 1 October 2026 for Rs. 18,880 (including GST) for the annual maintenance of your 20 computers for 2026-27. As agreed, the amount is payable by 15 October 2026. Please remit it by NEFT to our account with Southern Commercial Bank (details on the invoice), quoting the invoice number. Thank you for your continued support. Yours sincerely, For BRIGHT COMPUTER SYSTEMS A. Vignesh Accounts Officer Encl.: Invoice No. BCS/2026/88

3.16 Payment Confirmation Letter

DefinitionA letter confirming that a payment has been received and credited against the correct bill, and stating any balance. A payer may also write to confirm a payment made, giving the transaction details.
PurposeTo give written proof of payment, close the bill, prevent disputes and repeated demands, and thank the customer.
SenderPayee - seller or creditor (or the payer, confirming a payment made)
ReceiverPayer - buyer or client (or the payee)
Key phrases"We confirm receipt of your payment of Rs. ... by NEFT on ..."; "The amount has been adjusted against our invoice No. ..."; "Your account now shows a nil balance."; "Our official receipt No. ... is enclosed."
StructureConfirm the amount, date, mode and reference (UTR or cheque No.) → Say which bill it settles → State the balance → Enclose the receipt and thank the customer
Exam vocabularyreceipt - written proof of payment; UTR - Unique Transaction Reference; nil balance - nothing remains due; subject to realisation - valid once cheque clears; settle - pay in full
Subject: Confirmation of payment - Invoice No. BCS/2026/88 Dear Mr. Raman, We confirm receipt of Rs. 18,880 by NEFT on 12 October 2026 (UTR ending 7093). The amount has been adjusted in full against our Invoice No. BCS/2026/88, and your account now shows a nil balance. Our official Receipt No. 1142 is enclosed. Thank you for your prompt payment. Yours sincerely, For BRIGHT COMPUTER SYSTEMS A. Vignesh Accounts Officer Encl.: Receipt No. 1142

3.17 Cancellation Letter

DefinitionA letter that cancels (withdraws) an order, booking, contract or subscription already placed, usually giving the reason.
PurposeTo stop the supplier before the goods are dispatched or the service is given, to avoid later disputes, and to get back any advance paid. Tone: prompt, clear and polite.
SenderBuyer / customer who placed the order or booking
ReceiverSeller, supplier or service provider
Key phrases"We regret that we have to cancel our Order No. ... dated ..."; "owing to unforeseen circumstances"; "Please do not dispatch the goods."; "Kindly refund our advance of Rs. ..."
StructureIdentify the order (No., date, goods) → Cancel it clearly and give a brief reason → Ask the seller not to dispatch and to confirm the cancellation → Ask for a refund of any advance → Close politely to keep goodwill
Exam vocabularycountermand - cancel an earlier order; unforeseen circumstances - unexpected events; cancellation charges - fee for cancelling; refund - money returned; advance - payment before supply
Subject: Cancellation of Order No. PBH/77 Dear Sir/Madam, We regret that we have to cancel our Order No. PBH/77 dated 1 September 2026 for 40 steel bookshelves. The goods were due by 20 September 2026 and have not been delivered even after our reminder dated 25 September 2026. Please do not dispatch the goods now, and kindly refund our advance of Rs. 20,000 at the earliest. Yours faithfully, For PAVAI BOOK HOUSE K. Meena Partner

3.18 Sales Letter

DefinitionA persuasive letter to prospective or existing customers that introduces a product or service and persuades the reader to buy. It follows the AIDA plan and is often sent to many readers by post or email (direct mail), each copy personalised.
PurposeTo create demand, increase sales, announce offers and reach many customers at low cost.
SenderSeller, manufacturer or service provider (sales or marketing department)
ReceiverProspective and existing customers
Key phrases"Would you like to ...?"; "Here is good news for ..."; "This offer is open only till ..."; "Call us today to book a free demonstration."
StructureAttention - a striking opening → Interest - what the product is and does → Desire - benefits, proof, guarantee, offer → Action - tell the reader exactly what to do now
Exam vocabularyAIDA - Attention, Interest, Desire, Action; USP (Unique Selling Proposition) - feature that beats rivals; prospect - likely customer; direct mail - letters sent straight to customers; call to action - line urging reader to act
Subject: Free installation of solar water heaters till 30 November 2026 Dear Mr. Venkatesan, Would you like free hot water every morning? Our Surya 200-litre solar water heater is fitted on your roof in one day and supplies hot water for a family of five. It cuts your power bill every month and carries a five-year warranty. Till 30 November 2026, installation is free. Call us today to book a free roof inspection. Yours sincerely, For SURYA SOLAR SYSTEMS G. Priya Sales Manager

AIDA - the plan of every sales letter (lines from the example above):

StepWhat it doesExample line
A - AttentionCatches the eye with a question, a fact or an offer"Would you like free hot water every morning?"
I - InterestDescribes the product and how it works"... fitted on your roof in one day ..."
D - DesireMakes the reader want it: benefits, proof, guarantee, offer"... five-year warranty ... installation is free."
A - ActionTells the reader exactly what to do, now"Call us today to book a free roof inspection."

3.19 Circular Letter (business circular: new branch, change of address, admission/retirement of a partner, price revision) HIGH-FREQUENCY

DefinitionA letter with the same content sent to many readers at once - customers, suppliers, dealers, bankers - with news of common interest: a new branch, a change of address, the admission or retirement of a partner, a price revision. (A government circular is different - see Section 6.)
PurposeTo inform many people quickly, cheaply and in the same words, and keep their goodwill. A circular on a partner's retirement also serves as notice to the firm's customers and suppliers.
SenderThe firm (proprietor, partners or management)
ReceiverMany readers at once - customers, suppliers, dealers, bankers
Key phrases"We are pleased to announce that ..."; "We are opening a new branch at ... on ..."; "With effect from ..., our office will function at ..."; "Owing to the rise in raw-material costs, we have to revise our prices from ..."
StructureAnnounce the news and the date it takes effect → Give details (address, branch, partner's background, new prices) → Explain the reason or the benefit → Assure continued quality and service → Request continued support
Exam vocabularywith effect from (w.e.f.) - from the stated date; specimen signature - sample signature for records; partnership deed - partners' written agreement; price revision - change in prices; retirement (of a partner) - leaving the firm
Subject: Admission of a new partner Dear Sir/Madam, We are pleased to inform you that Mr. P. Arun, our Sales Manager for the past ten years, has been admitted as a partner in our firm with effect from 1 November 2026. The firm will continue under the same name, and our policies remain unchanged. Mr. Arun will sign for the firm as shown below. We trust you will continue to support us as before. Yours faithfully, For NOYYAL TEXTILES T. Suresh Managing Partner Specimen signature of Mr. P. Arun: ....................

3.20 Recommendation / Reference Letter (business context: recommending a firm or person; trade reference)

DefinitionA letter giving an opinion on the standing, reliability or ability of a firm or person. It may (a) recommend a firm or person to a third party, or (b) be a trade reference - a reply to a credit (status) inquiry about a firm's creditworthiness. Credit references also come from credit information companies (e.g. CIBIL).
PurposeTo help the receiver decide whether to trade with, give credit to, or appoint the firm or person; the opinion must be honest and confidential.
SenderReferee - a supplier (trade referee), a bank (bank reference) or a firm that knows the person
ReceiverThe firm that asked for the reference, or a prospective business partner
Key phrases"In reply to your inquiry of ..., we are pleased to give the following information."; "We have much pleasure in recommending ... to you."; "We consider them good for credit up to Rs. ..."; "This information is given in strict confidence and without any responsibility on our part."
StructureRefer to the inquiry (usually without naming the firm: "the firm named in your letter") → State how long you have known the firm → Give facts: payment record, reliability, standing → Give your opinion (cautiously if unfavourable) → Add the confidentiality and no-responsibility clause
Exam vocabularyreferee - person giving the reference; credit reference - information on creditworthiness; in strict confidence - not to be disclosed; without responsibility - no liability for opinion; unfavourable reference - cautious negative opinion
Subject: Confidential - your status inquiry dated 2 November 2026 Dear Sir/Madam, In reply to your inquiry, we are pleased to inform you that the firm named in your letter has bought from us on credit for the past six years. They have always settled our bills within the agreed 30 days, and we consider them good for credit up to Rs. 1,00,000. This information is given in strict confidence and without any responsibility on our part. Yours faithfully, For ANBU PRINTERS M. Lakshmi Manager

3.21 Introduction Letter (introducing a new firm or a representative)

DefinitionA letter that introduces a new firm, or a firm's representative (salesperson, agent), to customers and business contacts. It only introduces; a recommendation letter (3.20) also vouches for reliability.
PurposeTo open new business contacts and help a new firm or a representative to be received and trusted.
SenderThe new firm, or the firm that employs the representative
ReceiverExisting or prospective customers, dealers and business contacts
Key phrases"We have pleasure in introducing ..., our new Sales Representative for ..."; "We have started business as ... at ..."; "The bearer of this letter, Mr. ..., is ..."; "We shall appreciate any courtesy you extend to her."
StructureName the person or firm being introduced → Give brief background (experience, role, products) → State the purpose of the visit or the business offered → Request co-operation and close with thanks
Exam vocabularyrepresentative - person acting for a firm; call on - visit formally; bearer - person carrying this letter; credentials - proof of identity and authority; territory - area assigned to salesperson
Subject: Introducing our new Sales Representative Dear Mr. Senthil, We have pleasure in introducing Ms. N. Anitha, who has joined us as Sales Representative for Madurai and Tirunelveli. Ms. Anitha will call on you during the first week of November 2026 to show you our new range of coloured paper and to take your orders. She is authorised to quote prices and delivery dates. We shall appreciate any courtesy you extend to her. Yours sincerely, For KAVERI PAPER MILLS R. Karthik Sales Manager

3.22 Acknowledgement Letter

DefinitionA short letter telling the sender that a letter, order, payment, application, documents or goods have been received and, where needed, when a full reply or action will follow. It confirms RECEIPT only: acknowledging an order does not accept it - acceptance is given in a confirmation letter (3.5).
PurposeTo assure the sender that the item has arrived, to keep a record of receipt, and to gain time politely when a full reply needs checking.
SenderThe receiver of the letter, goods or payment
