ReceiverThe person or firm that sent it
Key phrases"We acknowledge with thanks receipt of your letter dated ..."; "The matter is receiving our attention."; "We shall write to you in detail within ... days."; "Please quote our reference No. ... in future correspondence."
StructureAcknowledge receipt (what, date, reference) → Thank the sender → Say what will happen next and when → Give a reference number and close courteously
Exam vocabularyacknowledge - admit receiving something; interim reply - temporary reply before full answer; inward register - record of letters received; reference number - number for tracing correspondence; under consideration - being examined
Subject: Your letter dated 23 October 2026 - Invoice No. KPM/512 Dear Mr. Senthil, We acknowledge with thanks receipt of your letter dated 23 October 2026 about 10 reams of paper supplied under our Invoice No. KPM/512. The matter is receiving our immediate attention. We have asked our quality-control section to check the batch, and we shall write to you in detail within three days. Yours sincerely, For KAVERI PAPER MILLS R. Karthik Sales Manager

3.23 Quick Revision Table

LetterWritten bySent toOne-line purpose
Inquiry (3.2)Prospective buyerSeller / supplierAsk for prices, samples and terms
Quotation (3.3)SellerBuyer who inquiredGive prices, terms and validity period
Order (3.4)BuyerSellerPlace an order with exact details
Confirmation (3.5)SellerBuyerAccept the order and restate its terms
Dispatch (3.6)Seller (consignor)Buyer (consignee)Goods sent; invoice and LR / RR enclosed
Delivery (3.7)Seller or transporter; buyer (receipt)Buyer; seller (receipt)Fix delivery, or confirm receipt
Complaint (3.8)Buyer / customerSeller / service providerReport defect, delay, shortage or wrong goods
Claim (3.9)Buyer (claimant)Seller; carrier or insurerDemand a specific remedy or compensation
Adjustment (3.10)SellerBuyer (complainant)Settle the complaint: grant, part-grant or refuse
Credit (3.11)Buyer; sellerSeller; referees; buyerRequest, check, grant or refuse credit
Debit (3.12)Buyer (mainly); seller; bankSeller; buyer; account holderExplain a debit or cover a debit note
Collection (3.13)Seller / creditorBuyer who has not paid (debtor)Recover overdue money (series of letters)
Reminder (3.14)Party waiting for actionParty that has not actedRemind about any pending matter
Payment request (3.15)Seller / service providerBuyer / clientAsk for payment that is due
Payment confirmation (3.16)Payee (seller)Payer (buyer)Confirm payment received; state balance
Cancellation (3.17)Buyer / customerSeller / service providerWithdraw an order or booking
Sales (3.18)Seller / marketerProspective customersPersuade the reader to buy (AIDA)
Circular (3.19)FirmMany readers at onceAnnounce branch, address, partner or price news
Recommendation / Reference (3.20)Referee (supplier, bank or firm)Firm that askedGive an opinion on reliability or credit
Introduction (3.21)FirmCustomers / business contactsIntroduce a new firm or a representative
Acknowledgement (3.22)Receiver of a letter, goods or paymentIts senderSay it has been received; action to follow

3.24 Do Not Confuse

Full comparison tables: Section 15.

DO NOT CONFUSE: Complaint (buyer) reports the problem; claim (buyer) demands a specific remedy or compensation; adjustment (SELLER) is the reply that settles it - full grant, partial grant or polite refusal.

DO NOT CONFUSE: A reminder can be about ANY pending matter (payment, reply, delivery) and is usually one polite letter; a collection letter is only about OVERDUE MONEY and comes in a series that gets firmer (first reminder → second reminder → final notice).

DO NOT CONFUSE: A circular INFORMS many readers of the same news (new branch, new address, new partner, new prices); a sales letter PERSUADES readers to buy, using AIDA.

DO NOT CONFUSE: An acknowledgement only says something has been RECEIVED (action to follow); a confirmation says it is ACCEPTED or FINAL (order accepted, payment credited, terms agreed). An "acknowledgement of order" only reports that the order has been received; the letter that accepts it is the confirmation letter (3.5).

MUST REMEMBER: The BUYER writes the inquiry, order, complaint, claim and cancellation; the SELLER writes the quotation, confirmation, dispatch advice, adjustment, payment request and collection letters. Business cycle: Inquiry → Quotation → Order → Confirmation → Dispatch → Delivery → (Complaint → Claim → Adjustment) → Payment request → Reminder / Collection (only if unpaid) → Payment confirmation.

MUST REMEMBER: Model business letters use full block, with the Subject BEFORE the salutation. Pair correctly: "Dear Sir/Madam," → "Yours faithfully,"; "Dear Mr. Raman," → "Yours sincerely,". "For FIRM NAME" goes above the signatory's name and designation. No contractions, no exclamation marks, no "do the needful".

4 Banking and Financial Letters

Banking and financial letters deal with accounts, cheques, cards, loans, credit and payments. Most go from a customer to the Branch Manager; a few come from the bank (bank reference) or from one firm to another (credit reference, reminders, cheque dishonour notice). All are Formal. Full exam-style banking letters: see Section 10.

4.1 General Rules for Bank Letters

RuleDetails
Addressee"The Branch Manager, <bank>, <branch>, <city>" - write to the branch where your account is kept (your home branch). Address the manager by designation, not by name.
Details to includeAccount holder's name (exactly as in the bank records), account number (masked, e.g. XXXXXXXX4521), account type (Savings / Current / Loan / Fixed Deposit), customer ID (CIF number) if any, and the registered mobile number.
SubjectPrecise - the request plus the account, e.g. "Subject: Request for a new cheque book - SB A/c No. XXXXXXXX4521". Never just "Subject: Request".
Salutation and close"Dear Sir/Madam," (or "Sir," / "Madam,") → "Yours faithfully,".
BodyShort and factual, in three parts: (1) who you are and which account; (2) the facts - dates, amounts, cheque or reference numbers; (3) the action you want. One request per letter.
EnclosuresSelf-attested ID proof (and address proof where needed) and supporting papers (statement, SMS alert, return memo), listed below the name: "Encl.: 1. ... 2. ...". Self-attested = you sign the photocopy yourself.
SignatureSign as per the bank records (your specimen signature); a mismatch can lead to rejection. Firm: authorised signatory with the firm's seal. Joint account: as per the mode of operation.
SAFETYNever write your PIN, OTP, CVV or passwords - not in a letter, email, form or phone call. Mask the account number where the full number is not needed; give only the last four digits of a card.
In the examUse the bank, branch and names given in the question. If your name and address are not given, write "Examination Hall" as your address and sign as "ABC" / "XYZ".

Addressee block (full-block style):

To The Branch Manager, Southern Commercial Bank, Anna Nagar Branch, Chennai - 600 040

4.2 Request for Bank Statement

PurposeTo get a statement of account for a stated period - e.g. for income-tax filing, a loan, a visa or an audit.
Written by → toAccount holder → The Branch Manager (home branch)
Must includeMasked A/c No. and account type; the exact period ("from ... to ..."); the purpose; how you will receive it (branch / post / registered email); consent to pay charges, if any.
Key phrases"I request you to issue a statement of my account for the period from ... to ..."; "duly signed and sealed by the bank"; "Please debit the applicable charges, if any, to my account."
Subject: Request for account statement - SB A/c No. XXXXXXXX4521 Dear Sir/Madam, I hold a savings bank account in your branch (A/c No. XXXXXXXX4521, Customer ID 40318827). I request you to issue a statement of this account for the period from 1 April 2025 to 31 March 2026. I need the statement, duly signed and sealed by the bank, to file my income-tax return. I shall collect it from the branch in person. Please debit the applicable charges, if any, to my account. Yours faithfully, R. Karthik

4.3 Request for Cheque Book

PurposeTo ask for a new cheque book when the present one is nearly used up. (A letter is used when the requisition slip in the cheque book is not available.)
Written by → toAccount holder (individual or firm) → The Branch Manager
Must includeMasked A/c No. and account type; number of leaves; delivery (collect at the branch / send to the registered address); for a firm, the authorised signatory's signature with the seal.
Key phrases"Please issue a cheque book of 50 leaves for my current account No. ..."; "Our present cheque book will be used up shortly"; "Please send it to our address registered with the bank."
Subject: Request for a new cheque book - Current A/c No. XXXXXXXX7788 Dear Sir/Madam, We maintain a current account in your branch in the name of Sri Murugan Stationers (A/c No. XXXXXXXX7788). Our present cheque book will be used up shortly. Please issue a new cheque book of 50 leaves for this account. Please send it by courier to our address registered with the bank. Yours faithfully, S. Senthil Proprietor, Sri Murugan Stationers

4.4 Stop-Payment Request HIGH-FREQUENCY

PurposeThe account holder instructs the bank NOT to pay a cheque that is lost, stolen, wrongly written or issued by mistake. It works only if the cheque has not yet been paid - so act at once (if urgent, use net banking or the helpline, then confirm in writing).
Written by → toAccount holder (drawer of the cheque) → The Branch Manager
Must includeMasked A/c No.; cheque number, date, amount and payee's name; the reason; a request for written confirmation; consent to pay the charges.
Key phrases"Please stop payment of Cheque No. ... dated ... for Rs. ... in favour of ..."; "The cheque has been lost / was issued by mistake"; "Please confirm that the payment has been stopped."
Subject: Request to stop payment of Cheque No. 245817 Dear Sir/Madam, I request you to stop payment of Cheque No. 245817 dated 20 September 2026 for Rs. 12,500, drawn on my savings bank account No. XXXXXXXX3196 in favour of M/s. Kaveri Traders. The cheque was lost in the post and has not reached the payee. Please record this instruction immediately and confirm it to me in writing. The stop-payment charges may be debited to my account. Yours faithfully, K. Priya

4.5 ATM / Debit-Card Complaint HIGH-FREQUENCY

PurposeTo report an ATM or debit-card problem - cash not dispensed but account debited, wrong amount, card retained by the machine, card lost / stolen - and ask for reversal, blocking or a new card.
Written by → toCard holder → The Branch Manager (home branch) or the bank's card / grievance cell
Must includeMasked A/c No.; last four digits of the card only; date, time and amount; ATM location and ID; transaction reference number (from the SMS or slip); what went wrong; complaint number, if any; copies of the SMS / statement. Never the PIN, OTP or CVV.
Key phrases"Cash was not dispensed, but my account was debited with Rs. ..."; "Please reverse the amount to my account"; "Please block my card immediately and issue a new one"; "compensation, if applicable, as per current RBI rules [VERIFY CURRENT RULE]".
Subject: Cash not dispensed at ATM but account debited - SB A/c No. XXXXXXXX5083 Dear Sir/Madam, On 25 September 2026 at 7.40 p.m., I tried to withdraw Rs. 10,000 from your ATM at Gandhipuram, Coimbatore (ATM ID: SCB0412), with my debit card ending 3057. No cash was dispensed, but my account was debited with Rs. 10,000 (transaction reference No. 626807412345). I reported this to your customer care the same evening (Complaint No. 772145). Copies of the SMS alert and my account statement are enclosed. Please credit Rs. 10,000 back to my account as soon as possible. Yours faithfully, N. Arun Encl.: 1. Copy of the SMS alert 2. Copy of the account statement

4.6 Address-Change Request

PurposeTo update a new address in the bank's records (a KYC update) after shifting house.
Written by → toAccount holder → The Branch Manager
Must includeMasked A/c No. / customer ID; old and new address with pin code; date of shifting; self-attested proof of the new address (e.g. Aadhaar, passport, voter ID, driving licence); a request to update all accounts.
Key phrases"I have shifted my residence to the following address with effect from ..."; "Please update my address in your records for all my accounts"; "I enclose a self-attested copy of my ... as proof of my new address."
Subject: Change of address - SB A/c No. XXXXXXXX2764 Dear Sir/Madam, I request you to change the address recorded for my savings bank account No. XXXXXXXX2764, as I have shifted my residence with effect from 1 September 2026. My new address is 18, Lake View Road, Velachery, Chennai - 600 042. I enclose a self-attested copy of my Aadhaar card as proof of the new address. Please update this address for all my accounts and send future letters and statements to it. Yours faithfully, T. Meena Encl.: Self-attested copy of Aadhaar card

4.7 Account-Closing Letter

PurposeTo close a bank account (moving to another town, account no longer needed) and have the balance paid out or transferred.
Written by → toAccount holder (for a joint account, usually all holders sign) → The Branch Manager
Must includeMasked A/c No.; a brief reason; how the balance should be paid (transfer - enclose a cancelled cheque of the other account - or a pay order); unused cheque leaves (with numbers) and the debit card returned. Clear dues and stop auto-debits first.
Key phrases"I request you to close my savings bank account No. ..."; "Please transfer the closing balance, after deducting charges if any, to ..."; "I am returning the unused cheque leaves (Nos. ... to ...) and my debit card, cut into two."
Subject: Request to close SB A/c No. XXXXXXXX6419 Dear Sir/Madam, As I have been transferred to Tirunelveli, I request you to close my savings bank account No. XXXXXXXX6419 with your branch. Please transfer the closing balance, after deducting charges if any, to the account shown in the enclosed cancelled cheque. I am returning the unused cheque leaves (Nos. 318406 to 318420) and my debit card, cut into two. Yours faithfully, G. Suresh Encl.: 1. Cancelled cheque 2. Unused cheque leaves (Nos. 318406 to 318420) 3. Debit card (cut into two)

4.8 Loan Application HIGH-FREQUENCY

PurposeTo apply for a loan - education, home, vehicle, business or crop - stating the amount, purpose, repayment ability and security. Usually sent with the bank's loan application form.
Written by → toApplicant → The Branch Manager
Must includePurpose and amount; details of the course / house / vehicle / business; income and co-applicant details; repayment period; security or guarantor; enclosures (admission letter or quotation, income proof, ID and address proof).
Key phrases"I request you to sanction an education loan of Rs. ... for ..."; "My father, Mr. ..., will be the co-applicant"; "I undertake to repay the loan in EMIs (equated monthly instalments) after the moratorium period" (moratorium = a period when no repayment is due, e.g. during the course).
Subject: Application for an education loan of Rs. 4,50,000 Dear Sir/Madam, I have been admitted to the four-year B.E. (Civil Engineering) course at a government-aided college in Coimbatore from 2026-27. The total cost, including tuition, hostel and books, is Rs. 5,60,000, of which my family will meet Rs. 1,10,000. I request you to sanction an education loan of Rs. 4,50,000. My father, Mr. K. Selvam, who has held a savings account in your branch for ten years, will be the co-applicant. I enclose the admission letter, the fee structure, my father's income certificate and our self-attested ID and address proofs. I undertake to repay the loan in EMIs after the moratorium period, as per the bank's rules. Yours faithfully, S. Divya Encl.: 1. Admission letter 2. Fee structure 3. Father's income certificate 4. Self-attested ID and address proofs

4.9 Loan Repayment Request (Rescheduling / EMI Date / Pre-closure and NOC)

PurposeTo change how a loan is repaid: (a) rescheduling EMIs during a temporary difficulty (longer tenure, smaller EMI or a short break); (b) changing the EMI date to suit the salary date; (c) pre-closure - repaying the whole loan early - and a No Objection Certificate (NOC), the lender's letter that no dues remain.
Written by → toBorrower → The Branch Manager (loan section)
Must includeMasked loan A/c No.; type of loan; the exact request; the reason (with proof for rescheduling, e.g. crop loss, salary cut, illness); for pre-closure, the exact amount payable and the NOC; for a vehicle loan, removal of the bank's hypothecation (its charge on the vehicle) from the registration certificate (RC).
Key phrases"Please reschedule my loan by extending the tenure and reducing the EMI"; "Please change my EMI date from the 5th to the 10th of every month"; "I wish to pre-close my loan; please let me know the total amount payable"; "Please issue a No Objection Certificate (NOC) after the loan is closed."
Subject: Pre-closure of two-wheeler loan A/c No. XXXXXXXX6620 and issue of NOC Dear Sir/Madam, I have a two-wheeler loan with your branch (Loan A/c No. XXXXXXXX6620), sanctioned in June 2024, and I wish to pre-close it in full this month. Please let me know the exact amount payable, including charges if any, so that I can pay it before 31 October 2026. After the loan is closed, please issue a No Objection Certificate (NOC) and a letter to remove the bank's hypothecation from the registration certificate (RC) of my vehicle. Yours faithfully, A. Vignesh

4.10 Interest Certificate Request

PurposeTo get a certificate of the interest paid on a loan (home / education) or earned on deposits, with TDS (tax deducted at source) details, for a financial year - usually for the income-tax return.
Written by → toBorrower / depositor → The Branch Manager
Must includeMasked loan or deposit A/c No.; the financial year (e.g. 2025-26); what it must show (principal repaid and interest paid, or interest earned and TDS); the purpose; how it should be sent.
Key phrases"Please issue an interest certificate for the financial year 2025-26"; "showing the principal repaid and the interest paid during the year"; "I need it to file my income-tax return."
Subject: Request for interest certificate - Home Loan A/c No. XXXXXXXX9034 Dear Sir/Madam, I have a home loan with your branch (Loan A/c No. XXXXXXXX9034). Please issue an interest certificate for the financial year 2025-26 showing the principal repaid and the interest paid during the year. I need it to file my income-tax return. Please send the certificate to my registered email address, lakshmi@example.com. Yours faithfully, M. Lakshmi

4.11 Bank Reference Letter

PurposeA bank's statement about its customer's account and general standing, given on request - e.g. to a new supplier who wants to know whether the customer is reliable.
Written by → toThe customer's bank (Branch Manager) → the firm or person who asked, with the customer's consent
Must includeSince when and what type of account; how it has been conducted; a general opinion in careful words (usually no exact balances); a "strict confidence, without responsibility" clause.
Key phrases"has maintained a current account with us since ..."; "The account has been conducted satisfactorily"; "given in strict confidence and without any responsibility on the part of the bank or its officials".
Subject: Bank reference - M/s. Sri Murugan Stationers, Madurai Dear Sir/Madam, At the request of our customer, M/s. Sri Murugan Stationers, Madurai, we confirm that the firm has maintained a current account with our branch since 2015. The account has been conducted satisfactorily. In our opinion, the firm is respectable and good for its normal business engagements. This information is given in strict confidence and without any responsibility on the part of the bank or its officials. Yours faithfully, K. Ramesh Branch Manager

4.12 Credit Reference Letter

PurposeInformation about a person's or firm's creditworthiness from trade referees (other suppliers) or credit information companies (e.g. CIBIL). The usual letter is a trade referee's reply to a credit (status) inquiry.
Written by → toTrade referee (an existing supplier of the firm) → the supplier who made the inquiry
Must includeHow long you have dealt with the firm; the usual credit (amount and period); the payment record; your opinion of a safe credit limit; "in confidence and without responsibility".
Key phrases"They have always settled their accounts on time"; "In our opinion, they are good for credit up to Rs. ..."; unfavourable reply: "We would advise caution in granting credit of this size."
Subject: Credit reference - M/s. Vetri Stationers, Tiruchirappalli Dear Sir/Madam, Thank you for your letter of 2 October 2026 about M/s. Vetri Stationers, Tiruchirappalli, who have given our name as a trade reference. They have bought paper from us for the last six years on 30 days' credit, usually for about Rs. 60,000 a month, and have always paid on time. In our opinion, they are good for credit up to Rs. 1,00,000. This information is given in confidence and without any responsibility on our part. Yours faithfully, P. Anitha Accounts Manager, Kaveri Paper Mills

4.13 Letter of Credit - Importer's Request to Open an L/C HIGH-FREQUENCY

PurposeThe importer (applicant) asks his bank to open a Letter of Credit (L/C) - the bank's written undertaking to pay the exporter (beneficiary) when the specified documents are presented.
Written by → toImporter (applicant) → his bank (the issuing bank), with the bank's L/C application form
Must includeBeneficiary's name and address; amount and currency; goods, quantity and price; proforma invoice No. and date; type of credit (irrevocable; sight or usance; confirmed if required); FOB / CIF and ports; latest shipment date and expiry date; documents required (commercial invoice, bill of lading, packing list, certificate of origin; insurance policy under CIF); advising bank; how margin and charges will be paid.
Key phrases"Please open an irrevocable Letter of Credit for ... in favour of ..."; "available against presentation of the following documents"; "Please debit our current account for the margin money and your charges."
Subject: Request to open an irrevocable Letter of Credit for USD 24,000 Dear Sir/Madam, We have ordered two packaging machines from M/s. Orchid Machinery Co., Singapore (Proforma Invoice No. OM/PI/318 dated 10 September 2026). Please open an irrevocable sight Letter of Credit for USD 24,000 in their favour, on FOB Singapore terms. The credit should be available against the commercial invoice, a full set of clean on-board bills of lading, the packing list and a certificate of origin. The latest date of shipment is 15 November 2026 and the expiry date 30 November 2026. Please advise the credit through your correspondent bank in Singapore and debit our current account No. XXXXXXXX8842 for the margin money and your charges. Our L/C application form is enclosed. Yours faithfully, R. Ganesh Partner, Kaveri Traders Encl.: L/C application form

EXAM TIP: L/C parties: applicant (importer), issuing bank (the importer's bank), beneficiary (exporter), advising / confirming bank (usually in the exporter's country). Types: irrevocable (cannot be cancelled or changed without the agreement of the beneficiary and the banks concerned), confirmed (a second bank adds its own promise to pay), sight (paid when the documents are presented), usance (paid after a fixed period, e.g. 90 days).

4.14 Credit Facility Request (Overdraft / Cash Credit)

PurposeA business asks its bank for working capital: an overdraft (withdrawing more than the balance in a current account, up to a sanctioned limit) or cash credit (a limit against hypothecation of stock and book debts - the goods stay with the borrower but are charged to the bank). Interest is charged only on the amount used.
Written by → toProprietor / partner / director → The Branch Manager
Must includeFirm's name, type and years in business; nature of business and turnover; facility and limit required; purpose; security offered (stock, fixed deposit, property, guarantor); enclosures - financial statements, income-tax and GST returns, stock statement.
Key phrases"We request you to sanction a cash credit limit of Rs. ... against hypothecation of our stock and book debts"; "to meet our working capital requirements"; "an overdraft facility of Rs. ... against our fixed deposit".
Subject: Request for a cash credit limit of Rs. 15,00,000 Dear Sir/Madam, Vaigai Textiles, our proprietary concern, has traded in cotton fabrics in Erode since 2012 and has maintained a current account (A/c No. XXXXXXXX1407) with your branch for ten years. Our turnover for 2025-26 was about Rs. 1.8 crore. To meet our working capital needs for the coming season, we request you to sanction a cash credit limit of Rs. 15,00,000 against hypothecation of our stock and book debts. We offer our fixed deposit of Rs. 3,00,000 as additional security. Our financial statements for the last three years and our income-tax and GST returns are enclosed. Yours faithfully, L. Mohan Proprietor, Vaigai Textiles Encl.: 1. Financial statements for the last three years 2. Income-tax and GST returns

4.15 Payment Reminder

PurposeA polite letter reminding a customer that a payment is overdue - the first step of the collection series: first reminder (polite) → second reminder (firmer) → final notice / demand (warns of legal action).
Written by → toSeller / creditor → Buyer / debtor
Must includeInvoice number, date, amount and due date; a polite request to pay; a copy of the invoice or statement; an "ignore if already paid" line. No threats in a first reminder.
Key phrases"This is a friendly reminder that our Invoice No. ... dated ... for Rs. ... was due on ..."; "Perhaps this has been overlooked"; "If you have already made the payment, please ignore this letter."
Subject: Payment reminder - Invoice No. KPM/2026/418 Dear Sir/Madam, This is a friendly reminder that our Invoice No. KPM/2026/418 dated 10 August 2026 for Rs. 86,400 was due for payment on 9 September 2026. We have not yet received your payment. Perhaps this has been overlooked. A copy of the invoice is enclosed for your reference. Please arrange the payment at your earliest convenience. If you have already paid, please ignore this letter. Yours faithfully, P. Anitha Accounts Manager, Kaveri Paper Mills Encl.: Copy of Invoice No. KPM/2026/418

4.16 Outstanding-Payment Letter

PurposeA firm letter to a customer whose dues remain unpaid after reminders: it states the total outstanding, fixes a final date and says what will follow (the final notice / demand stage).
Written by → toSeller / creditor → Buyer / debtor who has not paid
Must includeStatement of account (invoices, payments, balance outstanding); dates of earlier reminders; a final date; the consequence (stopping supplies / legal action); an offer to discuss any genuine difficulty. Firm but courteous.
Key phrases"Despite our reminders dated ... and ..., the amount of Rs. ... remains outstanding"; "Please settle the account on or before ..."; "Failing this, we shall be compelled to take legal action to recover the amount."
Subject: Outstanding balance of Rs. 1,24,900 - final notice Dear Sir/Madam, Despite our reminders dated 20 September 2026 and 5 October 2026, the balance of Rs. 1,24,900 on your account remains unpaid. A statement of account covering our Invoice Nos. KPM/2026/418 and KPM/2026/472 is enclosed. We value your business and do not wish to take any unpleasant step. If you have any difficulty, please contact us at once. Please settle the full amount on or before 31 October 2026. Otherwise, we shall be compelled to stop further supplies and take legal action to recover the amount. Yours faithfully, P. Anitha Accounts Manager, Kaveri Paper Mills Encl.: Statement of account

4.17 Cheque Dishonour Letter (Notice under Section 138, NI Act) HIGH-FREQUENCY

PurposeThe payee's written demand after the drawer's cheque is dishonoured. The bank returns the cheque with a return memo; the payee may send a legal demand notice under Section 138 of the Negotiable Instruments Act, 1881 within 30 days of receiving the return memo; the drawer then has 15 days to pay.
Written by → toPayee (often through an advocate) → Drawer of the cheque
Must includeCheque number, date, amount, bank and branch; what it was given for; date of presentation; the reason in the return memo (e.g. "Funds insufficient") and the date the memo was received; a demand to pay within 15 days of receiving the notice. Send it by Registered Post with Acknowledgement Due (RPAD) and keep the proof.
Key phrases"returned unpaid with a return memo stating 'Funds insufficient'"; "We hereby demand payment of Rs. ... within 15 days of your receiving this notice"; "failing which we shall be constrained to initiate legal proceedings against you under Section 138 of the Negotiable Instruments Act, 1881".
Subject: Dishonour of Cheque No. 508341 - demand notice under Section 138, NI Act, 1881 Dear Sir/Madam, You gave us Cheque No. 508341 dated 30 October 2026 for Rs. 1,24,900, drawn on Southern Commercial Bank, Fairlands Branch, Salem, in settlement of your outstanding dues. We presented it through our bank on 2 November 2026, but it was returned unpaid with a return memo stating "Funds insufficient", which we received on 5 November 2026. We hereby demand payment of Rs. 1,24,900 within 15 days of your receiving this notice. If you fail to pay within this period, we shall be constrained to initiate legal proceedings against you under Section 138 of the Negotiable Instruments Act, 1881. Yours faithfully, P. Anitha Authorised Signatory, Kaveri Paper Mills

4.18 Transaction-Related Correspondence (Wrong Debit / Failed Transfer / Disputed Transaction)

PurposeTo report a wrong or double debit, a failed NEFT / UPI transfer (money debited but not received by the beneficiary) or a disputed (unauthorised) transaction, and ask the bank to investigate and set it right. Complain to the bank first; if unresolved, the customer may approach the RBI Ombudsman as per current RBI rules [VERIFY CURRENT RULE].
Written by → toAccount holder → The Branch Manager (or the bank's grievance redressal officer)
Must includeMasked A/c No.; date, time and amount; mode (NEFT / UPI / card); UTR (Unique Transaction Reference) number or UPI transaction ID; beneficiary details (for transfers); what went wrong; complaint number, if any; copies of the statement / SMS / screenshot; the action wanted. For fraud: report at once and block the card / UPI.
Key phrases"My account has been debited twice for the same transaction"; "The transfer failed, but my account has been debited"; "I did not authorise this transaction and have not shared any OTP or PIN"; "Please investigate and reverse the amount to my account."
Subject: Failed UPI transfer - amount debited but not credited to the beneficiary Dear Sir/Madam, On 22 September 2026, I transferred Rs. 8,000 by UPI from my savings account No. XXXXXXXX2208 to my brother's account (UPI transaction ID 626512345678). The app showed "Transaction failed", but my account was debited. The amount has neither reached my brother nor been reversed to my account. I reported this to your customer care on 23 September 2026 (Complaint No. 45871). Please investigate and credit Rs. 8,000 back to my account as soon as possible. My account statement and a screenshot of the failed transaction are enclosed. Yours faithfully, S. Kavya Encl.: 1. Account statement 2. Screenshot of the failed transaction

4.19 Clear Distinctions HIGH-FREQUENCY

The seven most-confused credit terms. Learn the "Issued by" column first (more pairs: see Section 15).

TermWhat it isIssued byIssued toPurposeOne-line example
Credit letterA letter about CREDIT: a request to open a credit account, a credit (status) inquiry to referees, or a letter granting or refusing creditBuyer (request); seller (inquiry, grant or refusal)Seller; referees; buyerTo arrange, check, grant or refuse creditSri Murugan Stationers ask Kaveri Paper Mills to allow them 30 days' credit.
Letter of Credit (L/C)A BANK's written undertaking to pay the seller / exporter (beneficiary) when specified documents are presented; used mainly in foreign tradeThe buyer's bank (issuing bank), at the request of the buyer / importer (applicant)Seller / exporter (beneficiary), usually through an advising bankTo assure the seller of payment and the buyer of the documentsSouthern Commercial Bank opens an L/C for Kaveri Traders in favour of a Singapore exporter.
Credit referenceInformation about a person's or firm's creditworthiness from trade referees (other suppliers) or credit information companies (e.g. CIBIL)Trade referees or credit information companiesThe supplier or lender who asksTo decide whether, and how much, credit can be givenKaveri Paper Mills writes that Vetri Stationers have always paid within 30 days.
Bank referenceA bank's statement about its customer's account and general standing, given on requestThe customer's bankThe inquirer (supplier, business partner), with the customer's consentTo confirm the customer's account and general standing"The firm's current account has been conducted satisfactorily since 2015."
Business (trade) creditThe supplier allows the buyer to pay later (e.g. 30 / 60 days); bank credit facilities for business include overdraft and cash creditSupplier (trade credit); bank (overdraft, cash credit)Buyer; business borrowerTo let a business buy now and pay later, or meet its working capital needsKaveri Paper Mills allows 30 days' credit; Vaigai Textiles gets a cash credit limit from its bank.
Debit noteTells the seller that his account has been debited (goods returned or a deduction claimed); a seller may also send one to charge an extra amount (undercharge)Buyer (usually); seller for an underchargeSeller; buyer for an underchargeTo claim a deduction (or charge an extra amount)The buyer returns 10 damaged reams of paper and sends a debit note for Rs. 4,500.
Credit noteREDUCES the amount the buyer owes (goods returned, overcharge, allowance)SellerBuyerTo reduce the buyer's duesThe seller accepts the 10 returned reams and sends a credit note for Rs. 4,500.

MEMORY TRICK: Credit NOTE cuts the bill (seller gives relief); Debit NOTE deducts (buyer claims back).

MEMORY TRICK: L/C = the bank's promise to pay. A credit LETTER is a letter ABOUT credit; a Letter OF Credit is the bank's promise.

MEMORY TRICK: Bank reference: the bank speaks. Credit reference: the market speaks (other suppliers, CIBIL).

DO NOT CONFUSE: Credit advice / debit advice = an intimation (usually from a bank) that an account has been credited / debited. It is neither a credit note (seller to buyer) nor a credit letter.

EXAM TIP: Side note only - under GST law, both debit notes and credit notes are issued by the supplier; for letter-writing and commerce questions, use the rule in the table above.

4.20 Must Remember

MUST REMEMBER: Every bank letter: "The Branch Manager, <bank>, <branch>, <city>" → precise subject with the masked account number → "Dear Sir/Madam," → "Yours faithfully," → signature as per the bank records.

MUST REMEMBER: Never write your PIN, OTP, CVV or passwords in any letter. Mask the account number (e.g. XXXXXXXX4521) and give only the last four digits of a card.

MUST REMEMBER: Stop-payment letter = cheque number + date + amount + payee's name. It works only before the cheque is paid.

MUST REMEMBER: Cheque dishonour: the bank returns the cheque with a return memo → the payee may send a demand notice under Section 138 of the Negotiable Instruments Act, 1881 within 30 days of receiving the return memo → the drawer then has 15 days to pay.

MUST REMEMBER: L/C = a bank's written undertaking to pay the exporter (beneficiary) against documents, opened at the request of the importer (applicant).

MUST REMEMBER: Credit note: SELLER → buyer, reduces the bill. Debit note: BUYER → seller, claims a deduction. Collection series: first reminder (polite) → second reminder (firmer) → final notice / demand (warns of legal action).

5 Transactional Letters

5.1 What is a Transactional Letter?

A transactional letter is a business letter written at one stage of a buying-and-selling deal - from the buyer's first inquiry to the seller's final receipt. Each letter moves the deal one step forward and keeps a written record of what was offered, ordered, sent, received and paid.

  • Register: Formal - "Dear Sir/Madam," → "Yours faithfully,"; to a named person, "Dear Mr. Raman," → "Yours sincerely,".
  • Linked: every letter quotes the earlier references - inquiry date, quotation number, order number, invoice number.
  • Exact: quantities, prices, dates and document numbers must be correct.
  • Binding: an order accepted by the seller becomes a binding contract, so state the terms clearly.

Who Writes What

Buyer's lettersSeller's letters
Inquiry - asks for prices, samples and termsQuotation - gives prices, discounts, terms and validity
Order / Purchase Order - places the orderAcceptance or Rejection of the order (order confirmation)
Acknowledgement of goods received (GRN; shortage / damage report)Dispatch advice; Delivery (goods with the delivery challan)
Query on the invoice, if anyInvoice and invoice-related communication
Payment letter (advice of payment)Reminder (if payment is late); Receipt (acknowledges payment)
Complaint and Claim (if something goes wrong)Adjustment - settles the complaint or claim
Cancellation of the order (before dispatch)Refund, Replacement or Credit note

MEMORY TRICK: The buyer ASKS, ORDERS, PAYS and COMPLAINS; the seller QUOTES, CONFIRMS, SENDS, BILLS and SETTLES.

5.2 The Transaction Flow HIGH-FREQUENCY

A typical trade transaction passes through nine stages. Each stage has its own letter or document.

  1. INQUIRYBuyer → Seller: asks for the catalogue, prices, samples and terms
  2. QUOTATIONSeller → Buyer: states prices, discounts, delivery and payment terms, and validity
  3. ORDERBuyer → Seller: places a numbered purchase order with specifications and a delivery schedule
  4. CONFIRMATIONSeller → Buyer: acknowledges the order and accepts it (or rejects it with reasons)
  5. DISPATCHSeller → Buyer: sends the goods through a carrier, with a dispatch advice quoting the LR / RR / B/L / AWB
  6. DELIVERYCarrier / Seller → Buyer: hands over the goods with the delivery challan; the buyer signs the proof of delivery and records a GRN
  7. INVOICESeller → Buyer: sends the tax invoice with the amount, taxes and due date
  8. PAYMENTBuyer → Seller: pays by NEFT / RTGS / cheque / DD and sends an advice of payment
  9. RECEIPTSeller → Buyer: acknowledges the payment; the account for the order is closed

One line: INQUIRY → QUOTATION → ORDER → CONFIRMATION → DISPATCH → DELIVERY → INVOICE → PAYMENT → RECEIPT

EXAM TIP: In real trade the tax invoice often travels with the goods; the chain lists INVOICE after DELIVERY because it starts the payment stage - learn the order as given.

5.3 Stage-by-Stage Terminology

MCQs and fill-in-the-blank questions often test these meanings.

Stage 1: INQUIRY

TermMeaning
Inquiry / enquiryA letter asking for information - prices, terms, samples. Both spellings are accepted (British: "enquiry" = asking for information, "inquiry" = a formal investigation; American English: "inquiry" for both).
CatalogueA booklet or file listing the seller's goods with pictures, descriptions and code numbers.
Price listA list of the seller's current prices for each item.
SamplesSmall quantities or specimens sent to show the quality of the goods.
Trade termsThe conditions of sale - price basis, discounts, payment period, packing and delivery.

Typical phrase: "Please send us your latest catalogue and price list, together with your best trade terms for regular orders."

Stage 2: QUOTATION

TermMeaning
QuotationThe seller's statement of prices, terms and conditions, sent in reply to an inquiry (before the order).
Firm offer / validityA quotation that holds good for a stated period - its validity (e.g. "valid for 30 days"); orders received within it get the quoted prices.
Trade discountA deduction from the list price allowed to traders who buy for resale, often for bulk orders; shown on the invoice itself.
Cash discountA deduction allowed for prompt payment within a stated period, e.g. "2% if paid within 10 days".
EstimateAn approximate cost of goods or work; the final bill may differ.
TenderA formal (often sealed) offer in response to an invitation, usually from public bodies.
Proforma invoiceA preliminary bill sent before supply - for advance payment or import formalities.

Typical phrase: "We are pleased to quote as follows. This quotation is valid for 30 days from the date of this letter."

Stage 3: ORDER

TermMeaning
IndentAn order for goods: in foreign trade, an order placed by an importer through an agent (indent house); in offices, an internal requisition for stores.
Purchase order (PO)A formal numbered document issued by the buyer; binding once accepted by the seller.
Order numberThe reference number of the order; the seller quotes it on all later letters, delivery documents and invoices.
SpecificationsExact details of the goods - size, grade, model, colour, quantity and packing.
Delivery scheduleWhen, where and in what lots the goods must be delivered.
Trial orderA small first order placed to test the quality of the goods and service.

Typical phrase: "Please supply the following items as per our Purchase Order No. 245 dated 5 October 2026."

Stage 4: CONFIRMATION

TermMeaning
Order acknowledgementThe seller's reply confirming only that the order has been RECEIVED; by itself it does not mean the order is accepted (acceptance comes in the order confirmation).
AcceptanceThe seller agrees to supply as ordered; the accepted order becomes a binding contract.
RejectionThe seller politely declines the order (e.g. goods out of stock, prices changed, credit not possible), usually suggesting an alternative.
Order confirmationA formal confirmation of the accepted order - items, prices, delivery date and payment terms.
Counter-offerThe seller agrees but with changed terms (price, date, quantity); there is no contract until the buyer accepts the new terms.

Typical phrase: "Thank you for your Order No. 245 dated 5 October 2026. We are pleased to confirm that the goods will be dispatched by 10 October 2026."

Stage 5: DISPATCH

TermMeaning
ConsignmentThe goods sent in one lot to a buyer.
ConsignorThe person who sends the goods (usually the seller).
ConsigneeThe person to whom the goods are sent (usually the buyer).
Dispatch adviceThe seller's notice that the goods have been sent - date, carrier, number of packages and the LR / RR / B/L / AWB number.
Packing listA list of the packages and what each contains (quantity, weight, marks).
LR / RRLorry Receipt (goods sent by road) / Railway Receipt (goods sent by rail) - the carrier's receipt for the goods.
Bill of lading (B/L) / Airway bill (AWB)The carrier's receipt for goods sent by sea / by air (see 5.6).

Typical phrase: "We have today dispatched 20 cartons by lorry through Vaigai Roadways; the LR (No. 4587) and the packing list are enclosed."

Stage 6: DELIVERY

TermMeaning
Delivery challanA document that travels with the goods, listing the items and quantities; the buyer signs a copy on receipt.
Proof of delivery (POD)The signed and dated challan or carrier's receipt showing that the buyer has received the goods.
InspectionChecking the goods on arrival for quantity, quality and damage.
Goods Received Note (GRN)The buyer's own record of the goods received, their quantity and condition.
Shortage / damage reportThe buyer's note of goods missing or damaged on arrival; it supports a complaint or claim (and an insurance claim, if any).

Typical phrase: "We acknowledge receipt of the 20 cartons sent under your Delivery Challan No. 312 in good condition."

Stage 7: INVOICE

TermMeaning
InvoiceThe bill issued by the seller for goods supplied - quantity, price, taxes, total and due date.
Tax invoiceThe invoice issued by a GST-registered seller, showing the GSTIN, HSN code, taxable value and the GST charged.
GSTINGoods and Services Tax Identification Number - the 15-character number of a GST-registered business (e.g. 33XXXXXXXXXX1Z5; 33 = Tamil Nadu).
HSN codeHarmonised System of Nomenclature code - the code that classifies goods for tax (services use SAC codes).
E&OEErrors and Omissions Excepted - printed on invoices and quotations; the seller keeps the right to correct errors later.
Due dateThe last date by which payment must be made.
Credit periodThe number of days allowed for payment after the invoice date, e.g. 30 days.

Typical phrase: "Please find enclosed our Tax Invoice No. 1123 dated 12 October 2026 for Rs. 48,260, payable within 30 days."

Stage 8: PAYMENT

TermMeaning
RemittanceMoney sent by the buyer to the seller in payment.
NEFT / RTGSElectronic bank transfers: NEFT (National Electronic Funds Transfer) is settled in batches; RTGS (Real Time Gross Settlement) is settled one by one in real time and is meant for large amounts. Each transfer has a UTR number.
Demand draft (DD)A prepaid instrument issued by a bank, payable to the named payee; safer than a cheque because the bank has already received the money.
ChequeA written, unconditional order by an account holder (drawer) to his bank (drawee) to pay a stated sum on demand to the payee.
Advice of paymentThe buyer's letter telling the seller that payment has been made - amount, mode, UTR / cheque number and the invoices covered.
Part paymentPayment of only a part of the amount due.
Balance dueThe amount still to be paid after a part payment.

Typical phrase: "We have today remitted Rs. 48,260 by NEFT (UTR No. SCBN262851234567) in full settlement of your Invoice No. 1123."

Stage 9: RECEIPT

TermMeaning
ReceiptA written acknowledgement by the payee that money has been received - amount, date, mode and the invoice covered.
Acknowledgement of paymentThe seller's letter confirming that the payment has been received and credited to the buyer's account.
VoucherA document that supports an entry in the books of account (e.g. receipt voucher, payment voucher).
Statement of accountA statement listing the invoices, payments and the balance due.
Full and final settlementA payment that clears all dues, leaving no further claim.
Closing the accountSettling all dues so that the balance is nil; the transaction is complete.

Typical phrase: "We acknowledge with thanks the receipt of Rs. 48,260 by NEFT in full settlement of our Invoice No. 1123; your account now shows a nil balance."

DO NOT CONFUSE: Quotation (prices offered, before the order) - Proforma invoice (preliminary bill before supply, for advance payment or import) - Tax invoice (the actual bill for goods supplied).

DO NOT CONFUSE: Trade discount = for being a trader (deducted from the list price on the invoice). Cash discount = for paying quickly (allowed only if paid within the stated period).

MEMORY TRICK: -OR / -ER gives, -EE receives: the consignor sends, the consignee receives; the payer pays, the payee is paid; the endorser transfers, the endorsee receives. (Careful: the drawee of a cheque is the BANK that is ordered to pay.)

5.4 The 16 Transactional Letters

Quick-revision table; most of these letters have full cards and model letters in Section 3.

LetterWritten byPurposeKey phrase
InquiryBuyerAsks for prices, catalogue, samples and terms"Please send us your latest price list and terms of supply."
QuotationSellerStates prices, discounts, delivery and payment terms, and validity"We are pleased to quote as follows; this offer is valid for 30 days."
OrderBuyerPlaces a definite order - quantity, specifications, price, delivery schedule"Please supply the following items as per our Purchase Order No. ..."
AcceptanceSellerAccepts the order and confirms the delivery date and terms"We are pleased to accept your Order No. ... and will dispatch the goods by ..."
RejectionSellerDeclines the order politely, gives the reason and suggests an alternative"We regret that we are unable to accept your order because ..."
DispatchSellerInforms the buyer that the goods have been sent - carrier, packages, LR / RR / B/L / AWB number"We have today dispatched the goods by lorry; the LR is enclosed."
DeliverySeller or transporter (buyer confirms receipt)Fixes the delivery date, or confirms that the goods have arrived and in what condition"The goods will be delivered at your godown on ... against our Delivery Challan No. ..."
Invoice-related communicationSeller (buyer may query)Sends the invoice, corrects errors or answers queries on the bill"Please find enclosed our Invoice No. ... for Rs. ..., due for payment on ..."
PaymentBuyerSends the payment with its details (advice of payment)"We have remitted Rs. ... by NEFT (UTR No. ...) in full settlement of your Invoice No. ..."
ReminderSeller (buyer, for a late delivery - see 3.14)Reminds the buyer of an overdue payment - polite first, then firmer (see 4.15 and 4.16)"This is a reminder that our Invoice No. ... is now overdue."
ComplaintBuyerReports a problem - defect, delay, shortage or wrong goods"We regret to inform you that ten of the cartons received were damaged."
ClaimBuyerDemands a specific remedy - refund, replacement or price reduction"We request you to replace the damaged goods or refund Rs. ..."
AdjustmentSellerSettles the complaint or claim - full grant, partial grant or polite refusal with reasons"We apologise for the inconvenience and are sending replacements free of cost."
CancellationBuyerCancels an order before dispatch, giving the reason"We regret that we have to cancel our Order No. ... dated ..."
RefundSellerReturns money to the buyer - after a claim, a cancelled order or an overpayment"We have refunded Rs. ... to your account by NEFT today."
ReplacementSellerSends fresh goods in place of defective or wrong ones"Replacements for the defective items have been dispatched today, free of charge."

5.5 When Things Go Wrong

If the goods are defective, late, short or wrong, the letters follow this chain:

  1. COMPLAINTBuyer → Seller: reports the problem - defect, delay, shortage or wrong goods - quoting the order, invoice and LR numbers
  2. CLAIMBuyer → Seller: demands a specific remedy - refund, replacement or price reduction
  3. ADJUSTMENTSeller → Buyer: settles the complaint or claim - full grant, partial grant or polite refusal with reasons
  4. REFUND / REPLACEMENT / CREDIT NOTESeller → Buyer: returns the money, sends fresh goods, or reduces the amount the buyer owes

One line: COMPLAINT → CLAIM → ADJUSTMENT → REFUND / REPLACEMENT / CREDIT NOTE

DO NOT CONFUSE: CANCELLATION is not part of this chain. The buyer cancels an order BEFORE dispatch (e.g. delay in confirmation, change in requirement): quote the order number and date, give the reason and ask for written confirmation (and a refund of any advance). After dispatch, use complaint → claim → adjustment.

EXAM TIP: A buyer returning goods may send a debit note; the seller settles it with a credit note (see 4.19).

5.6 Delivery Terms and Transport Documents

Delivery Terms

TermFull formMeaningIn a quotation
Ex-worksEx-works (also ex-factory / ex-godown)The buyer collects the goods from the seller's premises; transport cost and risk from there are the buyer's."Rs. 450 per unit, ex-works Coimbatore"
FORFree on RailThe seller bears all expenses up to loading the goods on the railway wagon at the named station; the railway freight is paid by the buyer."Rs. 465 per unit, FOR Coimbatore"
FOBFree on BoardThe seller's cost and risk end when the goods are loaded on board the ship at the port of shipment; the buyer pays sea freight and insurance."USD 12 per unit, FOB Chennai"
CIFCost, Insurance and FreightThe seller pays cost, insurance and freight up to the destination port."USD 13.50 per unit, CIF Singapore"

DO NOT CONFUSE: FOB names the port of SHIPMENT ("FOB Chennai"); CIF names the port of DESTINATION ("CIF Singapore"). Under CIF the seller PAYS up to the destination port, but the risk passes to the buyer once the goods are on board.

Transport and Delivery Documents

DocumentModeIssued byUse
Lorry Receipt (LR)RoadRoad transport companyReceipt for goods booked by lorry; the consignee presents it to take delivery.
Railway Receipt (RR)RailRailwayReceipt for goods booked by rail; the consignee presents it to take delivery.
Bill of Lading (B/L)SeaShipping company (carrier)Receipt for goods shipped, evidence of the contract of carriage and a document of title to the goods.
Airway Bill (AWB)AirAirline or its agentReceipt for goods sent by air; it is not a document of title.
Delivery challanTravels with the goodsSeller (consignor)Lists the items and quantities; the buyer signs a copy as proof of delivery.
Goods Received Note (GRN)Buyer's own recordBuyer (stores department)Records the goods actually received and their condition; checked against the order and invoice before payment.

EXAM TIP: LR = road, RR = rail, B/L = sea, AWB = air. The bill of lading is a document of title and a key document under a Letter of Credit; the airway bill is not a document of title.

5.7 Memory Trick and Must Remember

MEMORY TRICK: "Intelligent Queens Order Costly Diamonds; Delivery Includes Proper Receipts" = I-Q-O-C-D-D-I-P-R = Inquiry, Quotation, Order, Confirmation, Dispatch, Delivery, Invoice, Payment, Receipt. (Dispatch before Delivery: goods leave before they arrive.)

MUST REMEMBER: Complaint = the buyer reports; Claim = the buyer demands a remedy; Adjustment = the seller's reply that settles it; then Refund / Replacement / Credit note. Cancellation comes BEFORE dispatch.

MUST REMEMBER: Quote the reference numbers - quotation, order, LR / B/L, invoice and UTR numbers - in every later letter. An order accepted by the seller becomes a binding contract.

6 Government and Administrative Correspondence

6.1 Introduction

Government offices do not use one kind of "letter" for everything. Each purpose has its own fixed form, with a fixed layout, a fixed opening phrase and a fixed rule for the salutation, the close and the signature. In the Government of India these forms are prescribed by the Central Secretariat Manual of Office Procedure (CSMOP); Tamil Nadu offices follow their own office manuals. For every form, learn four things: when it is used, who sends it to whom, its format and how it differs from a similar form.

Forms of official communication

GroupForms (see)Direction / use
LettersOfficial letter (6.25 a), D.O. letter (6.9), reminder (6.11), acknowledgement (6.16), forwarding letter (6.23), covering letter (6.24)Office → other governments, public bodies, officers and the public
Inter-office communicationsOffice Memorandum (6.3), memo (6.2), endorsement (6.10), inter-departmental note (U.O. note; U.O. = unofficial)Department ↔ department; within one office
OrdersGovernment Order (G.O.), Order, Office Order (6.4), Proceedings (6.8)Authority → officers, staff and persons concerned
AnnouncementsNotification (6.6), Resolution, circular (6.5), notice (6.7), press communique / press noteGovernment → many offices or the public
Requests and grievancesApplication (6.14), representation (6.12), petition (6.13), requisition (6.15)Citizen, employee or section → authority
Meetings and reportsAgenda (6.19), minutes (6.18), report (6.17)Secretary → members; officer → superior
Parts of a communicationReference (6.20), enclosure (6.21), annexure (6.22)Link a communication to earlier papers; attach documents

EXAM TIP: A press communique is formal, and the press is expected to print it exactly as issued; a press note is less formal, and the press may edit or shorten it. Both give wide publicity to a Government decision.

First person or third person?

Each form uses a fixed "person". In an official letter the officer writes "I am directed to ...": the "I" is the officer, but the decision belongs to the Government or the head of office, not to the writer. In an Office Memorandum the officer refers to himself or herself as "the undersigned" (third person). Orders, notifications, circulars and notices are impersonal. Only the D.O. letter is written in a personal, first-person voice.

PersonStandard openingUsed in
First person, on behalf of an authority"I am directed to ..."Official letter; reminders and forwarding letters sent by an office
First person, personal"I would like to draw your personal attention to ..."D.O. letter; also an individual's application or representation ("I request ...")
Third person ("the undersigned")"The undersigned is directed to ..."Office Memorandum
Third person / impersonal"It is hereby notified that ...", "It has been decided ...", "Sanction is hereby accorded to ..."Notification, Resolution, Order, Office Order, circular, notice, minutes

Formal and impersonal tone

Official writing is formal, impersonal, brief and exact. Write facts, not feelings. The passive voice is common because the decision belongs to the office, not to the person who signs ("Sanction is accorded", "It has been decided"). Standard phrases: with immediate effect; until further orders; for information and necessary action; under intimation to this office (= and inform this office); of even number (= bearing the same file number); vide (= see / as per); the letter cited (= the letter quoted in the Ref: line). Avoid outdated phrases: write "take the necessary action", not "do the needful"; write "reply", not "revert back" (see Section 8).

FeatureOfficial correspondence rule
Number and dateEvery communication carries a file number and a date: "No. 1234/A2/2026, dated 12 October 2026".
AddresseeOfficers are addressed by designation ("The District Collector, Madurai"), not by name - except in a D.O. letter.
PersonOfficial letter and D.O. letter: first person. Office Memorandum: third person ("the undersigned"). Orders, notifications, circulars, notices: impersonal.
Tone and voiceFormal, polite, firm and impersonal; the passive voice is common. Only the D.O. letter is personal and friendly.
SubjectOne communication deals with one subject. A short "Subject:" / "Sub:" line, with no full stop.
ReferencesEarlier communications are quoted by number and date in a "Ref:" line.
ParagraphsShort. Long communications have numbered paragraphs; in usual Government of India practice the first paragraph is not numbered and the rest are numbered 2, 3, ...
Salutation and closeOnly in letters: official letter "Sir/Madam," + "Yours faithfully,"; D.O. letter "Dear Thiru X," + "Yours sincerely,". Office Memorandum, orders, notifications, circulars, notices and memos have neither.
SignatureName and designation. An officer signing on behalf of the head of office writes "for" before the designation ("for District Collector, Madurai").
CopiesA "Copy to:" list, or a signed endorsement: "Copy forwarded to ... for information and necessary action."
ChannelAn employee writes to a higher authority through proper channel: the paper goes through the immediate superior (a "Through: ..." line is written above or below the addressee).

6.2 Memorandum

DefinitionA short internal written communication within an organisation. It has a To / From / Date / Subject header and no salutation and no complimentary close. Short form: memo; plural: memoranda or memorandums.
PurposeTo pass information, instructions, requests or reminders quickly to people in the same organisation, and to keep a written record. In Indian offices a memo is also issued to an employee to call for an explanation (e.g. for unauthorised absence).
Who issues itAny officer or manager, or the head of a section or department of the organisation.
Who receives itStaff, colleagues, sections or departments of the same organisation.
Differs fromLetter: goes outside the organisation and has a salutation and a close; a memo stays inside and has neither. Office Memorandum: a Government form sent between Ministries/Departments in the third person (6.3). (In Indian news, "submitted a memorandum to the Minister" means a written statement of demands, like a petition.)
Example
MEMORANDUM To: All Section Heads From: S. Meena, Administrative Officer Date: 12 October 2026 Subject: Submission of attendance registers Please send the attendance registers for September to this office by 15 October 2026.

6.3 Office Memorandum (O.M.) HIGH-FREQUENCY

DefinitionA Government form of communication used between Ministries/Departments (also with attached offices and with employees). It is written in the third person ("The undersigned is directed to ..."), has no salutation and no complimentary close, and is signed with the officer's name and designation.
PurposeTo convey decisions, instructions or information to other Departments; to call for information; to reply to employees (e.g. on their requests). An O.M. addressed to all Ministries works as a circular.
Who issues itA Ministry/Department; signed by an authorised officer (e.g. an Under Secretary or a Deputy Secretary).
Who receives itOther Ministries/Departments, attached and subordinate offices, and the Department's own employees. The addressee ("To") is written at the bottom left, below the signature.
Differs fromOfficial letter: first person ("I am directed to ..."), with "Sir/Madam," and "Yours faithfully,"; used for other governments, public bodies and the public. The O.M. is third person, with no salutation and no close. Memorandum (memo): a short internal note in any organisation (6.2).
Example"The undersigned is directed to refer to this Department's O.M. of even number dated 1 September 2026 and to say that ..." (of even number = bearing the same file number)

6.4 Office Order

DefinitionA written order about the internal administration of an office: distribution of work, grant of leave, postings within the office and similar matters. No salutation, no close.
PurposeTo give binding instructions to the staff of the office on internal matters, and to keep a record of them.
Who issues itThe Head of Office, or an officer authorised by him or her (e.g. the Administrative Officer).
Who receives itThe employees concerned in the same office; copies go to the sections concerned and to the employees' personal files.
Differs fromOrder: used for financial sanctions and disciplinary matters; an Office Order deals with routine internal administration. Circular: gives the same information to many offices or persons; an Office Order is a binding instruction within one office.
Example"Thiru R. Arun, Junior Assistant, Establishment Section, is posted to the Accounts Section with immediate effect."

6.5 Circular HIGH-FREQUENCY

DefinitionA communication that carries the same information or instructions to many offices or persons at the same time. A government circular has no salutation and no complimentary close.
PurposeTo make sure that everyone concerned gets the same message at once: new instructions, rules, holidays, meetings, campaigns. Receivers are often asked to acknowledge receipt.
Who issues itA Government department or a Head of Office; also companies, banks, schools and associations.
Who receives itA group: all offices under a department, all staff, all members, or all students and parents.
Differs fromNotice: displayed on a notice board or published for people to read; a circular is sent to each office or person. Notification: published in the Official Gazette; a circular is not. A business circular letter (e.g. to customers) is different: it has a salutation such as "Dear Customer,".
Example"All Heads of Offices are requested to send the list of vacant posts in their offices to this office by 30 October 2026."

6.6 Notification HIGH-FREQUENCY

DefinitionA formal Government communication that publishes rules, orders, appointments, promotions, etc. in the Official Gazette (the Gazette of India or the Tamil Nadu Government Gazette).
PurposeTo make a rule, order or appointment known officially to everyone. Publication in the Gazette is the official, legal way of announcing it; statutory rules and orders are "notified".
Who issues itA Ministry/Department of the Central or State Government, in the name of the President or the Governor; signed by an authorised officer (e.g. a Secretary or Joint Secretary to Government).
Who receives itThe public at large (through the Gazette); copies go to the offices and persons concerned.
Differs fromNotice: a simple announcement on a notice board or in a newspaper, usually not in the Gazette. Resolution: also published in the Gazette, but announces important policy decisions or the appointment of committees or commissions. In everyday use, a recruitment advertisement (e.g. a TNPSC recruitment notification) is also called a "notification".
Example"It is hereby notified that Thiru S. Suresh, Deputy Secretary to Government, is promoted as Joint Secretary to Government with effect from 1 October 2026."

6.7 Notice

DefinitionA short formal announcement of an event, decision, rule or warning, put up on a notice board, published in a newspaper, or served on a particular person. A public notice has the name of the office, the word "NOTICE", a date, a title, the message, and the name and designation of the issuing authority; no salutation, no close. (A legal notice served on one person is often written as a letter, with a salutation and a close - see Section 4.)
PurposeTo inform a group or the public (meeting, holiday, tender, auction), or to give a formal warning or intimation to a person (legal notice; show-cause notice, which asks a person to explain why action should not be taken).
Who issues itThe head of an office or institution, the secretary of an association, a Government authority, a court, or an advocate on behalf of a client.
Who receives itThe persons concerned (staff, students, members, the general public), or one named person (legal notice, show-cause notice).
Differs fromCircular: sent to each office or person; a notice is displayed or published for all to read. Notification: published in the Official Gazette. Agenda: a notice of a meeting gives the date, time and place; the agenda lists the items to be discussed.
Example"NOTICE: The office will remain closed on 2 October 2026 on account of Gandhi Jayanti."

6.8 Proceedings

DefinitionIn Tamil Nadu, the form in which Heads of Departments and District officers issue orders. It is headed "Proceedings of the ..." (e.g. "Proceedings of the District Collector, Madurai") and has fixed parts: Present:, Rc. No., Dated, Sub:, Ref: and ORDER:.
PurposeTo record a decision and issue orders on a matter (appointment, transfer, posting, leave, sanction), together with the references on which the order is based.
Who issues itA Head of Department or a District officer such as the District Collector. The "Present:" line names the officer who passes the order.
Who receives itThe person or office concerned ("To"), with copies to the other offices concerned ("Copy to").
Differs fromGovernment Order (G.O.): issued by the Secretariat departments of the State Government; Proceedings are issued below the Secretariat, by Heads of Departments and District officers. (In general English, "proceedings" also means the events of a meeting or a legal case, e.g. "court proceedings".)
Example"Proceedings of the District Collector, Madurai. Present: Tmt. K. Priya, I.A.S. Rc. No. 1234/A2/2026, Dated 12 October 2026." (full format: 6.25 g)

6.9 D.O. Letter (Demi-official letter) HIGH-FREQUENCY

DefinitionA letter from one officer to another, written in the first person singular in a personal, friendly tone. It begins "Dear Shri X," (Tamil Nadu: "Dear Thiru X,"), ends "Yours sincerely," and carries a D.O. No. at the top.
PurposeTo draw the personal attention of the officer addressed; to expedite a delayed matter; to exchange views or information without the formality of official correspondence.
Who issues itA Government officer, in his or her own name; the sender's name and designation are printed at the top.
Who receives itAnother officer, addressed by name. The addressee's name and address are usually written at the bottom left, below the signature.
Differs fromOfficial letter: impersonal; addressed by designation; "Sir/Madam," + "Yours faithfully,"; "I am directed to ...". The D.O. letter is personal; addressed by name; "Dear Thiru X," + "Yours sincerely,"; written in the officer's own voice.
Example"Dear Thiru Vignesh, I shall be grateful if you could personally look into the pension papers of Thiru M. Ganesan, retired Tahsildar, which have been pending with your office since June."

6.10 Endorsement HIGH-FREQUENCY

Definition(Government) a short note written at the foot of a communication to forward copies ("Copy forwarded to ... for information / necessary action") or to return a paper to the sender. (Banking) a signature on the back of a cheque or other negotiable instrument to transfer it.
PurposeTo send copies, pass a paper on, or return it quickly - without writing a separate letter.
Who issues itThe office that issues (or has received) the communication; the endorsement carries the file number and date and is signed separately.
Who receives itOffices or persons other than the main addressee (for copies); the original sender (when a paper is returned).
Differs fromForwarding / covering letter: a separate letter; an endorsement is only a note on the same paper. "Copy to:": a plain list of copy holders; an endorsement is numbered, dated, signed and states the purpose ("for information", "for necessary action").
Example"Copy forwarded to the Tahsildar, Madurai North, for information and necessary action." (full format: 6.25 h)

6.11 Reminder

DefinitionA follow-up communication sent when a reply or action on an earlier communication is still awaited. It quotes the number and date of the earlier communication.
PurposeTo draw attention to the pending matter and ask for an early reply or action.
Who issues itThe office or person that sent the original communication.
Who receives itThe office or person from whom the reply or action is due.
Differs fromFresh letter: explains the whole matter; a reminder only refers to the earlier letter and asks for the reply. D.O. letter: if formal reminders fail, a D.O. letter is often sent to draw the officer's personal attention. (A seller's payment reminders to a buyer form a separate collection series.)
Example"I am directed to invite your attention to this office letter No. 1234/A2/2026 dated 1 September 2026 and to request you to send a reply at an early date."

6.12 Representation HIGH-FREQUENCY

DefinitionA formal written statement by an individual (usually an employee or a citizen) to an authority, presenting a grievance or asking for reconsideration of a decision. An employee usually sends it through proper channel.
PurposeTo seek redress of a personal grievance (pay, seniority, promotion, transfer) or to ask the authority to reconsider an order.
Who issues itOne person - usually an employee (service matters) or a citizen (his or her own case).
Who receives itThe competent or higher authority; an employee's representation goes through the immediate superior, who forwards it with remarks.
Differs fromPetition: often signed by many people and seeks redress or action on a common problem; a representation is usually one person's own case. Application: asks for something new under the rules (leave, a certificate); a representation states a grievance or questions a decision already taken.
Example"I request you to reconsider my transfer to Tirunelveli, as my mother is undergoing treatment in a hospital in Madurai."

6.13 Petition HIGH-FREQUENCY

DefinitionA formal request to an authority seeking redress or action, often signed by many people. It is also a legal term for a formal application to a court (e.g. a writ petition).
PurposeTo seek relief or action on a common problem (roads, drinking water, street lights, a bus service) or, in a court, to seek legal relief.
Who issues itA group of citizens, residents or members (sometimes one person); in a court, the petitioner.
Who receives itAn authority such as the District Collector, a Commissioner, a Minister or the Chief Minister; in legal use, a court.
Differs fromRepresentation: usually one person's own grievance (often an employee's service matter), sent through proper channel; a petition is often collective and goes directly to the authority. In law, a writ petition is filed in a High Court (Article 226) or in the Supreme Court (Article 32).
Example"We, the undersigned residents of Anna Nagar, Salem, request you to provide street lights on the main road, as the area is unsafe after dark." (the residents' signatures follow)

6.14 Application

DefinitionA formal written request by a person to an authority for something he or she is eligible for: a job, leave, a certificate, a licence, admission, a loan, or information (e.g. under the Right to Information Act, 2005).
PurposeTo obtain a benefit, a permission or a service allowed by the rules.
Who issues itAn individual: a candidate, employee, student or citizen.
Who receives itThe authority competent to grant it (employer, Head of Office, Principal, Tahsildar, Public Information Officer). An employee's application to a higher authority goes through proper channel.
Differs fromRepresentation: states a grievance or asks for reconsideration of a decision already taken; an application asks for something new. Requisition: an office's demand for supplies, staff or an action; an application is an individual's request.
Example"I request you to grant me casual leave for two days, on 14 and 15 October 2026, to attend my sister's wedding."

6.15 Requisition

DefinitionA formal written demand or request for supplies, staff or an action - e.g. a requisition to the stores for stationery, or a requisition by members for convening a meeting.
PurposeTo obtain what the office officially needs (stationery, equipment, vehicles, staff), or to require an action such as calling a special meeting.
Who issues itA section, department or officer that needs the items or staff; members of a company, society or association (for a meeting).
Who receives itThe stores or purchase section, the appointing or recruiting authority, or the Secretary / Board (for a meeting).
Differs fromApplication: an individual's request for a personal benefit; a requisition is an official demand for the office's needs or for an action. Purchase order: goes to an outside supplier; a requisition is an internal request that usually comes before the purchase order.
Example"We, the undersigned members, request you to convene a special general body meeting of the Association to discuss the revision of the annual subscription."

6.16 Acknowledgement

DefinitionA short communication (or a receipt slip) confirming that a letter, application, document, payment or goods has been received.
PurposeTo assure the sender that the item has reached; to give a reference number and date for follow-up. It may add that a detailed reply will follow.
Who issues itThe receiving office or person (in offices, the receipt section that registers incoming mail - called "dak" in Central Government offices and "tapal" in Tamil Nadu offices).
Who receives itThe sender of the original item.
Differs fromReply: deals with the matter itself; an acknowledgement only confirms receipt. Acknowledgement Due (AD) card: the postal card sent with registered post, signed by the addressee and returned to the sender as proof of delivery.
Example"Your application dated 5 October 2026 has been received and registered as No. 1234/A2/2026. A reply will be sent in due course."

6.17 Report

DefinitionA formal written account of facts, findings or progress (or of the result of an inspection or inquiry), usually with conclusions and recommendations, submitted to the authority that asked for it or at fixed intervals (e.g. a monthly progress report).
PurposeTo give the authority accurate facts so that it can decide or act.
Who issues itAn officer, inspector, inquiry officer or committee.
Who receives itThe superior authority, or the body that called for it.
Differs fromMinutes: the record of one meeting's discussions and decisions; a report presents facts and findings on a subject, often with recommendations. Letter: a report is longer, arranged under headings (Introduction, Findings, Conclusions, Recommendations) and written in an impersonal style.
Example"As directed in the reference cited, the undersigned inspected the Panchayat Union Middle School, Pudur, on 9 October 2026 and submits the following report."

6.18 Minutes HIGH-FREQUENCY

DefinitionThe official written record of a meeting's proceedings and decisions, prepared after the meeting and confirmed at the next meeting. Minutes are written in the past tense and in the third person (reported speech).
PurposeTo keep a permanent, authentic record of who attended, what was discussed, what was decided (resolutions) and who will act.
Who issues itThe Secretary (or convener) of the meeting prepares them; the Chairperson signs them after they are confirmed.
Who receives itAll members (present and absent) and the officers who must act on the decisions; the original is kept in the minute book.
Differs fromAgenda: the list of items circulated before the meeting; minutes are written after it. Report: deals with a subject or an inquiry; minutes cover one meeting only.
Example
Minutes of the Staff Welfare Committee meeting held on 9 October 2026 Present: Tmt. R. Kavya (Chairperson) and eight members 1. The minutes of the last meeting were read and confirmed. 2. Resolved that the Annual Day be held on 18 December 2026.

6.19 Agenda HIGH-FREQUENCY

DefinitionThe list of items to be discussed at a meeting, in the order in which they will be taken up. It is circulated before the meeting, with the notice of the meeting.
PurposeTo let members prepare in advance, and to keep the meeting orderly and on time.
Who issues itThe Secretary or convener, with the approval of the Chairperson.
Who receives itAll members entitled to attend the meeting.
Differs fromMinutes: the record written after the meeting. Notice of meeting: gives the date, time and place; the agenda gives the items (the two are usually sent together). Grammar: "agenda" is used as a singular noun today ("The agenda is ready"; plural "agendas").
Example
AGENDA 1. Confirmation of the minutes of the last meeting 2. Arrangements for the Annual Day 3. Any other matter with the permission of the Chair

6.20 Reference

DefinitionIn official correspondence, the number and date of an earlier letter, order or document on the same matter, quoted in a "Ref:" line (or in the text) so that the receiver can trace the file.
PurposeTo link the present communication with earlier ones, and to save time in finding the background of the matter.
Who issues itThe writer of the communication, who quotes the earlier papers.
Who receives itThe addressee, who uses it to find the file.
Differs fromSubject: says what the letter is about; the reference says which earlier communication it follows. In job applications, a "reference" (referee) is a person who can speak about the candidate's character and work - a different meaning.
Example"Ref: Your letter No. 567/B1/2026 dated 21 September 2026." In the body, "the letter cited" means the letter quoted in the Ref: line.

6.21 Enclosure

DefinitionA separate document sent along with a letter (e.g. a copy of a certificate, a bill, a cheque, a form). It is listed at the foot of the letter, below the signature, after "Encl.".
PurposeTo send supporting papers and tell the receiver exactly what is attached, so that nothing is missed.
Who issues itThe sender of the letter.
