I have also submitted the following documents to the [HR / ADMINISTRATION] department: [LIST OF DOCUMENTS]. I thank you for giving me this opportunity. I request you to accept my joining report and take it on record. I assure you that I shall perform my duties with sincerity and dedication. Yours faithfully, [SIGNATURE] [NAME] [DESIGNATION]

EXAM TIP: Appointment letter: employer → a named candidate, so "Dear Mr./Ms. [SURNAME]," + "Yours sincerely,", with post, salary, probation, joining date and a deadline for acceptance. Joining report: employee → employer on the day of joining, quoting the appointment letter's number and date.

MUST REMEMBER: All 17 templates share 5 things - (1) full-block layout: every line starts at the left margin, with one blank line between blocks; (2) the sender's address (or letterhead) at the top and the date below it, written as 12 October 2026 - your name never goes at the top; (3) a salutation that matches the close: Dear Sir/Madam, → Yours faithfully, | Dear Mr. Raman, → Yours sincerely, | Dear Priya, → Your loving friend, (the heading TO WHOM IT MAY CONCERN also takes Yours faithfully,); (4) a three-part body: opening (why you write) → main (facts and details) → closing (the action you want and a polite end); (5) a signature block: close, signature and name - plus designation when you write in an official capacity. Formal and business templates also carry the "To" block and the Subject BEFORE the salutation.

10 Full Examination-Style Examples

Every example in this section follows the same six steps: Question → Situation → Correct format → Complete answer → Important vocabulary → Why the format is correct. The letter sheet in each example is the complete answer - learn its layout line by line. Examples 10.1 to 10.8 are informal (personal) letters and 10.9 to 10.11 are semi-formal; formal, official and business letters follow from 10.12. For blank templates, see Section 9; for register differences, see Section 12.

MUST REMEMBER: Exam identity rule - if the question gives a name and address, use exactly those (every example below does this). If it gives none, write "Examination Hall" as the address and sign as "ABC" or "XYZ" - in informal letters too ("Your loving son, ABC"). Never reveal your real identity in an exam answer.

10.1 Letter to a Parent Informal

Question: You are Arun, a first-year B.Sc. student staying in Room No. 21, Men's Hostel, Guindy, Chennai - 600 032. Write a letter to your father describing your hostel life and asking for his permission and Rs. 4,500 for a college educational tour. (about 150-200 words)

Situation: Writer: Arun, a first-year student in a Chennai college hostel | Receiver: his father | Relationship: son and father | Purpose: to share news of hostel life and ask for permission and money for an educational tour | Register: Informal

Correct format: Sender's address (short) → Date → "Dear Appa," → Body (home news; hostel life; the tour request; family greetings) → "Your loving son," → First name. No subject line and no receiver's address.

Room No. 21, Men's Hostel, Guindy, Chennai - 600 032 12 October 2026 Dear Appa, I received your letter yesterday and I'm happy to know that everyone at home is well. I'm fine here, and I'm slowly getting used to hostel life. The hostel is clean and the food is simple but tasty. I share my room with two classmates, Hari from Salem and Pradeep from Erode. We get up at six, go for a short walk and study together from seven to nine every night. The warden is strict about timings, but he's kind and helpful. On Sundays, I wash my clothes and play cricket with my friends. Now I have some good news. Our college has organised a four-day educational tour to Bengaluru and Mysuru from 10 to 13 November. We'll visit a science museum, a dairy plant and a software company, and two of our professors will travel with us. The total cost is Rs. 4,500, including travel, food and stay, and it has to be paid by 25 October. Please give me your permission and send the amount to my bank account. I promise to spend it carefully. Please convey my love to Amma and tell Meena to study well for her half-yearly exams. I'm eagerly waiting for your reply. Your loving son, Arun

Important vocabulary: get used to - become familiar with something new (followed by a noun or an -ing form: "getting used to hostel life"); warden - the person in charge of a hostel; educational tour - a trip arranged so that students can learn outside the classroom; organise - arrange, plan (British spelling; American "organize"); permission - approval to do something; convey - pass on (greetings or a message); eagerly - with keen interest and expectation.

Why this format is correct:

  • A letter to a parent is personal, so it is informal: short address, date, "Dear Appa," and no subject line or receiver's address.
  • "Dear Appa," pairs with "Your loving son,"; only the first name (Arun) follows. "Yours faithfully," never belongs in a family letter.
  • One idea per paragraph: home news → hostel life → the request → family greetings.
  • The request is specific - what (permission and Rs. 4,500), why (an educational tour) and by when (25 October) - so the father can act at once.
  • Contractions (I'm, he's, we'll) are allowed in informal letters like this one; never use them in formal letters.

10.2 Congratulations Letter to a Friend Informal

Question: You are Kavya of 24, Anna Nagar, Madurai - 625 020. Your friend Nandhini has cleared the TNPSC Group 4 examination. Write a letter congratulating her. (about 150-200 words)

Situation: Writer: Kavya | Receiver: her close friend Nandhini | Relationship: friends | Purpose: to congratulate Nandhini on clearing the TNPSC Group 4 examination and wish her well | Register: Informal

Correct format: Sender's address (short) → Date → "Dear Nandhini," → Body (congratulations at once; praise for her effort; good wishes; a plan to meet) → "Your loving friend," → First name. No subject line and no receiver's address.

24, Anna Nagar, Madurai - 625 020 28 October 2026 Dear Nandhini, Congratulations on clearing the TNPSC Group 4 examination! Your mother phoned mine this morning with the good news, and I was so happy that I decided to write to you at once. You truly deserve this success. I still remember how you studied at the district library every day and revised your notes late into the night. Last time, you missed the cut-off by just a few marks, but you didn't lose heart. You worked on your weak areas, took a mock test every week and kept going. Your hard work and patience have finally paid off. I'm sure you'll be an honest and caring public servant who treats every citizen with respect. Your success has inspired me too. I've decided to prepare seriously for the next Group 2 examination, and I'll need your guidance. Please give my regards to your parents. They must be very proud of you. Let's celebrate together when you come home to Madurai next month. Once again, many congratulations and best wishes for a bright career! Your loving friend, Kavya

Important vocabulary: clear (an examination) - pass it successfully; deserve - have earned something by your own effort; cut-off - the minimum mark needed to be selected; lose heart - become discouraged; mock test - a practice test in the pattern of the real examination; pay off - (of effort) bring success; public servant - a person who works for the government and serves the public; inspire - make someone want to do something good.

Why this format is correct:

  • A letter to a close friend is informal: short address, date, "Dear Nandhini," and no subject line or receiver's address.
  • The congratulations come in the very first sentence, so the purpose is clear at once.
  • Specific memories (the library, the missed cut-off, the weekly mock tests) make the praise sincere and personal.
  • Preposition: congratulate someone on something - "Congratulations on clearing ...".
  • Exclamation marks are acceptable in an informal letter to show joy; never use them in a formal letter.

10.3 Invitation Letter to a Friend Informal

Question: You are Meena of 7, Gandhiji Road, Thanjavur - 613 001. Write a letter inviting your friend Janani to your elder sister's wedding in Thanjavur. (about 150-200 words)

Situation: Writer: Meena | Receiver: her close friend Janani, who lives in another town | Relationship: friends | Purpose: to invite Janani to her elder sister's wedding and give the date, time and venue | Register: Informal

Correct format: Sender's address (short) → Date → "Dear Janani," → Body (the happy news; date, time and venue; stay and travel help; a warm request to come) → "Your loving friend," → First name. No subject line and no receiver's address.

7, Gandhiji Road, Thanjavur - 613 001 5 November 2026 Dear Janani, I hope you and your family are keeping well. I'm writing with some happy news: my elder sister Revathi is getting married to Prakash, a bank officer from Salem. The wedding will take place on Sunday, 6 December 2026, at Sri Lakshmi Mahal, Medical College Road, Thanjavur. The muhurtham is between 9.00 and 10.30 a.m. There will be a reception at the same venue at 7.00 p.m. on the previous evening, Saturday, 5 December. I've enclosed the invitation card, but I wanted to invite you personally too. Please come a day early with your parents and stay with us. Appa will pick you up from the railway station, so don't worry about the journey. After the wedding, I'll take you to see the Big Temple, which you've always wanted to visit. It won't feel like a real celebration without you, so please don't say no. Revathi Akka has also asked me to tell you that she's expecting you. Do reply soon and let me know your travel plans. Your loving friend, Meena

Important vocabulary: get married to - marry ("married to", not "married with"); take place - happen (used for planned events); muhurtham - the auspicious time fixed for the wedding ceremony; reception - a party held to celebrate a wedding and welcome the guests; venue - the place where an event is held; personally - yourself, not only through a printed card; pick (someone) up - go and collect someone, usually in a vehicle.

Why this format is correct:

  • An invitation to a friend is informal: short address, date, "Dear Janani," and no subject line or receiver's address.
  • All the key facts - whose wedding, day, date, time and venue - are grouped in one paragraph, so the reader can plan her journey.
  • The day, date and time are written in full ("Sunday, 6 December 2026"; "between 9.00 and 10.30 a.m.") so that there is no confusion.
  • Personal touches (stay with us, a pick-up from the station, a visit to the Big Temple) are what a printed invitation card cannot give.
  • It ends with a warm request for a reply, then "Your loving friend," and the first name.

10.4 Thank-You Letter to an Uncle Informal

Question: You are Karthik of 36, Race Course Road, Coimbatore - 641 018. Write a letter to your uncle thanking him for the English dictionary he sent you as a birthday gift. (about 150-200 words)

Situation: Writer: Karthik, a student | Receiver: his uncle | Relationship: nephew and uncle (an elder relative) | Purpose: to thank his uncle for the birthday gift and say how useful it is | Register: Informal

Correct format: Sender's address (short) → Date → "Dear Uncle," → Body (thanks for the gift; how you use it; family news; thanks again) → "Your loving nephew," → First name. No subject line and no receiver's address.

36, Race Course Road, Coimbatore - 641 018 20 August 2026 Dear Uncle, Thank you very much for the wonderful birthday gift. The English dictionary reached me on the morning of my birthday, and it was the best surprise of the day. I was also touched by the kind message you wrote on the first page. It's exactly what I needed. As you know, I often find English spelling and pronunciation difficult. This dictionary gives the pronunciation of every word in phonetic symbols, along with its meanings, synonyms, antonyms and example sentences. Every night, I learn ten new words and write a sentence with each of them in a notebook. I've also started to look up every new word I come across in the newspaper. My English teacher saw the dictionary last week and advised me to use it every day. Amma and Appa send their regards to you and Aunty. We're all looking forward to your visit during the Deepavali holidays. Thank you once again for such a thoughtful and useful gift. I'll treasure it always. Your loving nephew, Karthik

Important vocabulary: thoughtful - showing care for another person's needs; pronunciation - the way a word is spoken (spelling: pronunciation, not "pronounciation"); phonetic symbols - signs that show how a word is pronounced; synonym / antonym - a word with the same / the opposite meaning; look up - search for a word in a dictionary; come across - find or meet by chance; treasure - keep something carefully because it is precious to you.

Why this format is correct:

  • A letter to a relative is informal: short address, date, "Dear Uncle," and no subject line or receiver's address.
  • A good thank-you letter names the gift and says HOW it is useful; this makes the thanks sincere, not a mere formality.
  • "Your loving nephew," follows the family pattern "Your loving + relation" (son, daughter, nephew, niece); only the first name follows.
  • Repeating the thanks in the last paragraph is a natural, polite way to end this type of letter.
  • Grammar point: "Thank you for + noun or -ing form" - "Thank you for the wonderful gift", "Thank you for sending it" (never "Thank you for send ...").

10.5 Condolence Letter to a Friend Informal

Question: You are Divya of 10, Periyar Nagar, Erode - 638 001. Write a letter of condolence to your friend Harini, whose grandfather passed away last week. (about 150-200 words)

Situation: Writer: Divya | Receiver: her close friend Harini | Relationship: friends | Purpose: to express sympathy on the death of Harini's grandfather and offer comfort and help | Register: Informal (sensitive, dignified tone)

Correct format: Sender's address (short) → Date → "Dear Harini," → Body (sympathy; a warm memory of the grandfather; comfort and an offer of help; the family's sympathy) → "Your loving friend," → First name. No subject line and no receiver's address.

10, Periyar Nagar, Erode - 638 001 16 September 2026 Dear Harini, I was deeply saddened to hear that your grandfather passed away last week. Please accept my heartfelt condolences. You were very close to him, and I can only imagine how much you and your family must be missing him. I have many happy memories of him. Whenever I visited your house, he welcomed me with a warm smile and told us stories from his years as a school headmaster. He always said that hard work is never wasted, and I still remember those words before every examination. He lived a full and meaningful life, and he was respected by everyone who knew him. I know that no words can ease your grief at this time. Please take care of your health and stay strong for your grandmother, who needs your support now. I will come to see you next Sunday. Until then, if there is anything I can do, please do not hesitate to call me. My parents join me in offering our deepest sympathy to you and your family. Your loving friend, Divya

Important vocabulary: condolences - words of sympathy when someone has died ("Please accept my heartfelt condolences"); pass away - die (a gentle, polite expression); deeply saddened - very sad; heartfelt - sincere, deeply felt; grief - deep sorrow, especially after a death; ease - make less painful; hesitate - pause before doing something because you are unsure; sympathy - sharing another person's sorrow.

Why this format is correct:

  • It is a letter to a close friend, so the layout is informal: short address, date, "Dear Harini," and no subject line or receiver's address.
  • The tone is dignified: no jokes, no exclamation marks, no news about yourself, and full forms (I will, do not) instead of contractions.
  • A simple, safe order: sympathy first → a good memory of the person → comfort and a practical offer of help.
  • Gentle expressions ("passed away", "Please accept my heartfelt condolences") are preferred to blunt ones.
  • It is short and sincere; a condolence letter is not the place for long advice or philosophy.

10.6 Apology Letter to a Friend Informal

Question: You are Vignesh of 9, Bazaar Street, Salem - 636 001. Write a letter to your friend Gokul apologising for missing his birthday party. (about 150-200 words)

Situation: Writer: Vignesh | Receiver: his close friend Gokul | Relationship: friends | Purpose: to apologise for missing Gokul's birthday party, explain why and make amends | Register: Informal

Correct format: Sender's address (short) → Date → "Dear Gokul," → Body (apology; the honest reason; making amends; belated wishes) → "Your loving friend," → First name. No subject line and no receiver's address.

9, Bazaar Street, Salem - 636 001 14 July 2026 Dear Gokul, I'm really sorry that I missed your birthday party last Saturday. I know you were expecting me, and I feel very bad about letting you down. Just as I was getting ready to leave that evening, my grandmother slipped in the bathroom and hurt her knee. Appa was away in Chennai, so I had to take her to the hospital. The doctor examined her and asked for an X-ray. Luckily, nothing was broken, but by the time we reached home, it was almost ten o'clock. In all the confusion, I forgot to call you, and my phone battery had run out too. I should have let you know, and I'm sorry that I didn't. I've bought you a small gift: the book of detective stories that you've wanted for a long time. Let's meet for lunch next Sunday, and I'll give it to you then. It'll be my treat, and I promise to make up for missing your special day. Please forgive me. Belated birthday wishes, and many happy returns! Your loving friend, Vignesh

Important vocabulary: let (someone) down - disappoint someone who was depending on you; slip - lose your balance on a smooth or wet surface; run out - be completely used up (battery, money, time); should have + past participle - shows regret about something not done in the past ("I should have let you know"); make up for - do something good to correct a mistake; treat - something special that you pay for to please someone ("It'll be my treat"); belated - coming late.

Why this format is correct:

  • A letter to a friend is informal: short address, date, "Dear Gokul," and no subject line or receiver's address.
  • Remember A-R-M for an apology: Apologise at once → give the Reason honestly → Make amends (a gift, a plan to meet).
  • The writer takes responsibility ("I should have let you know") instead of blaming others; this makes the apology sincere.
  • One clear reason is enough; do not pile up excuses. Keep the focus on the friend's feelings ("I know you were expecting me").
  • Belated wishes end the letter on a warm note before "Your loving friend," and the first name.

10.7 Advice Letter to a Younger Brother Informal

Question: You are Lakshmi of 18, Bharathi Street, Velachery, Chennai - 600 042. Your younger brother Kavin is in Class 12 and spends too much time on his mobile phone. Write a letter advising him on the wise use of the mobile phone in his board-exam year. (about 150-200 words)

Situation: Writer: Lakshmi, Kavin's elder sister | Receiver: her younger brother Kavin, a Class 12 student | Relationship: elder sister and younger brother | Purpose: to advise him to use his mobile phone wisely in his board-exam year | Register: Informal

Correct format: Sender's address (short) → Date → "My dear Kavin," → Body (reason for writing; a balanced view of the phone; practical advice; encouragement) → "Yours affectionately," → First name. No subject line and no receiver's address.

18, Bharathi Street, Velachery, Chennai - 600 042 2 November 2026 My dear Kavin, I hope you're well and preparing seriously for your board examinations. Amma told me on the phone that you've been spending a lot of time on your mobile phone these days. That's why I'm writing to you. Don't get me wrong. A mobile phone is a useful tool if you use it wisely. You can watch video lessons, look up difficult topics and download previous years' question papers. The problem begins when you open a game or social media "for just five minutes" and an hour disappears. Then the phone becomes a distraction. This is your board-exam year, and every hour counts. Here's my advice. Keep your phone in the living room, not in your study room. Turn off notifications while you study. Limit entertainment to half an hour after dinner, and don't use the phone for an hour before bed, so that you can sleep well. You'll be surprised how much more you can finish in a day. I'm not asking you to give up the phone. I'm only asking you to control it instead of letting it control you. I'm sure you'll make us all proud with your results. Take care of your health. Yours affectionately, Lakshmi

Important vocabulary: Don't get me wrong - do not misunderstand me; wisely - with good sense and judgement; distraction - something that takes your attention away from what you should be doing; every hour counts - every hour is important; notifications - alerts that a phone shows for new messages and updates; limit - keep within a fixed amount; give up - stop doing or using something completely.

Why this format is correct:

  • A letter to a younger brother is informal: short address, date, "My dear Kavin," and no subject line or receiver's address.
  • "Yours affectionately," suits an elder writing to a younger family member; the first name follows.
  • The advice is balanced and practical: it accepts the phone's good uses and gives clear steps instead of only scolding.
  • Short imperative sentences ("Keep your phone ...", "Turn off ...") make the advice easy to follow.
  • It ends with encouragement and care, so the tone stays loving, not preachy.

10.8 Letter Describing a Visit Informal

Question: You are Suresh of 22, Gandhi Nagar, Vellore - 632 006. Write a letter to your friend Dinesh describing your visit to Mamallapuram (Mahabalipuram) during the Pongal holidays. (about 150-200 words)

Situation: Writer: Suresh | Receiver: his close friend Dinesh | Relationship: friends | Purpose: to describe a family trip to Mamallapuram and invite Dinesh to visit it | Register: Informal

Correct format: Sender's address (short) → Date → "Dear Dinesh," → Body (when and where; a little background; the places seen, in order; feelings; an invitation) → "Your loving friend," → First name. No subject line and no receiver's address.

22, Gandhi Nagar, Vellore - 632 006 22 January 2026 Dear Dinesh, I hope you had a wonderful Pongal. I spent the holidays with my family at Mamallapuram, and I must tell you all about it. Mamallapuram, also called Mahabalipuram, is a small town on the coast, south of Chennai. It was an important seaport of the Pallava kings, who created most of its monuments in the seventh and eighth centuries. In 1984, UNESCO declared these monuments a World Heritage Site. Our first stop was the Shore Temple. It stands right on the seashore, facing the Bay of Bengal, and we could hear the waves as we walked around it. Next, we saw Arjuna's Penance, a huge carving on two giant rocks, full of gods, people and animals. I loved the lifelike elephants most. Finally, we visited the Five Rathas. These temples are carved out of solid rock and are named after the Pandavas and Draupadi. It was amazing to think that sculptors created all this over a thousand years ago without any modern machines. We ended the day with a long walk on the beach. You must see Mamallapuram. Let's go there together during the summer holidays. I'd love to be your guide! Your loving friend, Suresh

Important vocabulary: monument - a building or structure of historical importance; heritage - valuable things handed down from the past; UNESCO - United Nations Educational, Scientific and Cultural Organization; sculptor - an artist who makes figures by carving stone, wood or metal; carved out of solid rock - cut and shaped from the natural rock itself (rock-cut), not built from separate stones; penance - hard prayer and self-discipline for a spiritual purpose; ratha - chariot (the Five Rathas are also called the Pancha Rathas).

Why this format is correct:

  • A letter to a friend is informal: short address, date, "Dear Dinesh," and no subject line or receiver's address.
  • A descriptive letter follows a clear order: when and where → background → the places seen, one by one → feelings → an invitation.
  • Sequence words ("Our first stop", "Next", "Finally") guide the reader from one place to the next.
  • The facts are few and correct (Pallava kings, seventh and eighth centuries, UNESCO World Heritage Site in 1984); a letter is not a history essay, so the focus stays on what the writer saw and felt.
  • Tenses: the simple past for what happened on the trip ("we saw", "we visited"); the present tense for facts that are still true ("It stands right on the seashore", "These temples are carved ...").

10.9 Letter to a Former Teacher Requesting a Recommendation Letter for Postgraduate Admission Semi-formal

Question: You are S. Anitha of 45, Kamarajar Street, Palayamkottai, Tirunelveli - 627 002. You completed your B.Sc. in Chemistry this year. Write a letter to your former teacher, Dr. R. Sundaram, Associate Professor of Chemistry, Porunai Arts and Science College, Palayamkottai, Tirunelveli - 627 002, requesting a letter of recommendation for admission to an M.Sc. programme. (about 150-200 words)

Situation: Writer: S. Anitha, a recent B.Sc. graduate | Receiver: Dr. R. Sundaram, her former teacher and project guide | Relationship: former student and teacher (respectful but personal) | Purpose: to request a letter of recommendation for M.Sc. admission | Register: Semi-formal

Correct format: Sender's address → Date → Receiver's name and address → Subject → "Dear Dr. Sundaram," → Body (who you are; the request; details and deadline; thanks) → "Yours sincerely," → Full name.

45, Kamarajar Street, Palayamkottai, Tirunelveli - 627 002 15 June 2026 Dr. R. Sundaram, Associate Professor of Chemistry, Porunai Arts and Science College, Palayamkottai, Tirunelveli - 627 002 Subject: Request for a letter of recommendation Dear Dr. Sundaram, I hope you are keeping well. I am S. Anitha, and I completed my B.Sc. in Chemistry in your department this April. You taught me organic chemistry and guided my final-year project on the water quality of the Thamirabarani river. I am applying for admission to the M.Sc. Chemistry programme at Coromandel University, Chennai, for the academic year 2026-2027. The university requires a letter of recommendation from a teacher who knows the applicant's academic work well. As you supervised my project, I would be grateful if you could write one for me. The letter has to be sent directly to the university by email before 30 June 2026. If you agree, I will send you my mark statements, a copy of my project report and the university's recommendation form, so that you have all the details you need. I know how busy you are, and I truly appreciate your time. Thank you for your guidance throughout my degree course. You can reach me at anitha@example.com. Yours sincerely, S. Anitha

Important vocabulary: former - earlier, previous ("my former teacher"); letter of recommendation - a letter in which a teacher or employer supports someone's application; programme - a course of study (British spelling; American "program"); supervise - guide and oversee someone's work; grateful - thankful; mark statement - an official record of a student's marks (also called a mark sheet); appreciate - value; be thankful for.

Why this format is correct:

  • A letter to a former teacher is semi-formal - respectful but personal: it has the receiver's name and address and a subject line, but a warmer tone than an official letter.
  • "Dear Dr. Sundaram," (title + name) pairs with "Yours sincerely," and the full name. If you begin with "Respected Sir,", close with "Yours faithfully," - never mix the pairs.
  • The first paragraph reminds the teacher who the writer is (degree, subject, project), because a teacher may have taught hundreds of students.
  • The request is specific: what (a recommendation letter), for what (M.Sc. admission), how (by email) and by when (30 June 2026).
  • No contractions: semi-formal letters use full forms (I am, I would).

10.10 Letter to the Landlord Requesting Repair of a Leaking Roof and a Broken Water Tap Semi-formal

Question: You are R. Senthil, a tenant at 12/4, Nehru Street, Thillai Nagar, Tiruchirappalli - 620 018. Write a letter to your landlord, Mr. K. Ramasamy, 8, North Chitrai Street, Srirangam, Tiruchirappalli - 620 006, requesting him to repair the leaking roof and the broken water tap in your house. (about 150-200 words)

Situation: Writer: R. Senthil, a tenant | Receiver: Mr. K. Ramasamy, the owner of the house | Relationship: tenant and landlord (known to each other; a practical matter) | Purpose: to request urgent repair of a leaking roof and a broken water tap | Register: Semi-formal

Correct format: Sender's address → Date → Receiver's name and address → Subject → "Dear Mr. Ramasamy," → Body (purpose; problem 1: the roof; problem 2: the tap; the action requested; thanks) → "Yours sincerely," → Full name.

12/4, Nehru Street, Thillai Nagar, Tiruchirappalli - 620 018 4 November 2026 Mr. K. Ramasamy, 8, North Chitrai Street, Srirangam, Tiruchirappalli - 620 006 Subject: Repair of the leaking roof and the broken water tap Dear Mr. Ramasamy, I am writing to request two urgent repairs in your house at the above address, where I have been living as a tenant since June 2024. Since the monsoon rains began last week, water has been leaking through the roof of the bedroom. There is a wide crack in the ceiling near the fan, and the wall below it has become damp. We have to keep buckets on the floor whenever it rains, and I am worried that the water may reach the electrical wiring. In addition, the tap in the kitchen is broken. It does not close fully, and water keeps dripping day and night. As a result, the overhead tank empties quickly and a lot of water is wasted. I would be grateful if you could arrange for a mason and a plumber to attend to these problems within the next few days, before the heavy rains of the coming weeks. I will be at home after 6.00 p.m. on weekdays and all day on Saturdays and Sundays to let them in. Thank you for your understanding. I look forward to an early reply. Yours sincerely, R. Senthil

Important vocabulary: tenant - a person who pays rent to live in a house; landlord - a person who rents out a house (a woman owner: landlady); leak - (of water) come in through a hole or crack; damp - slightly wet; drip - fall in drops; mason / plumber - a worker who builds with bricks, stone and cement / a worker who fits and repairs water pipes and taps; attend to - deal with, take care of.

Why this format is correct:

  • Tenant and landlord know each other, but the matter is practical, so the letter is semi-formal: receiver's name and address, a subject line, "Dear Mr. Ramasamy," and "Yours sincerely," with the full name.
  • The subject line shows the purpose at a glance; it is a short noun phrase with no full stop.
  • Each problem gets its own paragraph with facts (where, since when, what damage) and a reason for urgency (monsoon, wiring, wasted water).
  • The request is polite but clear: who is needed (a mason and a plumber), how soon (within a few days) and when the house is open to them.
  • "I would be grateful if you could ..." is firm yet courteous - far better than "You must repair ...".

10.11 Letter to a Neighbour Politely Requesting That Loud Music Be Lowered during Exam Days Semi-formal

Question: You are K. Priya of 15, Anna Street, Dindigul - 624 001. Your neighbour, Mr. S. Rajan of 17, Anna Street, Dindigul - 624 001, plays loud music in the evenings. Write a letter politely requesting him to lower the volume during your examination days. (about 150-200 words)

Situation: Writer: K. Priya, a B.Com. student | Receiver: Mr. S. Rajan, her neighbour | Relationship: neighbours (known to each other, but not close) | Purpose: to request politely that the music be kept low during her examinations | Register: Semi-formal

Correct format: Sender's address → Date → Receiver's name and address → (Subject - optional, left out here) → "Dear Mr. Rajan," → Body (polite opening; the problem; a limited request; thanks and goodwill) → "Yours sincerely," → Full name.

15, Anna Street, Dindigul - 624 001 10 November 2026 Mr. S. Rajan, 17, Anna Street, Dindigul - 624 001 Dear Mr. Rajan, I hope you and your family are well. I am writing to make a small request, and I hope you will not mind. My B.Com. university examinations begin on 17 November and continue until 4 December. These are my final-year examinations, so they are very important to me. These days, I study every evening until about 11.00 p.m. Recently, the music from your house has been quite loud in the evenings, and I find it difficult to concentrate, especially when I am solving problems in accountancy. I know that your family enjoys music, and I have no wish to spoil your evenings. However, I would be very grateful if you could keep the volume low after 8.00 p.m. until my examinations are over on 4 December. It would be a great help to me. Thank you for your understanding and cooperation. My parents send their regards, and we would be happy to see you at our house whenever you are free. Yours sincerely, K. Priya

Important vocabulary: I hope you will not mind - a polite way to introduce a request; concentrate - give your full attention to something; accountancy - the subject of keeping and checking financial accounts; spoil - make something less enjoyable; volume - the loudness of a sound; cooperation - willingness to help (also spelt co-operation); regards - good wishes ("My parents send their regards").

Why this format is correct:

  • Neighbours who know each other but are not close write semi-formally: receiver's name and address, "Dear Mr. Rajan," and "Yours sincerely," with the full name.
  • The subject line is optional in semi-formal letters. It is left out here to keep the tone neighbourly; "Subject: Request to lower the music volume during my examinations" would also be correct.
  • The request is limited and reasonable - a fixed time (after 8.00 p.m.) and a fixed period (until 4 December) - so it is easy to accept.
  • There are no blame words such as "nuisance" or "noise pollution"; polite forms ("I hope you will not mind", "I would be very grateful if you could ...") keep good relations.
  • The friendly close (thanks, regards and an invitation to visit) protects the relationship for the future.

10.12 Complaint to the Municipal Commissioner - Garbage and Blocked Drains Formal

Question: You are R. Karthik of 15, Bharathi Street, Ganapathy, Coimbatore - 641 006. Garbage has not been collected regularly in your street for three weeks, and the drains are blocked. Write a letter to the Commissioner, Coimbatore City Municipal Corporation, complaining about the problem and requesting immediate action. (about 150-200 words)

Situation: Writer: R. Karthik, a resident of the street | Receiver: The Commissioner, Coimbatore City Municipal Corporation | Relationship: citizen to a public official not known personally | Purpose: to complain and request action | Register: Formal

Correct format: Sender's address → Date → To + receiver's designation and address → Subject → Dear Sir/Madam, → Opening (the complaint in brief) → Main paragraph (facts, effects, earlier complaint) → Closing (action requested) → Yours faithfully, → Name → Encl.

15, Bharathi Street, Ganapathy, Coimbatore - 641 006 5 October 2026 To The Commissioner, Coimbatore City Municipal Corporation, Coimbatore - 641 001 Subject: Complaint regarding irregular garbage collection and blocked drains in Bharathi Street, Ganapathy Dear Sir/Madam, I am a resident of Bharathi Street, Ganapathy, and I wish to bring to your notice the poor sanitary condition of our street. For the past three weeks, garbage has not been collected regularly, and the drains have remained blocked for more than ten days. Earlier, the conservancy workers collected waste from every house daily, but now they come only once or twice a week. The bins at the street corner overflow, and stray dogs scatter the rubbish across the road. As the drains are choked with plastic waste, dirty water stagnates in front of our houses and gives off a foul smell. Mosquitoes are breeding rapidly, and several residents, including children and elderly people, have fallen ill with fever. We reported the matter in writing to the Sanitary Inspector of our ward on 25 September 2026, but no action has been taken so far. I therefore request you to direct the officials concerned to restore daily garbage collection, desilt the drains and spray disinfectant in the area at the earliest. Photographs of the present condition are enclosed. As the situation is a threat to public health, I hope you will treat this complaint as urgent. Yours faithfully, R. Karthik Encl.: 1. Photographs of the blocked drains and overflowing bins 2. Copy of our complaint to the Sanitary Inspector dated 25 September 2026

Important vocabulary: bring to your notice - formally inform an official about something; sanitary - connected with cleanliness and public health; conservancy workers - workers who collect garbage and clean streets and drains (Indian usage); stagnate - (of water) stop flowing and become foul; desilt - remove the mud and waste that has settled in a drain or channel; disinfectant - a chemical that kills germs; officials concerned - the officers responsible for the matter; at the earliest - as soon as possible.

Why this format is correct:

  • The letter is addressed by designation ("The Commissioner"), not by name: the Commissioner is the administrative head of the corporation, and the complaint concerns a public service.
  • The subject line names both problems and the exact street, so the office can send the letter to the right section at once. It is a short noun phrase with no full stop.
  • The body gives checkable facts - how long (three weeks, ten days), the effects (mosquitoes, fever) and the date of the earlier complaint. Facts make a complaint credible; angry words do not.
  • The closing paragraph states exactly what is wanted: daily collection, desilting and disinfection. A complaint that asks for no remedy is incomplete.
  • "Dear Sir/Madam," is used because the officer's gender is not known, and it pairs with "Yours faithfully,". The numbered Encl. line lists the evidence sent with the letter.

10.13 Letter to the District Collector - Bus Service and Safe Drinking Water Formal

Question: You are S. Meena of 4/112, Main Road, Pasumaipatti Village, Omalur Taluk, Salem District - 636 455. Your village has no bus service and no safe drinking water. Write a letter to the District Collector, Salem, requesting a bus service and safe drinking water for the village. (about 150-200 words)

Situation: Writer: S. Meena, on behalf of the villagers | Receiver: The District Collector, Salem | Relationship: citizen to the head of the district administration | Purpose: to request two basic public facilities | Register: Formal

Correct format: Sender's address → Date → To + receiver's designation and address → Subject (both requests) → Dear Sir/Madam, → Opening (who is writing and why) → Main paragraph 1 (bus service) → Main paragraph 2 (drinking water) → Closing (specific requests) → Yours faithfully, → Name → Encl. → Copy to

4/112, Main Road, Pasumaipatti Village, Omalur Taluk, Salem District - 636 455 7 October 2026 To The District Collector, Salem District, Salem - 636 001 Subject: Request for a bus service and safe drinking water for Pasumaipatti Village, Omalur Taluk Dear Sir/Madam, On behalf of the residents of Pasumaipatti Village, I wish to bring to your kind attention two serious problems faced by our village: the absence of a bus service and the lack of safe drinking water. No bus comes to our village. The nearest bus stop is five kilometres away on the main road. Students, daily-wage workers, patients and elderly people have to walk this distance or pay high fares for private vehicles. Students often reach school late, and in an emergency it is very difficult to take a patient to hospital in time. The village also lacks safe drinking water. The overhead tank has not been cleaned for months, and the water supplied through the public taps is muddy and smells bad. The hand pump near the school has been out of order since August. Women walk nearly two kilometres to fetch water, and several children have suffered from diarrhoea. I therefore request you to direct the authorities concerned to introduce a bus service to our village, at least in the morning and evening, and to have the overhead tank cleaned, the water tested and the hand pump repaired, so that we receive a regular supply of safe drinking water. A petition signed by the residents is enclosed. We shall be grateful for your early action. Yours faithfully, S. Meena Encl.: Petition signed by 212 residents of Pasumaipatti Village Copy to: The Block Development Officer, Omalur Panchayat Union, Salem District

Important vocabulary: on behalf of - as the representative of; bring to your kind attention - politely inform; daily-wage workers - labourers who are paid for each day's work; overhead tank - a raised water tank that supplies water by gravity; out of order - not working; diarrhoea - an illness often caused by unsafe water (British spelling); petition - a formal written request, usually signed by many people; Copy to - names the other persons who receive a copy of the letter.

Why this format is correct:

  • The District Collector heads the district administration and can direct the departments concerned, so a single letter to the Collector can raise both problems.
  • The two related requests are handled in two separate main paragraphs, and both are named in the subject line, so the reader sees the structure at a glance.
  • "On behalf of the residents" and the enclosed petition show that this is a community request, not a private one, which gives it more weight.
  • "Copy to" keeps the Block Development Officer, the officer nearer the village, informed. Encl. and Copy to come after the name, in that order (see Section 7).
  • The closing lists concrete actions, and the letter uses the standard pair "Dear Sir/Madam," + "Yours faithfully,".

10.14 Letter to the Inspector of Police - Loss of a Mobile Phone Formal

Question: You are P. Lakshmi of 18, Kamaraj Street, Palayamkottai, Tirunelveli - 627 002. You lost your mobile phone near the Palayamkottai bus stand. Write a letter to the Inspector of Police, Palayamkottai Police Station, reporting the loss and requesting that your complaint be registered and an acknowledgement issued. (about 150-200 words)

Situation: Writer: P. Lakshmi, owner of the phone | Receiver: The Inspector of Police, Palayamkottai Police Station | Relationship: citizen to a police officer not known personally | Purpose: to report a loss and have the complaint registered and acknowledged | Register: Formal

Correct format: Sender's address → Date → To + receiver's designation and police station → Subject → Dear Sir/Madam, → Opening (what was lost, when, where) → Main paragraphs (how it happened, details of the phone, steps already taken) → Closing (register the complaint, issue an acknowledgement) → Yours faithfully, → Name → Encl.

18, Kamaraj Street, Palayamkottai, Tirunelveli - 627 002 14 October 2026 To The Inspector of Police, Palayamkottai Police Station, Tirunelveli - 627 002 Subject: Loss of my mobile phone near Palayamkottai bus stand - request to register complaint Dear Sir/Madam, I wish to report that I lost my mobile phone near the Palayamkottai bus stand on 13 October 2026, between 6.00 p.m. and 6.30 p.m., and I request you to register my complaint. I had boarded a crowded town bus to return home. When I got down at the Kamaraj Street stop, I found that the phone was missing from my handbag. I searched the bus stand and called my number from a friend's phone, but the phone has remained switched off since then. The details of the phone are given below: Description: black smartphone with a blue back cover IMEI numbers: 35XXXXXXXXX4821 and 35XXXXXXXXX4839 Mobile number: 98XXXXXX21 Date of purchase: 10 March 2025 I have already asked my mobile service provider to block the SIM card, and I have changed the passwords of my email and banking apps. I request you to register my complaint and issue me an acknowledgement, which I may need for my records and while dealing with my service provider. Please let me know if the phone is traced. Yours faithfully, P. Lakshmi Encl.: 1. Photocopy of the purchase bill of the phone 2. Photocopy of my Aadhaar card

Important vocabulary: IMEI (International Mobile Equipment Identity) - a unique 15-digit number that identifies a mobile phone; a dual-SIM phone has two; register a complaint - record a complaint officially; acknowledgement - written proof that a letter or complaint has been received; block a SIM card - stop a SIM card from being used; traced - found after a search; service provider - the company that provides a mobile or internet connection; photocopy - a paper copy of a document; lost / stolen - write "stolen" only when you are sure that someone took the item.

Why this format is correct:

  • The opening gives the three key facts at once - what was lost, when and where - together with the request.
  • The identification details are set out one per line so that they are easy to read and copy. In a real complaint, write the full numbers; the IMEI is printed on the phone's box and often on the purchase bill.
  • The letter asks only for what the writer needs - registration of the complaint and an acknowledgement. It does not tell the police how to act, and it does not guess who took the phone.
  • Safety: never write a PIN, password or OTP in any letter, and mask long numbers in model answers, as done here.
  • "Dear Sir/Madam," + "Yours faithfully," is correct because the officer is addressed by designation and is not known personally. The Encl. line lists the proof of ownership and identity.

10.15 Letter to the Editor - Traffic Congestion near Schools Formal

Question: Write a letter to the Editor of a newspaper published from Tiruchirappalli, drawing the attention of the authorities to the traffic congestion near schools in the city during school hours. (about 150-200 words)

Situation: Writer: a resident of Tiruchirappalli (no name or address is given, so the exam identity rule applies) | Receiver: The Editor of a newspaper | Relationship: reader to editor; the real audience is the public and the authorities | Purpose: to have the letter published and prompt the authorities to act | Register: Formal

Correct format: "Examination Hall" (in place of the sender's address) → Date → To + The Editor, name of the newspaper, city → Subject → Sir, → Opening (the issue + request to publish) → Main paragraph (the problem and its effects) → Closing (suggestions + hope for action) → Yours faithfully, → ABC

Examination Hall 9 October 2026 To The Editor, The Hindu, Tiruchirappalli Subject: Traffic congestion near schools in Tiruchirappalli Sir, Through the columns of your widely read newspaper, I wish to draw the attention of the authorities concerned to the heavy traffic congestion near schools in Tiruchirappalli during school hours. I request you to publish this letter. Every morning and evening, the roads near many schools in the city are choked with school vans, autorickshaws, two-wheelers and cars. Parents park their vehicles on both sides of narrow roads, and vans stop in the middle of the road to pick up and drop children. Buses and even ambulances are held up for long periods. Worst of all, small children have to walk between moving vehicles. Near many schools there are no traffic police personnel, zebra crossings or warning signboards. I suggest that the traffic police deploy personnel near schools during peak hours, that separate pick-up and drop points be marked, and that parking be prohibited close to school gates. Schools can also stagger their closing times and encourage students to use school buses. I hope the authorities concerned will act before a serious accident takes place. Yours faithfully, ABC

Important vocabulary: through the columns of - by means of the pages of a newspaper; widely read - read by a large number of people; draw the attention of - make someone notice a problem; congestion - overcrowding of vehicles that slows down traffic; peak hours - the busiest hours of the day; deploy - send people to a place for a particular task; stagger (timings) - arrange times so that they do not all fall together; zebra crossing - a striped road crossing for pedestrians.

Why this format is correct:

  • The question gives no name or address, so the exam identity rule applies: "Examination Hall" as the address and "ABC" as the signature. Never write your real name in an exam answer.
  • A letter to the Editor has two audiences: the Editor, who is asked to publish it (first paragraph), and the public and the authorities, who are the real target (the rest of the letter).
  • The newspaper is given by name and city only, never a street address. When the question does not name a newspaper, choose a well-known one.
  • The tone is public-spirited: the problem, its effects and practical suggestions, with no blame on any school or person.
  • "Sir," is the traditional salutation for an Editor ("Dear Sir/Madam," is equally correct); both pair with "Yours faithfully,". "Yours truly," is also seen in letters to the Editor, but model letters use "Yours faithfully,".

10.16 Leave Letter - Sick Leave with Medical Certificate Formal

Question: You are K. Arun of 7, Nehru Street, Erode - 638 001, a Clerk at the Erode Main Branch of Southern Commercial Bank. You have viral fever, and your doctor has advised five days' rest. Write a letter to your Branch Manager requesting sick leave and enclosing a medical certificate. (about 150-200 words)

Situation: Writer: K. Arun, Clerk | Receiver: The Branch Manager, his immediate superior | Relationship: employee to superior | Purpose: to request sick leave for a fixed period | Register: Formal

Correct format: Sender's address (home) → Date → To + The Branch Manager, bank, branch, address → Subject (with the leave dates) → Respected Sir, → Opening (leave requested, dates, reason) → Main paragraph (illness, doctor's advice, arrangement for pending work) → Closing (rejoining, fitness certificate, contact, request to sanction) → Yours faithfully, → Name + designation → Encl.

7, Nehru Street, Erode - 638 001 12 October 2026 To The Branch Manager, Southern Commercial Bank, Erode Main Branch, Erode - 638 001 Subject: Request for sick leave from 12 October to 16 October 2026 Respected Sir, I request you to grant me sick leave for five days, from 12 October to 16 October 2026 (both days inclusive), as I am suffering from viral fever and am unable to attend the branch. I developed a high fever and severe body pain on the night of 10 October 2026. The next morning I consulted Dr. P. Ramesh, M.B.B.S., who diagnosed viral fever, prescribed medicines and advised me to take complete rest for five days. His medical certificate is enclosed. I have informed my colleague, Ms. R. Kavya, about the pending work at my counter, including two customer requests that must be completed this week, so that customers are not inconvenienced in my absence. All the registers under my charge are up to date. I shall rejoin duty immediately after the leave period and submit a fitness certificate at that time. If any information about my work is needed during my leave, I can be contacted on my mobile phone. I request you to sanction the leave. I regret the inconvenience caused. Yours faithfully, K. Arun Clerk, Employee No. 4521 Encl.: Medical certificate issued by Dr. P. Ramesh, M.B.B.S.

Important vocabulary: sick leave - leave granted to an employee who is ill (also called medical leave); both days inclusive - counting both the first and the last day; diagnose - identify an illness after examination; medical certificate - a doctor's written statement of an illness and the rest advised; fitness certificate - a doctor's statement that a person is fit to resume work; rejoin duty - return to work after leave; up to date - complete, with all the latest entries made; sanction - officially approve (the noun can also mean a penalty, as in "economic sanctions").

Why this format is correct:

  • The sender's address is the home address because the employee is writing from home; his designation and employee number go below the name so that the office can identify him.
  • The exact dates appear in the subject line and again in the opening with "both days inclusive", so there is no doubt about the number of days requested.
  • The body gives the reason briefly and shows responsibility: the doctor's advice, the certificate and the arrangement made for pending work.
  • "Respected Sir," is accepted Indian usage for a superior ("Respected Madam," for a woman manager; "Dear Sir," is also correct), and it pairs with "Yours faithfully,".
  • The Encl. line records the medical certificate - the document that supports the request.

10.17 Job Application - Office Assistant in a Cooperative Bank Formal

Question: You are G. Vignesh of 26, Anna Street, S.S. Colony, Madurai - 625 016. You hold a B.Com. degree and a certificate in computer applications, and you have one year's experience as an accounts assistant. Thendral Cooperative Urban Bank Ltd., Madurai, has advertised the post of Office Assistant in The Hindu dated 5 October 2026. Write a letter of application for the post. (about 150-200 words)

Situation: Writer: G. Vignesh, a job seeker | Receiver: The General Manager, Thendral Cooperative Urban Bank Ltd. (a fictional bank) | Relationship: applicant to a prospective employer | Purpose: to apply for an advertised post and obtain an interview | Register: Formal

Correct format: Sender's address → Date → To + receiver's designation and address → Subject (post applied for) → Reference (the advertisement) → Dear Sir/Madam, → Opening (post + where it was advertised) → Main paragraphs (qualifications, experience) → Closing (enclosures, interview, thanks) → Yours faithfully, → Name + mobile + email → Encl. (numbered)

26, Anna Street, S.S. Colony, Madurai - 625 016 10 October 2026 To The General Manager, Thendral Cooperative Urban Bank Ltd., Head Office, Madurai - 625 001 Subject: Application for the post of Office Assistant Reference: Your advertisement in The Hindu dated 5 October 2026 Dear Sir/Madam, In response to your advertisement in The Hindu dated 5 October 2026, I wish to apply for the post of Office Assistant in your bank. I believe that my qualifications and experience meet your requirements. I completed my B.Com. degree in first class in 2025. I have also completed a six-month Certificate Course in Computer Applications and can work confidently with word processing, spreadsheets and accounting software. I can type accurately in both English and Tamil. For the past year I have been working as an Accounts Assistant at Sri Murugan Stationers, Madurai. My duties include maintaining the cash book, preparing vouchers, depositing cash in the bank and attending to customers. This work has taught me accuracy, punctuality and courtesy, which are essential in a bank. My resume and copies of my certificates are enclosed. I shall be glad to attend an interview at your convenience. If selected, I shall perform my duties sincerely and to the best of my ability. Thank you for considering my application. Yours faithfully, G. Vignesh Mobile: 98XXXXXX45 Email: vignesh@example.com Encl.: 1. Resume 2. Copy of B.Com. degree certificate 3. Copy of the certificate in computer applications 4. Experience certificate from Sri Murugan Stationers 5. Two passport-size photographs

Important vocabulary: in response to - as a reply to; post - a job or position in an organisation; resume - a short summary of a person's education, skills and experience (also called bio-data or curriculum vitae, CV); vouchers - documents that support payment or receipt entries in accounts; experience certificate - a letter from an employer confirming the period and nature of a person's work; at your convenience - at a time that suits you; prospective employer - an organisation that may employ you; covering letter - a letter sent with a resume or other documents, explaining what is sent and why (also called a cover letter).

Why this format is correct:

  • The subject line names the post, and the Reference line gives the source and date of the advertisement - an organisation may be filling several posts at one time.
  • Qualifications and experience are stated briefly, one paragraph each; the full details are left to the enclosed resume. Do not copy the whole resume into the letter.
  • Every enclosure is numbered, so the recruiter can check that nothing is missing. Send copies of certificates, not the originals.
  • The mobile number and email address are written below the name, so the bank can contact the candidate quickly.
  • The tone is confident and polite, with no pleading ("I am badly in need of this job") and no exaggeration. The pair used is "Dear Sir/Madam," + "Yours faithfully,".

10.18 Permission Letter - Blood Donation Camp Formal

Question: You are V. Divya, Secretary, Udhayam Youth Welfare Association, 14, Gandhiji Road, Thanjavur - 613 001. Your association plans to conduct a blood donation camp on Sunday, 25 October 2026. Write a letter to the Headmaster, Bharathi Vidyalaya Higher Secondary School, Medical College Road, Thanjavur - 613 004, seeking permission to use the school ground for the camp. (about 150-200 words)

Situation: Writer: V. Divya, an office-bearer writing for her association | Receiver: The Headmaster of a school | Relationship: an outside organisation to the head of an institution | Purpose: to obtain permission to use the school ground for one day | Register: Formal

Correct format: Sender's address (the association) → Date → To + receiver's designation and address → Subject (purpose, place, date) → Dear Sir, → Opening (permission sought: what, where, when) → Main paragraph (organisers, medical team, space needed) → Closing (assurances, enclosure, request for an early reply) → Yours faithfully, → Name + designation → Encl.

Udhayam Youth Welfare Association, 14, Gandhiji Road, Thanjavur - 613 001 5 October 2026 To The Headmaster, Bharathi Vidyalaya Higher Secondary School, Medical College Road, Thanjavur - 613 004 Subject: Request for permission to conduct a blood donation camp on the school ground on Sunday, 25 October 2026 Dear Sir, On behalf of the Udhayam Youth Welfare Association, I request your permission to conduct a voluntary blood donation camp on your school ground on Sunday, 25 October 2026, from 8.00 a.m. to 2.00 p.m. Our association has been engaged in social service in Thanjavur for the past six years. The camp will be conducted with the Blood Bank of Thanjavur Medical College Hospital, whose team of doctors and nurses will examine the donors and collect the blood. We expect about 150 donors. Your ground is spacious, easy to reach and close to the hospital, which makes it the most suitable venue. We shall need the ground for a shamiana and registration counters, and two ground-floor classrooms for the medical team. As the camp will be held on a Sunday, school work will not be disturbed. We assure you that we shall bear all the expenses, arrange drinking water and refreshments for the donors, and ensure that no school property is damaged. Our volunteers will manage the crowd and parking, and the ground will be cleaned and handed back by 4.00 p.m. A copy of the consent letter from the blood bank is enclosed. We request you to grant permission at the earliest so that we can inform the donors in time. Yours faithfully, V. Divya Secretary, Udhayam Youth Welfare Association Encl.: Copy of the consent letter from the Blood Bank, Thanjavur Medical College Hospital

Important vocabulary: permission - official consent to do something; voluntary - done willingly and without payment; venue - the place where an event is held; shamiana - a large tent or canopy put up for functions (Indian English; a similar temporary structure is called a pandal); refreshments - light food and drinks; consent letter - a letter agreeing to something or to take part in it; bear the expenses - pay the costs; office-bearer - an elected official of an association, such as the President, Secretary or Treasurer.

Why this format is correct:

  • A permission letter must answer what (a blood donation camp), where (the ground and two classrooms), when (date and exact hours), who (the association and the medical team) and why this venue. The subject line carries the purpose, place and date.
  • Assurances about expenses, cleanliness, safety of property and a fixed time for handing back remove the Headmaster's likely objections; mentioning that classes will not be disturbed helps too.
  • The letter is written for an organisation, so the association's address is at the top and the writer's designation appears below her name.
  • The enclosed consent letter from the blood bank shows that the camp is genuine and medically supervised.
  • "Dear Sir," suits the designation "Headmaster" ("Dear Madam," for a Headmistress) and pairs with "Yours faithfully,".

10.19 Inquiry Letter - Details of a Diploma Course Formal

Question: You are N. Priya of 3, Thiruvalluvar Street, Katpadi, Vellore - 632 007. You have completed Class 12. Write a letter to the Registrar, Thamarai College of Arts and Science, Vellore - 632 001, asking for details of its Diploma in Computer Applications - eligibility, fees, duration and admission dates. (about 150-200 words)

Situation: Writer: N. Priya, a prospective student | Receiver: The Registrar of a college (fictional) | Relationship: member of the public to an institution | Purpose: to obtain information about a course | Register: Formal

Correct format: Sender's address → Date → To + receiver's designation and address → Subject → Dear Sir/Madam, → Opening (who you are, which course) → Main paragraph (numbered questions) → Closing (prospectus, how to reply, thanks) → Yours faithfully, → Name → Encl.

3, Thiruvalluvar Street, Katpadi, Vellore - 632 007 8 October 2026 To The Registrar, Thamarai College of Arts and Science, Vellore - 632 001 Subject: Inquiry about the Diploma in Computer Applications course Dear Sir/Madam, I have completed my Higher Secondary (Class 12) course in the Commerce group, and I wish to join the Diploma in Computer Applications offered by your college. I request you to send me full details of the course. I would be grateful if you could let me know the following: 1. the eligibility conditions for admission, including the minimum marks required, if any; 2. the duration of the course and the class timings; 3. the total fee, the mode of payment and whether the fee can be paid in instalments; 4. the dates for the issue and submission of application forms, and the date on which classes will begin. I would also like to know whether the college offers placement support to students who complete the course. Kindly send me a copy of the prospectus and an application form. A self-addressed stamped envelope is enclosed for your reply; you may also reply by email to priya@example.com. Thank you for your time. Yours faithfully, N. Priya Encl.: Self-addressed stamped envelope

Important vocabulary: inquiry / enquiry - a request for information; both spellings are accepted (British tradition: enquiry = asking for information, inquiry = formal investigation); eligibility - the conditions a candidate must satisfy to be admitted; duration - the length of time a course lasts; instalment - one of the parts in which a sum of money is paid over a period (British spelling; American "installment"); mode of payment - the way a payment is made, such as cash, demand draft or online transfer; prospectus - a booklet giving details of an institution's courses, fees and rules; self-addressed stamped envelope - an envelope carrying your own address and a postage stamp, sent so that the receiver can reply easily; placement - help given to students to find jobs.

Why this format is correct:

  • An inquiry letter asks; it does not complain or demand. The opening says who is writing and what information is needed.
  • The questions are numbered, which makes the reply easy and ensures that none of the four points (eligibility, fees, duration, admission dates) is missed.
  • The closing tells the college how to reply: the enclosed self-addressed stamped envelope (traditional) and an email address (modern).
  • The letter is addressed to "The Registrar" by designation. If an institution has no Registrar, address the Principal or the Admission Officer.
  • Polite forms such as "I would be grateful if you could ..." and "Kindly send ..." suit a request to an institution, with the pair "Dear Sir/Madam," + "Yours faithfully,".

10.20 Letter to a Government Authority - Power Cuts and Low Voltage Formal

Question: You are A. Anitha of 27, Lakshmi Street, Velachery, Chennai - 600 042. For the past month your street has been facing frequent power cuts and low voltage. Write a letter to the Assistant Engineer of the local electricity distribution office, requesting immediate action. (about 150-200 words)

Situation: Writer: A. Anitha, an electricity consumer | Receiver: The Assistant Engineer, Electricity Distribution Section Office | Relationship: consumer to the officer in charge of local supply | Purpose: to complain and request a lasting solution | Register: Formal

Correct format: Sender's address → Date → To + receiver's designation and office address → Subject → Reference (earlier complaints) → Dear Sir/Madam, → Opening (the problem + service connection number) → Main paragraph (frequency, timing, effects, likely cause) → Closing (specific action requested) → Yours faithfully, → Name

27, Lakshmi Street, Velachery, Chennai - 600 042 15 October 2026 To The Assistant Engineer, Electricity Distribution Section Office, Velachery, Chennai - 600 042 Subject: Frequent power cuts and low voltage in Lakshmi Street, Velachery Reference: Complaint Nos. 2716 and 2983 registered on 18 and 25 September 2026 Dear Sir/Madam, I wish to bring to your notice the frequent power cuts and low voltage that the residents of Lakshmi Street, Velachery, have been facing for the past month. My service connection number is XXXXXXX452. Power fails four or five times a day, and each cut lasts from half an hour to two hours, often late at night. No advance notice is given. In the evenings, between 6.00 p.m. and 10.00 p.m., the voltage drops so low that lights become dim, fans run slowly and water pumps do not start. Sudden voltage fluctuations have damaged the refrigerators and television sets of some households. Students preparing for examinations and elderly and sick residents are the worst affected. The transformer serving our street appears to be overloaded, as many new houses and flats have been built here recently. The complaints mentioned above brought only temporary relief. I therefore request you to inspect the transformer and the overhead lines in our street, take steps to increase the supply capacity, and give advance notice of any planned shutdown for maintenance. I hope you will take early action to restore a regular power supply at the proper voltage. Yours faithfully, A. Anitha

Important vocabulary: power cut - an interruption in the electricity supply (also called a power outage); load shedding - planned power cuts made when the demand for electricity is higher than the supply; low voltage - supply below the normal level, so that lights are dim and appliances do not work properly; voltage fluctuation - frequent rise and fall in voltage; transformer - equipment that changes the voltage of electricity; a distribution transformer lowers it for supply to homes; overloaded - carrying more load than its capacity; shutdown - a planned stopping of supply for repair or maintenance work; service connection number - the number that identifies a consumer's electricity connection (also called consumer number).

Why this format is correct:

  • The Assistant Engineer is in charge of the local distribution office, so a street-level problem goes to this officer first; if it is not solved, the writer can then approach higher officers.
  • The Reference line quotes the earlier complaint numbers. This proves that the problem is continuing and lets the office trace its own records.
  • The service connection number identifies the writer's supply, and the facts (how often, how long, at what time, what damage) help the engineer find the cause.
  • The request is specific and practical - inspection, more capacity, advance notice of shutdowns - not a vague "please do something".
  • The register stays formal, with no exclamation marks or angry words, even though the writer is inconvenienced; the pair is "Dear Sir/Madam," + "Yours faithfully,".

10.21 Transfer Request through Proper Channel - Medical Grounds of a Parent Formal

Question: You are M. Suresh, Assistant, Office of the Deputy Director of Health Services, Ramanathapuram - 623 501. Your widowed mother, aged 78, lives alone in Madurai and needs constant care. Write a letter to the Director of Public Health and Preventive Medicine, Chennai - 600 006, through proper channel, requesting a transfer to Madurai on medical grounds. (about 150-200 words)

Situation: Writer: M. Suresh, a government employee | Receiver: the Head of Department (the Director), through the immediate superior (the Deputy Director) | Relationship: employee to a senior officer | Purpose: to request a transfer on the medical grounds of an aged parent | Register: Formal

Correct format: Sender's address (office) → Date → To + Head of Department's designation and address → Through: immediate superior → Subject → Respected Sir, → Opening (present post + request) → Main paragraph (the parent's condition, why the transfer is needed) → Closing (place requested + polite request) → Yours faithfully, → Name + designation → Encl.

Office of the Deputy Director of Health Services, Ramanathapuram - 623 501 22 October 2026 To The Director of Public Health and Preventive Medicine, Chennai - 600 006 Through: The Deputy Director of Health Services, Ramanathapuram Subject: Request for transfer to Madurai on medical grounds of my aged mother Respected Sir, I have been working as an Assistant in the Office of the Deputy Director of Health Services, Ramanathapuram, since June 2019. I request you to transfer me to Madurai on the medical grounds of my aged mother. My mother, Tmt. M. Kamala, is a 78-year-old widow living alone in Madurai. She suffers from heart disease and diabetes, and her doctor has advised regular check-ups and constant care. I am her only son. At present I travel more than 100 kilometres every weekend to look after her, but during the week there is no one to help her in an emergency. Last month she fell at home and had to be taken to hospital by neighbours. Her medical certificate is enclosed. I therefore request you to transfer me to the Office of the Deputy Director of Health Services, Madurai, or to any other office in Madurai, in my present post, whenever a vacancy arises. I shall be grateful for your kind consideration of my request. Yours faithfully, M. Suresh Assistant Encl.: Medical certificate of my mother, Tmt. M. Kamala

Important vocabulary: through proper channel - sent through one's immediate superior instead of directly to the higher authority; Head of Department (HoD) - the officer at the head of a government department; immediate superior - the officer directly above an employee; medical grounds - reasons based on illness; compassionate grounds - reasons based on personal or family hardship; forward - send a paper on to the next authority, often with remarks; whenever a vacancy arises - as soon as a post falls vacant; Tmt. (Thirumathi) - Tamil Nadu Government honorific for a married woman (Mrs.).

Why this format is correct:

  • "Through proper channel" means that an employee's application to a higher authority is sent through the immediate superior. The "Through: ..." line below the addressee shows this route (see Section 6); the Deputy Director forwards the letter with his or her remarks.
  • The office address is at the top because this is an official application that passes through that office; the writer's designation appears below his name.
  • The grounds are specific and supported by evidence: the mother's age and illnesses, the distance, the recent fall and an enclosed medical certificate. Vague reasons such as "family problems" are weak.
  • The request is precise - a named office in Madurai, the present post, "whenever a vacancy arises" - so it is easy to act on.
  • "Respected Sir," is accepted Indian usage for a superior ("Respected Madam," for a woman officer) and pairs with "Yours faithfully,". The mother is referred to with the Tamil Nadu honorific "Tmt.".

10.22 Business Inquiry: Prices and Trade Terms Formal

Question: You are S. Senthil, Proprietor of Sri Murugan Stationers, 45, West Masi Street, Madurai - 625 001. Write a letter to the Sales Manager, Kaveri Paper Mills, 8, Industrial Estate, Sivakasi - 626 123, asking for the latest price list, trade discount, delivery terms and payment terms for school notebooks.

Situation: Writer: S. Senthil, Proprietor, Sri Murugan Stationers (retailer) | Receiver: The Sales Manager, Kaveri Paper Mills (manufacturer and wholesaler) | Relationship: prospective buyer to seller; no earlier dealings | Purpose: to collect prices and terms before deciding to order | Register: Formal

Correct format: Letterhead → Ref. No. → Date → To + inside address → Subject → Salutation → Body (who we are, numbered questions, prospect of business, early reply) → Complimentary close → For firm, name, designation

SRI MURUGAN STATIONERS 45, WEST MASI STREET, MADURAI - 625 001 PHONE: 0452 2XXXXXX | EMAIL: murugan@example.com GSTIN: 33XXXXXXXXXX1Z5 Ref. No.: SMS/PUR/2026-27/118 5 October 2026 To The Sales Manager, Kaveri Paper Mills, 8, Industrial Estate, Sivakasi - 626 123 Subject: Inquiry about prices and trade terms for school notebooks Dear Sir/Madam, We are a retail stationery shop in Madurai and have been supplying notebooks and stationery to students of more than twenty schools and colleges in the city for the past twelve years. We saw your notebooks at a trade fair in Madurai and were impressed by their quality. We now wish to stock them in our shop. Please send us your latest price list and let us know the following: 1. The trade discount you allow to retailers, and any extra discount on large orders. 2. Your delivery terms, the time needed to deliver to Madurai, and who bears the freight. 3. Your payment terms, the credit period allowed, and any cash discount for early payment. 4. The minimum quantity you accept for each item. We are mainly interested in long-size notebooks (200 pages), king-size notebooks (160 pages) and four-line notebooks for primary classes. If your prices and terms are suitable, we expect to place a first order worth about Rs. 1,00,000 and to order regularly every term. We would be grateful for an early reply, as we plan to replenish our stock this month. Yours faithfully, For SRI MURUGAN STATIONERS S. Senthil Proprietor

Important vocabulary: inquiry / enquiry - a letter asking for information (both spellings are accepted); price list - the seller's list of current prices for each item; trade discount - a reduction in the list price allowed to traders who buy goods for resale; cash discount - a reduction allowed for payment within a fixed short time; credit period - the time allowed to the buyer to pay after delivery; freight - the charge for carrying goods; GSTIN - Goods and Services Tax Identification Number, a 15-character registration number ("33" is the code for Tamil Nadu).

Why this format is correct:

  • It is written on the firm's letterhead (name in capitals, address, phone, email, GSTIN) with a Ref. No. that the seller can quote in the reply.
  • The Sales Manager's name is not known, so "Dear Sir/Madam," pairs with "Yours faithfully,". The Subject comes before the salutation (house style; the traditional British layout puts it after the salutation - accepted variant).
  • The body follows the inquiry pattern: who we are → what we want (numbered questions) → prospect of regular orders → request for an early reply. Numbered questions make the reply easy and complete.
  • "For SRI MURUGAN STATIONERS" above the name shows that the proprietor signs on behalf of the firm.
  • An inquiry does not bind the writer to buy anything; the seller's reply to it is a quotation (see 10.23).

10.23 Quotation in Reply to an Inquiry Formal

Question: As R. Meena, Sales Manager, Kaveri Paper Mills, 8, Industrial Estate, Sivakasi - 626 123, reply to the inquiry of Mr. S. Senthil, Proprietor, Sri Murugan Stationers, 45, West Masi Street, Madurai - 625 001 (see 10.22). Quote your prices, trade discount, delivery terms and payment terms, and state how long the offer is valid.

Situation: Writer: R. Meena, Sales Manager, Kaveri Paper Mills (seller) | Receiver: Mr. S. Senthil, Proprietor, Sri Murugan Stationers (prospective buyer) | Relationship: seller replying to a new customer's inquiry | Purpose: to make a firm offer that the buyer can accept by placing an order | Register: Formal

Correct format: Letterhead → Ref. No. → Date → To + inside address (named person) → Subject → Reference → Dear Mr. Senthil, → Body (thanks, prices, terms, validity, invitation to order) → Yours sincerely, → For firm, name, designation → Encl.

KAVERI PAPER MILLS 8, INDUSTRIAL ESTATE, SIVAKASI - 626 123 PHONE: 04562 2XXXXX | EMAIL: sales.kaveri@example.com GSTIN: 33XXXXXXXXXX1Z5 Ref. No.: KPM/SAL/2026-27/204 8 October 2026 To Mr. S. Senthil, Proprietor, Sri Murugan Stationers, 45, West Masi Street, Madurai - 625 001 Subject: Quotation for school notebooks Reference: Your letter Ref. No. SMS/PUR/2026-27/118 dated 5 October 2026 Dear Mr. Senthil, Thank you for your letter and for your interest in our notebooks. We are pleased to quote our prices, which are inclusive of GST: 1. Long-size notebook, 200 pages, ruled (Code LN-200): Rs. 540 per dozen 2. King-size notebook, 160 pages, ruled (Code KS-160): Rs. 420 per dozen 3. Four-line notebook, 80 pages (Code FL-80): Rs. 216 per dozen Our terms are as follows: Trade discount: 5% on list prices; 10% on orders of Rs. 50,000 and above. Delivery: within 7 days of receipt of the order, at your shop in Madurai. Freight and transit insurance will be borne by us. Payment: within 30 days of the date of invoice, by NEFT or cheque. A cash discount of 2% is allowed if the invoice is paid within 10 days. Minimum order: 10 dozen for each item. The notebooks are packed 5 dozen to a waterproof carton. This quotation is valid until 31 October 2026. Orders received after that date will be accepted at the prices ruling on the date of the order. We enclose our price list and catalogue.
