Office Memorandum (O.M.)O.M. = Other Ministry, third person, no salutationNone → NoneBetween Ministries / Departments (also with attached offices and employees); "The undersigned is directed to ..."; signed with name and designation
Office OrderOffice Order stays inside the OfficeNone → NoneInternal administrative instructions: distribution of work, grant of leave, postings within the office
OrderOrder = M & M: Money and MisconductNone → NoneFinancial sanctions, disciplinary matters, etc.
NotificationNotification = in the GazetteNone → NonePublication of rules, orders, appointments, promotions, etc. in the Official Gazette
ResolutionResolution Resolves policy (also in the Gazette)None → NonePublic announcement of important policy decisions or the appointment of committees or commissions; published in the Gazette
CircularA Circular goes round in a circleNone → NoneThe same information sent to many offices or persons
EndorsementENDorsement = at the ENDNot a letterGovernment: a note at the foot of a communication forwarding copies ("Copy forwarded to ... for information / necessary action") or returning a paper to the sender. Banking: a signature on the back of a cheque or other negotiable instrument to transfer it.

MEMORY TRICK: "I am directed" (first person) = Official letter. "The undersigned is directed" (third person) = O.M.

MEMORY TRICK: Both go to the Gazette, but a Notification Names people and rules (appointments, promotions, rules), while a Resolution Resolves policy (policy decisions, committees, commissions).

MEMORY TRICK: Tamil Nadu: G.O. (Government Order) comes from the Government - the Secretariat departments. Proceedings come from Heads of Departments and District officers: "Proceedings of the District Collector, ... Present: Thiru ..., Rc. No. ..., Dated ..., Sub: ..., Ref: ..., ORDER: ...".

MEMORY TRICK: Honorifics: Shri - Smt. - Kumari (Central Government) = Thiru - Thirumathi (Tmt.) - Selvi (Tamil Nadu Government) = Mr. - Mrs. - Miss. "Hon'ble" for Ministers and judges; "M/s." (Messrs) for firms, e.g. M/s. Kaveri Traders.

MUST REMEMBER: Only two government forms carry a salutation and a close: the official letter (Sir → Yours faithfully) and the D.O. letter (Dear Shri Kumar → Yours sincerely). "Through proper channel" = an employee's application to a higher authority goes through the immediate superior, with a "Through: ..." line above or below the addressee.

13.9 One-page cheat card

SituationSalutationCloseKey opening phrase
Leave or permission from the Principal / Head of OfficeRespected Sir, / Respected Madam,Yours faithfully, (students: Yours obediently,)I request you to grant me leave for two days, from ... to ..., as ...
Complaint to a civic authority (Commissioner, Collector, Police)Dear Sir/Madam,Yours faithfully,I wish to bring to your notice that ...
Letter to the EditorSir, / Dear Sir/Madam,Yours faithfully,Through the columns of your newspaper, I wish to draw the attention of the authorities concerned to ...
Job applicationDear Sir/Madam,Yours faithfully,In response to your advertisement in The Hindu dated ..., I wish to apply for the post of ...
Inquiry or order to a firmDear Sir/Madam,Yours faithfully,Please send us your latest price list and terms for ... / Please supply the following items as per your quotation No. ... dated ...
Complaint or claim to a sellerDear Sir/Madam,Yours faithfully,We regret to inform you that the goods received against our Order No. ... dated ... are damaged.
Request to your bank (cheque book, stop-payment, change of address)Dear Sir/Madam, (to the Branch Manager)Yours faithfully,I hold a savings account (No. XXXXXXXX4521) in your branch and request you to ...
Government official letterSir, / Madam,Yours faithfully,I am directed to ...
Named person, semi-formal (teacher, neighbour, landlord)Dear Mr. Raman,Yours sincerely,I am writing to thank you for ...
Friend or familyDear Priya, / Dear Appa,Your loving friend, / Your loving son,I was very happy to receive your letter. / I hope you are all keeping well.

D.O. and O.M. patterns: see 13.8. No name or address in the question? Write "Examination Hall" and sign as "ABC" (11.9).

14 TNPSC and Competitive Exam Angle

This section shows how letter writing is examined in TNPSC, SSC, banking and UPSC-type exams: the objective (MCQ) angle, the descriptive angle, the areas asked most often, ten question patterns with worked examples, and a step-by-step strategy for writing a full letter in the exam hall. The detailed content is in the earlier sections; the "Revise in" column tells you where to go.

14.1 How Letter Writing Is Tested

Letter writing is tested in two ways. Objective (MCQ) questions ask you to recognise the correct form, term, phrase or order. Descriptive questions ask you to write a complete letter in the correct format, tone and length. The same rules serve both: if you know the format well, you can answer the MCQ and also write the letter.

Exam typeObjective (MCQ) angleDescriptive angle
TNPSC (Group 1, 2, 2A, 4)Where the paper has an English or language component: correct salutation and close, parts of a letter and their order, types of letters, formal phrases, vocabulary and error spotting. Terms such as G.O., O.M., D.O. letter and Notification also help in administration-related questions.Main (descriptive) papers may include official letter writing, precis or similar writing tasks, in English or Tamil as the notification specifies. Format, tone and relevance all matter.
SSC examsEnglish language questions: vocabulary, spelling, idioms, error spotting, sentence improvement and cloze tests. Letter words and phrases ("stationery", "with reference to", "look forward to hearing") appear inside these question forms.Some SSC exams have included a descriptive paper (essay, letter or application) in some years. Check whether the current pattern has one.
Banking and insurance examsEnglish language: error detection, fill in the blanks, cloze test and sentence rearrangement; banking and business vocabulary (cheque, draft, credit note, Letter of Credit, stop-payment).Officer-level main exams may include a descriptive test with a letter (complaint, request or formal letter to a bank, company or authority) and an essay.
UPSC and other central examsEnglish sections of objective exams: vocabulary, usage, error spotting and sentence arrangement.Descriptive English papers test writing skills such as precis, short essays and usage; the formal tone and clear structure learnt in letter writing apply directly. Official-correspondence terms help in governance topics.
Departmental and promotion testsOffice procedure terms: Office Memorandum, Office Order, Notification, Endorsement, Proceedings, "through proper channel".Noting and drafting: an official letter, D.O. letter, O.M., circular or office order written in the correct form.

MUST REMEMBER: Always check the latest official notification and syllabus.

14.2 Most Commonly Tested Areas

These areas appear again and again across exams. The example stems are PYQ-style illustrations, not actual previous-year questions.

14.3 Important Definitions

TermOne-line definition
Formal letterA letter to an official, an organisation or an unknown person for an official or business purpose, in a fixed format and formal language.
Semi-formal letterA letter to a known person (teacher, neighbour, landlord) on a personal or partly official matter; polite but personal: "Dear Mr. Raman," → "Yours sincerely,".
Informal letterA personal letter to family or friends in a friendly tone; no subject line and no receiver's address.
SalutationThe greeting that begins the letter, e.g. "Dear Sir/Madam,"; always followed by a comma.
Complimentary closeThe polite closing phrase written before the signature, e.g. "Yours faithfully,".
Subject lineA short noun phrase that states the purpose; begins "Subject:", comes before the salutation and has no full stop.
Enclosure (Encl.)A document sent with the letter, listed below the signature, e.g. "Encl.: 1. Copy of the bill".
Official letterA letter from a Government office to other governments, bodies, officials or the public; begins "I am directed to ..."; signed with designation.
Demi-official (D.O.) letterAn officer-to-officer letter in a personal, friendly tone and the first person, used to draw personal attention, expedite a matter, or exchange views or information.
Office Memorandum (O.M.)A communication between Ministries/Departments (also with attached offices and employees), written in the third person, with no salutation and no close.
NotificationPublication of rules, orders, appointments, promotions, etc. in the Official Gazette.
CircularThe same information sent to many offices or persons.
QuotationThe seller's statement of prices, terms and conditions, sent in reply to an inquiry and valid for a stated period.
Adjustment letterThe seller's reply that settles a complaint or claim: full grant, partial grant or polite refusal with reasons.
Letter of Credit (L/C)A bank's written undertaking, issued at the buyer's request, to pay the seller when specified documents are presented; used mainly in foreign trade.

14.4 Frequently Confused Terms

One line per pair for quick recall. Full comparison tables are in Section 15.

  • Yours faithfully vs Yours sincerely - "Yours faithfully," follows "Dear Sir/Madam," / "Sir," / "Respected Sir,"; "Yours sincerely," follows a named person ("Dear Mr. Raman,") and is used in D.O. letters.
  • Enquiry vs Inquiry - British tradition: enquiry = asking for information, inquiry = formal investigation; American English uses "inquiry" for both; "inquiry letter" and "enquiry letter" are both accepted.
  • Quotation vs Estimate - a quotation gives firm prices and terms with a validity period; an estimate gives only an approximate cost.
  • Quotation vs Tender - a quotation is the seller's reply to a buyer's inquiry; a tender is a formal (often sealed) offer in response to an invitation, usually from public bodies.
  • Complaint letter vs Claim letter - a complaint reports a problem (defect, delay, shortage, wrong goods); a claim demands a specific remedy (refund, replacement, price reduction).
  • Claim letter vs Adjustment letter - the buyer writes the claim; the seller writes the adjustment letter that settles it.
  • Credit note vs Debit note - a credit note is issued by the seller and reduces what the buyer owes; a debit note is issued by the buyer when returning goods or claiming a deduction (a seller may also send one for an undercharge).
  • Proforma invoice vs Invoice - a proforma invoice is a preliminary bill sent before supply; an invoice is the bill for goods actually supplied.
  • Letter of Credit vs Credit letter - an L/C is a bank's undertaking to pay the seller against specified documents; a credit letter is a business letter about credit (opening a credit account, status inquiry, granting or refusing credit).
  • Official letter vs D.O. letter - official: "Sir/Madam," → "Yours faithfully,", begins "I am directed to ..."; D.O.: "Dear Shri Kumar," → "Yours sincerely,", personal tone, first person.
  • Office Memorandum vs Office Order - an O.M. passes between Ministries/Departments in the third person; an Office Order gives internal instructions within an office (distribution of work, leave, postings).
  • Notification vs Resolution - a notification publishes rules, orders, appointments and promotions in the Gazette; a resolution announces important policy decisions or the appointment of committees or commissions (also in the Gazette).
  • Endorsement (government) vs Endorsement (banking) - government: a note at the foot of a communication forwarding copies ("Copy forwarded to ... for information") or returning a paper; banking: a signature on the back of a cheque to transfer it.
  • Complimentary vs Complementary - complimentary (as in "complimentary close") = polite, expressing respect; complementary = completing something.
  • Stationery vs Stationary - stationery = paper, envelopes and other writing materials; stationary = not moving.

MEMORY TRICK: stationEry - E for Envelopes; complIment - I say something polite; Credit note - Cuts what the buyer owes.

14.6 Question Patterns with Worked Examples

All 20 questions below are PYQ-style: written in the style of exam questions for practice. They are NOT actual previous-year questions. For each pattern, read the "How to attack" line first, try the two questions, then open the answer.

Pattern 1: Match the following

How to attack: Fix the one or two pairs you are surest of, then strike out every option that contradicts them; usually only one option survives.

PQ1. (PYQ-style) Match List I (communication) with List II (feature) and choose the correct code.

List IList II
A. Office Memorandum1. Published in the Official Gazette
B. Demi-official letter2. Same information sent to many offices or persons
C. Notification3. Third person; no salutation and no close
D. Circular4. Personal tone; "Dear Shri Kumar," and "Yours sincerely,"
  1. A-3, B-4, C-2, D-1
  2. A-4, B-3, C-1, D-2
  3. A-3, B-4, C-1, D-2
  4. A-1, B-4, C-3, D-2
Answer

Answer: (c) - O.M. = third person with no salutation or close; D.O. = personal tone; Notification = Gazette; Circular = same information to many.

PQ2. (PYQ-style) Match List I (document) with List II (description) and choose the correct code.

List IList II
A. Quotation1. Formal numbered document issued by the buyer to place an order
B. Purchase Order2. Seller's statement of prices and terms in reply to an inquiry
C. Credit note3. Bank's undertaking to pay the seller against specified documents
D. Letter of Credit4. Issued by the seller; reduces the amount the buyer owes
  1. A-1, B-2, C-4, D-3
  2. A-2, B-1, C-4, D-3
  3. A-2, B-1, C-3, D-4
  4. A-4, B-1, C-2, D-3
Answer

Answer: (b) - the seller quotes, the buyer issues the PO, the seller issues the credit note, and the bank gives the L/C undertaking.

Pattern 2: Fill in the blanks

How to attack: Read the whole sentence, decide what the blank needs (preposition, verb form or noun), then check that the completed sentence is formal and idiomatic.

PQ3. (PYQ-style) Fill in the blank: "I look forward to ______ from you."

  1. hear
  2. heard
  3. have heard
  4. hearing
Answer

Answer: (d) - in "look forward to", "to" is a preposition, so it takes the -ing form: "look forward to hearing".

PQ4. (PYQ-style) Fill in the blank: "With reference ______ your letter No. 245 dated 5 October 2026, we wish to inform you that the goods have been dispatched."

  1. of
  2. for
  3. to
  4. about
Answer

Answer: (c) - the fixed phrase is "with reference to".

Pattern 3: Sentence correction

How to attack: Check the usual suspects in order: redundant or outdated words ("revert back", "herewith"), tense with time words ("since" / "for"), the verb form after fixed phrases, and informal words; the answer must be both correct and formal.

PQ5. (PYQ-style) Choose the correct and most formal version of: "Kindly revert back at your earliest convenience."

  1. Please reply at your earliest convenience.
  2. Kindly revert back to us at your earliest convenience.
  3. Please revert back soon.
  4. Kindly revert back with the same at your earliest convenience.
Answer

Answer: (a) - "revert back" is redundant and outdated; use "reply" or "respond". "The same" used as a pronoun is also outdated.

PQ6. (PYQ-style) Choose the correct replacement for the part in bold: "The road in our street is not repaired since two years."

  1. was not repaired since two years
  2. is not being repaired for two years
  3. has not been repaired for two years
  4. has not been repaired since two years
Answer

Answer: (c) - an action continuing up to now needs the present perfect; "for" goes with a period (two years), "since" with a point of time (since 2024).

Pattern 4: Choose the correct phrase

How to attack: Prefer the short, modern, polite form; reject redundant words ("herewith"), dated expressions ("do the needful", "the same" as a pronoun) and anything informal.

PQ7. (PYQ-style) Which is the most appropriate modern replacement for the outdated request "Please do the needful"?

  1. Kindly do the needful at the earliest.
  2. Do whatever is needful.
  3. Please do the needful immediately.
  4. Please take the necessary action.
Answer

Answer: (d) - "do the needful" is outdated Indian office usage; "take the necessary action" is the clear modern form.

PQ8. (PYQ-style) Choose the best sentence to mention an enclosure in a formal letter.

  1. Please find enclosed herewith the bill.
  2. I enclose a copy of the bill.
  3. Enclosed herewith please find the same.
  4. The bill is enclosed herewith for your needful.
Answer

Answer: (b) - "I enclose" (or "Please find enclosed") is modern; "herewith" is redundant, and "the same" as a pronoun and "needful" are outdated.

Pattern 5: Identify the type of letter

How to attack: Ask three questions - who writes, to whom, and why; the purpose word (ask, quote, order, complain, claim, settle, remind) usually names the letter.

PQ9. (PYQ-style) A stationery shop in Salem writes to a paper mill asking for its latest price list, trade discount and delivery terms. This letter is a/an:

  1. Inquiry letter
  2. Order letter
  3. Quotation
  4. Adjustment letter
Answer

Answer: (a) - asking for information on prices and terms is an inquiry (enquiry) letter; the mill's reply with prices will be the quotation.

PQ10. (PYQ-style) A company replies to a customer's complaint about damaged goods, apologises and agrees to replace the goods free of cost. This reply is a/an:

  1. Claim letter
  2. Complaint letter
  3. Adjustment letter
  4. Collection letter
Answer

Answer: (c) - the seller's reply that settles a complaint or claim is an adjustment letter.

Pattern 6: Identify the appropriate salutation

How to attack: Decide the relationship first: unknown person or office → "Dear Sir/Madam,"; named person → "Dear Mr. Raman,"; family or friend → "Dear Priya,"; officer to officer (D.O.) → "Dear Shri Kumar,".

PQ11. (PYQ-style) You are writing to the Editor of a newspaper about the poor condition of a public park. The most appropriate salutation is:

  1. Hi Editor,
  2. My dear Editor,
  3. Dear Editor Sir,
  4. Dear Sir/Madam,
Answer

Answer: (d) - an editor is addressed "Dear Sir/Madam," (or "Sir,"), and the letter closes with "Yours faithfully,".

PQ12. (PYQ-style) A Deputy Secretary writes a demi-official letter to Thiru R. Kumar, Joint Director, asking him to expedite a pending file. The correct salutation is:

  1. Sir,
  2. Dear Thiru Kumar,
  3. Respected Sir,
  4. Dear Sir/Madam,
Answer

Answer: (b) - a D.O. letter uses honorific + name ("Dear Shri Kumar," in Central usage, "Dear Thiru Kumar," in Tamil Nadu) and closes with "Yours sincerely,".

Pattern 7: Identify the appropriate complimentary close

How to attack: Read the salutation, then pair it: Sir/Madam forms → "Yours faithfully,"; named person or D.O. letter → "Yours sincerely,"; family and friends → "Your loving ..."; O.M., Office Order, Notification and government Circular have no close at all.

PQ13. (PYQ-style) A formal letter begins "Dear Mr. Raman,". The correct complimentary close is:

  1. Yours sincerely,
  2. Yours faithfully,
  3. Your loving friend,
  4. Yours affectionately,
Answer

Answer: (a) - a named person takes "Yours sincerely,"; "Yours faithfully," follows "Dear Sir/Madam,".

PQ14. (PYQ-style) A student writes a leave letter to the Principal beginning "Respected Sir,". The correct complimentary close is:

  1. With love,
  2. Yours affectionately,
  3. Yours faithfully,
  4. Your loving student,
Answer

Answer: (c) - "Respected Sir," pairs with "Yours faithfully,"; school students may also use the traditional "Yours obediently,".

Pattern 8: Arrange the parts of a letter

How to attack: Fix the first and the last parts, then fill the middle in the fixed order: date → receiver's address → subject → salutation → body → close.

PQ15. (PYQ-style) Arrange these parts of a formal letter (full-block format, as used in this book) in the correct order. They come after the sender's address: P Salutation; Q Date; R Subject; S Receiver's address; T Complimentary close.

  1. S Q R P T
  2. Q R S P T
  3. R Q S P T
  4. Q S R P T
Answer

Answer: (d) - date → receiver's address → subject → salutation → (body) → close; the older British layout that puts the subject after the salutation is an accepted variant.

PQ16. (PYQ-style) Arrange the parts of an informal letter in the correct order: P Body; Q Salutation; R Sender's address; S Date; T Close and first name.

  1. R S Q P T
  2. R Q S P T
  3. S R Q P T
  4. R S P Q T
Answer

Answer: (a) - sender's address (short) → date → salutation → body → close → first name; an informal letter has no subject line and no receiver's address.

Pattern 9: Vocabulary-based

How to attack: Watch for look-alike spellings (stationery / stationary, complimentary / complementary, principal / principle) and choose the meaning that fits the letter context, not just any dictionary meaning.

PQ17. (PYQ-style) Choose the correctly spelt word: "Please send us your catalogue of office ______."

  1. stationary
  2. stationery
  3. stationarie
  4. stationery's
Answer

Answer: (b) - stationery = paper, envelopes and writing materials; stationary = not moving.

PQ18. (PYQ-style) In the term "complimentary close", the word "complimentary" means:

  1. completing or making whole
  2. free of charge
  3. final or concluding
  4. polite; expressing respect
Answer

Answer: (d) - it comes from "compliment" (a polite expression); "complementary" (with e) means completing. "Free of charge" is another meaning of the word, but not the meaning here.

Pattern 10: Statement-based ("Which of the following statements is/are correct?")

How to attack: Judge each statement alone as true or false, then pick the option; a single wrong word (buyer for seller, faithfully for sincerely, first person for third person) makes the whole statement false.

PQ19. (PYQ-style) Which of the following statements is/are correct?

1. An Office Memorandum is written in the third person and has no salutation.

2. A demi-official letter ends with "Yours faithfully,".

3. A Notification publishes rules, orders or appointments in the Official Gazette.

  1. 1 and 2 only
  2. 1 and 3 only
  3. 2 and 3 only
  4. 1, 2 and 3
Answer

Answer: (b) - statement 2 is wrong: a D.O. letter ends with "Yours sincerely,".

PQ20. (PYQ-style) Which of the following statements is/are correct?

1. "Yours faithfully," is the correct close for a letter beginning "Dear Mr. Raman,".

2. The subject line of a formal letter is a short phrase and does not end with a full stop.

3. Contractions such as "don't" and "can't" should be avoided in formal letters.

  1. 2 and 3 only
  2. 1 and 2 only
  3. 1 and 3 only
  4. 1, 2 and 3
Answer

Answer: (a) - statement 1 is wrong: a letter to a named person closes with "Yours sincerely,".

14.7 Strategy for Descriptive Letter Questions

A descriptive letter is marked on format, content, language and length together. A good plan and a quick final check protect marks that are otherwise lost to small slips.

Time plan

The time you can give a letter depends on the paper. Whatever it is, split it roughly in this ratio. The 20-minute column is only an example.

StageShare of timeExample: 20-minute letterWhat you do
PlanAbout 15%3 minutesRead the question; note who you are, the receiver, the purpose and the word limit; choose the register and the salutation-close pair; jot 3-4 content points.
WriteAbout 70%14 minutesWrite the format lines, then the opening, main and closing paragraphs, all in full-block form.
CheckAbout 15%3 minutesRun the 10-point checklist below; correct spelling, tense, punctuation and word count.

Plan - Write - Check: step by step

  1. Plan: Underline the key facts in the question - your role, the receiver, the problem or purpose, dates, places and the word limit.
  2. Plan: Decide the register: official or unknown receiver = formal; known person (not close) on a personal or partly official matter = semi-formal; family or friend = informal.
  3. Plan: Fix the salutation-close pair and your identity. If the question gives no name or address, use "Examination Hall" as the address and sign as "ABC"; if it gives them, use exactly those.
  4. Plan: Jot 3-4 content points in the order you will use them: purpose, facts, effect, request.
  5. Write: Format lines first, every line at the left margin: address, date, "To", receiver's designation and address, subject, salutation.
  6. Write: Opening paragraph - one or two sentences stating why you are writing.
  7. Write: Main paragraph(s) - facts in logical order: what happened, when, where, and its effect.
  8. Write: Closing paragraph - the exact action you want, ending with a polite sentence such as "Thank you for your time." inside the same paragraph.
  9. Write: Complimentary close and name, then "Encl.:" or "Copy to:" only if needed.
  10. Check: Run the 10-point checklist below. Correct a mistake neatly with a single line through the word; do not overwrite.

Handling the word limit

  • Follow the instruction exactly. If the question says "not exceeding 150 words", stay within 150.
  • If the question does not say whether the address, date and other format lines are counted, keep the body close to the limit and still write all the format lines in full.
  • Budget by paragraph. For a 150-word body: opening about 25 words, main paragraph(s) about 95 words, closing about 30 words.
  • Count quickly: count the words in three lines of your handwriting, take the average per line and multiply by the number of lines.
  • Too short: add a relevant fact (a date, a place, the effect on people, a specific request) - never filler sentences.
  • Too long: cut repeated points, long greetings and background stories; keep the facts and the request.
  • Do not drop any format part to save words; the format is part of the answer.

10-point format checklist (run it before moving on)

  1. Sender's address at the top-left (or "Examination Hall"); no name at the top.
  2. Date written as "12 October 2026".
  3. "To" on its own line, then the receiver's designation and full address, with the pin code written as "Madurai - 625 001".
  4. "Subject:" + a short noun phrase, placed before the salutation, with no full stop.
  5. "Reference:" line only if you are replying to earlier correspondence.
  6. Salutation suits the receiver and ends with a comma; never combine "Dear" and "Respected" in one salutation.
  7. Body in three parts - purpose → details → request or action - and on one purpose only.
  8. Close pairs with the salutation ("Dear Sir/Madam," → "Yours faithfully,"; "Dear Mr. Raman," → "Yours sincerely,"): capital Y, small f or s, no apostrophe, comma after.
  9. Name below the close ("ABC" if no name is given), designation where relevant, and "Encl.:" or "Copy to:" only if needed.
  10. Language: no contractions, slang, exclamation marks or outdated phrases; names, designations, figures and word count checked.

MUST REMEMBER: Plan → Write → Check; the salutation decides the close; the question decides your identity - never write your real name or address in an exam answer.

15 High-Yield Differences

Each pair below is compared in a Basis table and ends with one memory trick. The ten pairs marked HIGH-FREQUENCY should be revised first. 15.22 puts all 21 pairs in one table for last-minute revision. Related sections: business and transactional letters - Sections 3 and 5; banking letters - Section 4; government forms - Section 6; formal, semi-formal and informal letters in full - Section 12.

How to write a "distinguish between" answer: give a one-line definition of each term; then write 4 to 6 points of difference, each point on the SAME basis for both terms (meaning, sender, receiver, stage, purpose, format, effect); end with one example of each. A two-column table is the clearest layout. For a short answer, the one-line difference in 15.22 is enough.

15.1 Inquiry vs Enquiry HIGH-FREQUENCY

BasisInquiryEnquiry
Meaning (British tradition)A formal investigation into a matter, usually ordered by an authority; serious and official - it may end in findings, a report or punishmentAsking for information; a simple, routine question with no official consequence
Common phrasesorder / hold / conduct an inquiry; an inquiry into the matter; an inquiry reportmake an enquiry; reply to an enquiry; "Thank you for your enquiry of 5 October 2026."
Who makes itA court, commission, committee, the police or a government departmentAnyone - a customer, buyer, student or traveller who wants to know something
Verbinquire into = investigate: "The committee will inquire into the accident."enquire about = ask about: "She enquired about the train timings."
American English"Inquiry" is used for BOTH meaningsRare - "enquiry" is chiefly British
ExamplesA judicial inquiry into a fire accident; a Commission of Inquiry; a departmental inquiry against an officerA price enquiry to a supplier; an enquiry about admission; the enquiry counter at a railway station
In letter writing"Inquiry letter" - accepted; this book uses it"Enquiry letter" - equally accepted (British and Indian usage)

MEMORY TRICK: INquiry = INvestigation; Enquiry = Everyday asking - but "inquiry letter" and "enquiry letter" are both correct.

15.2 Inquiry letter vs Quotation letter

BasisInquiry letterQuotation letter
MeaningA letter asking a supplier for information - price list, catalogue, samples, discount, delivery and payment termsThe seller's statement of prices, terms and conditions for the goods asked about
Written by → sent toProspective buyer (a trader, institution or individual) → manufacturer, wholesaler or other sellerSeller → prospective buyer
StageFirst step of a business dealSecond step - sent in reply to the inquiry, before the order
PurposeTo collect information and compare suppliers before buyingTo offer the goods clearly and attractively and win the order
Main contentsGoods needed, approximate quantity, date required, questions on price, discount, delivery and payment terms; a request for a catalogue or samplesPrice per unit, trade discount, cash discount, taxes, packing and delivery terms (Ex-works, FOR, FOB, CIF), payment terms, delivery period, validity period
EffectNo commitment on either side - the buyer is not bound to buyA firm offer - the seller commits to the quoted prices for the validity period; the buyer is still free not to order
Example line"Please send us your latest price list for A4 notebooks and your terms of payment.""We quote Rs. 45 per notebook, less 10% trade discount. This quotation is valid for 30 days."

MEMORY TRICK: I comes before Q - the buyer's Inquiry asks, the seller's Quotation answers.

15.3 Quotation vs Invoice HIGH-FREQUENCY

BasisQuotationInvoice
MeaningThe seller's statement of prices, terms and conditions offered to a prospective buyerThe bill issued by the seller for goods supplied (or services rendered)
StageBEFORE the order - in reply to an inquiryAFTER the order - when the goods are supplied or dispatched (it often travels with the goods or follows by post or e-mail)
Issued by → toSeller → prospective buyerSeller → buyer (customer)
PurposeTo offer goods and win the orderTo state the amount due and ask for payment; it is also the seller's record of the sale and the buyer's proof of purchase
Main contentsPrices, discounts, taxes, delivery and payment terms, validity periodInvoice number and date, names and addresses of seller and buyer (with GSTIN), description, quantity, rate, taxes, total amount, due date; often "E&OE" (Errors and Omissions Excepted)
Financial effectNothing is payable; the buyer may accept it or ignore itPayment becomes due by the stated due date; both parties enter it in their accounts
Related termsEstimate = approximate cost; Tender = formal (often sealed) offer made in response to an invitation, usually from public bodiesProforma invoice = preliminary bill sent BEFORE supply (for advance payment or import formalities); it does not replace the final invoice

MEMORY TRICK: Quotation = "this is what it WILL cost" (before the order); Invoice = "this is what you OWE" (after supply).

15.4 Order vs Purchase Order

BasisOrder (order letter)Purchase Order (PO)
MeaningA letter in which the buyer places an order for goodsA formal, numbered document issued by the buyer to order goods
FormOrdinary business letter - salutation, paragraphs, complimentary closePrinted or computer-generated form - PO number, date, table of items, printed terms and conditions, authorised signature
Used bySmall firms, shopkeepers, individual buyers; one-off ordersCompanies, government offices and institutions that have a purchase department; large or repeated orders
How items are shownIn sentences or a short list inside the letterIn a fixed table: item, description, quantity, unit price, amount, total
IdentificationMay carry a Ref. No.; later letters refer to "our order dated ..."Unique PO number, quoted on the invoice, the delivery challan and all later letters
Legal / record valueThe buyer's offer to buy; binding once the seller accepts it or supplies the goodsBinding once accepted by the seller; also the buyer's official record - matched with the invoice and the Goods Received Note (GRN) before payment
Example"Please supply 500 A4 notebooks at Rs. 45 each as per your quotation dated 5 October 2026.""PO No. 2026/117 dated 12 October 2026 - A4 notebooks, 500 nos. @ Rs. 45 = Rs. 22,500 - delivery by 30 October 2026"

MEMORY TRICK: An order letter TALKS in paragraphs; a PO LISTS in a numbered form - every PO is an order, but not every order is a PO.

15.5 Complaint vs Claim

BasisComplaint letterClaim letter
MeaningThe buyer reports a problem - defect, delay, shortage, wrong goods, poor serviceThe buyer demands a specific remedy or compensation for a loss
FocusThe PROBLEM - what went wrongThe REMEDY - refund, replacement, price reduction or compensation
Written by → sent toBuyer/customer → seller; also a citizen → an authority (civic complaint)Buyer → seller; also consignee → carrier (transport company), insured person → insurance company
TimingAs soon as the problem is noticedWith or after the complaint, once the loss is known and can be valued; insurance and carrier claims must be made within the time allowed in the policy or contract
Main contentsOrder/invoice details, nature of the problem, a request to look into itOrder/invoice details, loss suffered, the exact remedy or amount demanded, proof (invoice copy, photographs, survey report)
ToneFirm but polite; states facts without anger, insults or threatsFirm, precise and specific; may fix a reasonable time limit for settlement
Example line"Twenty of the 500 notebooks supplied against our order No. 117 were water-damaged.""Please replace the 20 damaged notebooks or credit Rs. 900 to our account."

MEMORY TRICK: Complaint says WHAT went wrong; Claim says WHAT you want - in practice one letter often does both.

15.6 Complaint vs Adjustment

BasisComplaint letterAdjustment letter
MeaningA letter reporting a problem with goods or serviceThe seller's reply that settles a complaint or claim
Written by → sent toBuyer/customer → sellerSeller → the buyer/customer who complained
StageOpens the matter - after faulty, late or short goods are receivedCloses the matter - after the seller has checked the complaint
PurposeTo get the problem correctedTo settle the matter fairly, keep the customer's goodwill and avoid losing future business
Main contentsOrder/invoice details, the problem, the action requestedThanks for the letter, regret or apology, cause of the problem, the decision - full grant, partial grant or polite refusal with reasons - and an assurance for the future
ToneFirm, factual and politeCourteous and positive; a refusal is explained tactfully, never rudely
Example line"Twenty notebooks in your consignment of 12 October 2026 were water-damaged.""We regret the damage. Twenty fresh notebooks are being sent to you today at our cost."

MEMORY TRICK: The buyer COMPLAINS, the seller ADJUSTS - the complaint is the problem, the adjustment is the settlement.

15.7 Credit note vs Debit note HIGH-FREQUENCY

BasisCredit noteDebit note
MeaningA note telling the buyer that his account has been CREDITED - the amount he owes is REDUCEDA note telling the other party that his account has been DEBITED
Issued by → toSELLER → buyerBUYER → seller, when returning goods or claiming a deduction; a seller may also send one to the buyer to charge an extra amount
Common reasonsGoods returned by the buyer; overcharge in the invoice; allowance for damaged or short goods; a discount or rebate allowed after the invoiceGoods returned to the seller; overcharge or shortage claimed; (seller's debit note) undercharge in the invoice
Effect on the buyer's duesREDUCES the amount the buyer owesBuyer's debit note: claims a deduction from the amount payable. Seller's debit note: INCREASES the amount the buyer owes
Entry in the issuer's books (goods returned)Seller debits Sales Returns (Returns Inward) and credits the buyer's account; recorded in the Sales Returns BookBuyer debits the seller's account and credits Purchases Returns (Returns Outward); recorded in the Purchases Returns Book
Side note: GST lawIssued by the supplier when the invoice charged too much or goods are returnedAlso issued by the supplier - when the invoice charged too little
Example"Credit Note No. CN-45: your account has been credited with Rs. 900 for 20 damaged notebooks returned.""Debit Note No. DN-12: your account has been debited with Rs. 900 for 20 damaged notebooks returned to you."

MEMORY TRICK: Goods go back: the Buyer sends a Debit note, the Seller sends a Credit note ("B-D, S-C") - and a credit note always cuts the buyer's bill.

15.8 Credit letter vs Letter of Credit HIGH-FREQUENCY

BasisCredit letterLetter of Credit (L/C)
MeaningAny business letter ABOUT credit - asking for, checking, granting or refusing creditA BANK's written undertaking to pay the seller when specified documents are presented
Written / issued byA buyer, a seller or a trade refereeThe issuing bank, at the request of the buyer/importer (the applicant)
Sent toA supplier, customer, trade referee or bankThe seller/exporter (the beneficiary), usually through an advising bank in the seller's country
PurposeTo obtain or decide on trade credit - e.g. 30 or 60 days to payTo assure payment to a seller who does not know the buyer well - used mainly in foreign trade (e.g. an importer in Chennai buying machines from a German exporter)
KindsRequest to open a credit account; credit (status) inquiry to referees; letter granting credit; letter refusing credit. (A bank's "credit advice" only tells a customer that his account has been credited.)Irrevocable, confirmed, sight (paid when the documents are presented), usance (paid after a fixed period)
PartiesBuyer and seller (plus referees)Applicant, issuing bank, beneficiary, advising / confirming bank
Financial effectNo guarantee of payment - only a request, information or a decisionThe bank's own promise to pay against documents - the seller relies on the bank, not on the buyer

MEMORY TRICK: A credit letter TALKS about credit; a Letter of Credit is a BANK'S PROMISE to pay.

15.9 Reference letter vs Recommendation letter HIGH-FREQUENCY

BasisReference letterRecommendation letter
MeaningA general statement of facts about a person's character, conduct or workA letter that actively endorses (supports) a person for a SPECIFIC job, admission, scholarship or award
Written by → addressed toA former employer, principal or teacher → often "To Whom It May Concern" - no particular readerA teacher, guide or supervisor who knows the candidate's work closely → a specific recipient - an employer, a selection committee or an admissions office
PurposeTo confirm facts - period of work or study, duties, conductTo persuade the reader to select the person
ToneNeutral and factualWarm, positive and persuasive
ContentDates, post or class, main duties, general character and conduct; usually no comparison with othersSpecific achievements and qualities matched to the post or course, often with a comparison ("one of the best students I have taught"); a clear statement such as "I strongly recommend her"
UseGeneral - the same letter can be shown for many purposesWritten for one purpose - one post, course or award
Example line"Mr. S. Arun worked in our office as an Assistant from June 2022 to May 2026. His work and conduct were satisfactory.""I strongly recommend Ms. R. Kavya for admission to your M.A. English programme; she was the best student in her batch."

MEMORY TRICK: Reference = "this is what she is" (facts, for anyone); Recommendation = "please select her" (support, for someone specific).

15.10 Bank reference vs Credit reference

BasisBank referenceCredit reference
MeaningA bank's statement about its customer's account and general standingInformation about a person's or firm's creditworthiness - the ability and willingness to pay debts
Given byThe customer's own bankTrade referees (other suppliers who have given credit) or credit information companies (e.g. CIBIL)
Given whenOnly on request - a bank keeps its customer's affairs confidentialOn request, when a supplier or lender is deciding whether to give credit
Based onHow the bank account has been maintained and operatedActual payment record - credit allowed, dues, delays or defaults; a credit report or score
Nature of the replyBrief, general and cautious; balances and confidential details are not disclosedMore specific - credit limit allowed, payment habits, amounts outstanding
Used forChecking the general standing of a new customer or business partner, e.g. before opening a credit accountDeciding whether to grant credit or a loan, how much and for how long
Example line"The firm has maintained a current account with us since 2015; its dealings have been satisfactory.""They have bought from us on 30 days' credit for five years and have always paid on time."

MEMORY TRICK: Bank reference = what your BANK says about your account; Credit reference = what your CREDITORS (suppliers, CIBIL) say about your paying habits.

15.11 Circular vs Notice HIGH-FREQUENCY

BasisCircularNotice
MeaningThe same information sent (circulated) to many individual recipientsA formal announcement displayed publicly, or a formal legal intimation to a person
How it reaches peopleEach recipient gets a copy - by post, by e-mail, or in a file passed round for signaturePut up on a notice board or published in a newspaper or on a website; a legal notice is served on the person concerned
AudienceA defined group - offices, staff, members, customers (often a distribution list)The public or everyone concerned; a legal notice - one specific person
Issued byGovernment offices, heads of institutions, companies (to branches, dealers or customers)Institutions, offices, companies, courts; an advocate on behalf of a client (legal notice)
PurposeTo pass on instructions, information or changes - e.g. new office timings, change of address, opening of a new branchTo announce a meeting, event, holiday, tender, auction or examination schedule; a legal notice demands or warns
FormatGovernment circular: number, date, "CIRCULAR", subject, body, signature and designation - no salutation, no close. A business circular letter has "Dear Sir/Madam," and "Yours faithfully,"Public notice: name of the institution, heading "NOTICE", date, title, short body, signature, name and designation - no salutation, no close
Example line"All Heads of Department are requested to send the staff attendance statement for October 2026 by 5 November 2026.""The college will remain closed on 2 October 2026 on account of Gandhi Jayanti."

MEMORY TRICK: A Circular CIRCULATES - a copy goes round to each person; a Notice is NOTICED - one copy is put up for all to see.

15.12 Memorandum vs Office Memorandum HIGH-FREQUENCY

BasisMemorandum (memo)Office Memorandum (O.M.)
MeaningA short internal communication within an organisationThe formal government form for communication between Ministries/Departments
Used inAny organisation - company, bank, school, hospital, officeGovernment - Ministries and Departments
DirectionInside one organisation - manager to staff, section to section, staff to managerBetween Ministries/Departments; also with attached offices and with employees
Person and styleDirect and brief; first person or plain instructionsThird person - "The undersigned is directed to ..."
FormatHeading MEMO; To, From, Date and Subject lines; short body; initials or signature; no salutation, no closeO.M. No., name of the Ministry/Department, place and date, heading "OFFICE MEMORANDUM", subject, body; signed with name and designation; addressee at the bottom-left; no salutation, no close
Typical usesInstructions, reminders, requests, short reports, asking a staff member for an explanationAsking for or supplying information; conveying decisions and instructions of the Government to other Departments and to employees
Example line"To: All Branch Staff; From: Branch Manager; Date: 12 October 2026; Subject: Working hours during audit week""The undersigned is directed to refer to this Ministry's O.M. of even number dated ... and to say that ..."

MEMORY TRICK: A memo stays INSIDE one organisation; an O.M. travels BETWEEN government departments, in the third person.

15.13 Memorandum vs Letter HIGH-FREQUENCY

BasisMemorandum (memo)Letter
MeaningA short internal communication within an organisationA written message usually sent OUTSIDE the organisation - to firms, customers, officials, the public or friends
AudienceColleagues and staff of the same organisationOutsiders - customers, suppliers, government offices, individuals
FormatMEMO heading; To / From / Date / Subject block; no inside address, no salutation, no complimentary closeSender's address or letterhead, date, inside address, subject, salutation, body, complimentary close, signature
Length and toneShort and direct; often in numbered points; one topic per memoCan be longer; complete and courteous; tone depends on the type (formal, semi-formal or informal)
SignatureInitials or signature of the senderFull signature with name (and designation) below the complimentary close
DeliveryInternal mail, office file or e-mail - no envelope or postage neededPost, courier, hand delivery or e-mail
Example opening"All section heads will attend a staff meeting on 16 October 2026 at 4 p.m. in the conference hall." (no "Dear", no "Yours")"Dear Sir/Madam, Thank you for your order No. 117 dated 12 October 2026."

MEMORY TRICK: Memo = IN-house, with no "Dear" and no "Yours"; Letter = goes OUT, with salutation and close.

15.14 Petition vs Representation

BasisPetitionRepresentation
MeaningA formal request to an authority for redress (remedy) or action; also a legal application to a courtAn individual's formal statement to an authority presenting a grievance or seeking reconsideration of a decision
Made byOften signed by many - residents, villagers, members of an association (one person may also petition)Usually one individual - often an employee about his own service matter
Addressed toAn authority - Collector, Commissioner, Minister, the Government; a court (e.g. a writ petition)A higher authority - usually in the writer's own department or organisation, or the Government
Subject matterCommon or public problems - road, water supply, street lights, bus service; legal relief in a courtPersonal grievance - seniority, promotion, transfer, pay fixation; a request to reconsider an order
RouteSubmitted directly to the authority (e.g. at the Collector's grievance day meeting); in a court, filed as per the court's rulesThrough proper channel - via the immediate superior (a "Through: ..." line)
StructureFacts of the problem, the relief requested (in a court: the "prayer"), names, addresses and signatures of all petitionersFacts, reference to the order or rule, grounds for reconsideration, the specific relief requested; one signature with designation
Example line"We, the undersigned residents of Anna Nagar, request that street lights be provided on 4th Main Road.""I request that my seniority be refixed and my case for promotion be reconsidered."

MEMORY TRICK: Petition = many People, a Public problem; Representation = one person's own case, sent through proper channel.

15.15 Application vs Request

BasisApplicationRequest
MeaningA formal request for something one is eligible for - job, leave, admission, loan, licenceAny asking for information, permission, help or action
ScopeNarrow - one special kind of requestWide - includes applications and many other kinds of asking
FormatOften a prescribed form or fixed format; certificates, ID proof or photographs enclosed; an employee's application to a higher authority goes through proper channelAny suitable letter or e-mail; usually no prescribed form
EligibilityThe applicant must meet stated conditions - age, qualification, service, incomeNo eligibility conditions; the receiver may agree or decline
OutcomeA formal decision - selection, sanction, admission, grant of leave, or rejectionInformation, permission, help or action - as the receiver decides
ExamplesJob application, leave application, application for admission, loan application, application for a licence or scholarshipRequest for a catalogue, for an extension of time, for permission to use the school ground, for a meeting
Example line"I wish to apply for the post of Junior Assistant advertised in The Hindu.""I request you to extend the last date for fee payment by one week."

MEMORY TRICK: Every application is a request, but not every request is an application - you APPLY only for what you are ELIGIBLE for.

15.16 Enclosure vs Annexure

BasisEnclosureAnnexure
MeaningA separate document sent in the same envelope with a letterA document added at the end of a main document, numbered and forming part of it
Relation to the main documentSeparate - it travels with the letter but stands on its ownPart of the main document - the two are read together
Used withLetters and applications sent by post or by handReports, agreements, tenders, government orders, project proposals
Numbering / markingListed below the signature as "Encl.: 1. ... 2. ..."; the body says "I enclose ..."Numbered and titled - Annexure I, Annexure II (or A, B); the text refers to it: "(see Annexure II)"
PositionLoose sheets placed with the letter; the Encl. list is at the bottom-left of the letterAfter the last page of the main document, each annexure starting on a new page with its heading
Typical itemsCopies of certificates, a cheque or demand draft, an invoice copy, photographsTables, statements, lists of names, maps, specimen forms, copies of earlier orders
Example"Encl.: 1. Copy of SSLC mark sheet 2. Demand draft for Rs. 500""The list of selected candidates is given in Annexure I."

MEMORY TRICK: ENclosure = ENclosed in the ENvelope (separate); ANNEXure = ANNEXed, joined to the document (part of it).

15.17 Attachment vs Enclosure

BasisAttachmentEnclosure
MeaningA file sent with an e-mail (or a paper clipped or stapled to a document)A separate document sent in the same envelope with a letter
MediumMostly electronic - a digital file travels with the e-mailPaper - placed loose in the envelope with the letter
Typical itemsResume (PDF), scanned certificates, photographs, spreadsheets, presentationsCertificate copies, a cheque or demand draft, photographs, a brochure
How it is mentioned"Please find attached ..." / "I have attached ..."; an "Attachment:" line may be added"Please find enclosed ..." / "I enclose ..."; an "Encl.:" line below the signature
Check before sendingRight file, readable format (PDF is safest for documents), reasonable size, clear file nameEvery item in the Encl. list is actually inside the envelope
Common mistakeWriting "please find attached" and forgetting to attach the file"Please find enclosed herewith" - outdated; write "Please find enclosed" or "I enclose"
Example"Please find attached my resume in PDF format.""I enclose a demand draft for Rs. 500 and two passport-size photographs."

MEMORY TRICK: The e-mail ATTACHES; the envelope ENCLOSES.

15.18 Acknowledgement vs Confirmation

BasisAcknowledgementConfirmation
MeaningConfirms RECEIPT only - of a letter, order, payment, application or goods. (Spelling: acknowledgement - British; acknowledgment - American; both correct.)Confirms AGREEMENT - that something will go ahead as stated (an order, booking, meeting or arrangement)
CommitmentNone on the matter itself - "received; under consideration"Commits the sender - the terms, date or arrangement are now fixed
TimingImmediately on receiving somethingAfter a decision is taken or the terms are agreed
PurposeTo assure the sender that the item has arrived safely; avoids doubt, delay and repeat sendingTo remove uncertainty and put the agreed terms on record
Typical phrases"We acknowledge with thanks the receipt of your letter dated ..."; "This is to acknowledge receipt of ...""We are pleased to confirm your order No. ..."; "This is to confirm that ..."
Common formsAcknowledgement slip for an application; receipt for a payment; acknowledgement of an orderOrder confirmation; hotel or ticket booking confirmation; confirmation of a meeting or interview date
Example line"We have received your order No. 117 dated 12 October 2026; it is being processed.""We confirm your order No. 117 for 500 notebooks at Rs. 45 each, for delivery by 30 October 2026."

MEMORY TRICK: Acknowledgement = "we GOT it"; Confirmation = "it is ON - agreed".

15.19 Reminder vs Notice

BasisReminderNotice (final / demand notice)
MeaningA polite follow-up about a pending action, payment or replyA formal, firmer intimation that may carry legal consequences
Stage (collection series)First reminder (polite) → second reminder (firmer)Final notice / demand - sent when the reminders have failed
ToneCourteous; assumes the delay is an oversightFirm, serious and formal; states the consequence clearly but stays polite
ContentsReference to the earlier letter or invoice, what is pending, a request to actExact amount or action demanded, a final deadline, the consequence (legal action, stoppage of credit or supply)
Sent bySeller, creditor, office or anyone awaiting a replyCreditor, bank or landlord - often through an advocate (legal notice)
Legal effectNone - it keeps the relationship friendlyMay be the first step to legal action - e.g. the demand notice for a dishonoured cheque under Section 138 of the Negotiable Instruments Act, 1881, sent within 30 days of receiving the bank's return memo
Example line"This is a reminder that our invoice No. 245 for Rs. 22,500, due on 30 September 2026, is still unpaid.""Unless Rs. 22,500 is paid within 15 days of receipt of this notice, we shall be compelled to take legal action."

MEMORY TRICK: A Reminder REQUESTS (a polite nudge); a Notice WARNS (the last chance before action).

15.20 Formal vs Semi-formal HIGH-FREQUENCY

BasisFormal letterSemi-formal letter
MeaningA letter written for an official, business or public purposeA letter to a person we know, but not closely, on a matter that is partly personal and partly official
Written toOfficials, offices, companies, editors, unknown persons - usually by designationKnown persons in a respectful relationship - a former teacher, neighbour, landlord, colleague, a friend's parent
Salutation"Dear Sir/Madam," / "Sir," / "Respected Sir,""Dear Mr. Raman," / "Dear Mrs. Sundaram," (title + surname)
Complimentary close"Yours faithfully," ("Yours sincerely," when a named person is addressed as "Dear Mr. Raman,")"Yours sincerely,"
Subject line and addressSubject line needed; receiver's designation and full addressSubject line optional; receiver's name and address
Tone and languageImpersonal and precise; no personal news, no contractionsRespectful but warm; a short personal touch is allowed; no slang, contractions avoided
ExamplesJob application, complaint to the Corporation Commissioner, letter to the editor, letter to a bank managerLetter to a former teacher seeking advice, to a landlord about repairs, to a neighbour about a shared problem, thanking a friend's father

MEMORY TRICK: Formal writes to a POST ("Sir/Madam ... Yours faithfully"); semi-formal writes to a PERSON you know ("Dear Mr. Raman ... Yours sincerely").

15.21 D.O. letter vs Official letter HIGH-FREQUENCY

BasisD.O. (demi-official) letterOfficial letter
MeaningA personal, friendly letter from one officer to another on an official matter - "demi" means half: half official, half personalA formal letter from a Government office to other governments, bodies, officials or the public
Addressed toAn officer by name (officer to officer)An authority by designation, another government, an organisation or a member of the public
PurposeTo draw personal attention, to expedite (speed up) a matter, or to exchange views or informationTo convey decisions, sanctions, instructions, replies and information formally - the normal form for Government correspondence with other governments, bodies and the public
Person and toneFirst person singular ("I"); personal, friendly toneFormal and impersonal; the officer writes for the Government - "I am directed to ..."
Salutation → close"Dear Shri Kumar," (Tamil Nadu: "Dear Thiru Kumar,") → "Yours sincerely,""Sir," / "Madam," / "Sir/Madam," → "Yours faithfully,"
Layout"D.O. No." and the writer's name and designation at the top; addressee's name and address at the bottom-left; signed personallyLetter number at the top; "To" and the addressee's designation and address at the top; subject line; signed with name and designation
Example opening"I am writing to seek your personal help in completing the land records for the new bus stand at Erode.""I am directed to inform you that the Government has sanctioned Rs. 5 lakh for the repair of the school building."

MEMORY TRICK: D.O. = "Dear Shri" + "I" + "Yours sincerely" (officer to officer); Official = "Sir" + "I am directed" + "Yours faithfully".

15.22 Master summary table

One line per pair - enough for a short answer. For self-testing, cover the right-hand column and recall each difference aloud.

PairOne-line difference
Inquiry vs EnquiryInquiry = formal investigation; enquiry = asking for information (British tradition); American English uses "inquiry" for both.
Inquiry letter vs Quotation letterInquiry = the buyer asks for prices and terms; quotation = the seller's reply giving prices, terms and a validity period, before the order.
Quotation vs InvoiceQuotation = price offer BEFORE the order; invoice = the bill AFTER supply, which makes payment due.
Order vs Purchase OrderOrder letter = an order placed in letter form; PO = the buyer's formal numbered document, binding once accepted by the seller.
Complaint vs ClaimComplaint reports the problem; claim demands a specific remedy - refund, replacement, price reduction or compensation.
Complaint vs AdjustmentComplaint = the buyer's letter about a problem; adjustment = the seller's reply that settles it (full grant, partial grant or polite refusal with reasons).
Credit note vs Debit noteCredit note = seller to buyer, reduces what the buyer owes; debit note = buyer to seller for returns or deductions (a seller's debit note charges an undercharge).
Credit letter vs Letter of CreditCredit letter = any letter about trade credit; L/C = a bank's written undertaking to pay the seller against specified documents.
Reference letter vs Recommendation letterReference = neutral statement of facts, often "To Whom It May Concern"; recommendation = persuasive endorsement for a specific post, admission or award, to a specific reader.
Bank reference vs Credit referenceBank reference = a bank's statement on its customer's account and standing; credit reference = creditworthiness report from trade referees or credit information companies (e.g. CIBIL).
Circular vs NoticeCircular = copies sent to many individual recipients; notice = displayed publicly, or served as a formal legal intimation.
Memorandum vs Office MemorandumMemo = short internal note in any organisation; O.M. = the formal government form between Ministries/Departments - third person, no salutation, no close.
Memorandum vs LetterMemo = internal, To/From/Subject block, no salutation or close; letter = usually external, full format with salutation and close.
Petition vs RepresentationPetition = request for redress or action, often signed by many (also to a court); representation = one person's grievance or plea for reconsideration, through proper channel.
Application vs RequestApplication = formal request for something one is eligible for, often in a prescribed format; request = any asking - every application is a request, not the reverse.
Enclosure vs AnnexureEnclosure = separate document in the same envelope; annexure = numbered document at the end of a main document, forming part of it.
Attachment vs EnclosureAttachment = file sent with an e-mail (or clipped); enclosure = separate document sent in the same envelope with a letter.
Acknowledgement vs ConfirmationAcknowledgement confirms RECEIPT only; confirmation confirms AGREEMENT - that it will go ahead as stated.
Reminder vs NoticeReminder = polite follow-up on a pending matter; notice = firm, formal intimation that may lead to legal action (final demand).
Formal vs Semi-formalFormal = official purpose, "Dear Sir/Madam ... Yours faithfully"; semi-formal = a known person, "Dear Mr. Raman ... Yours sincerely".
D.O. letter vs Official letterD.O. = officer to officer, personal, "Dear Shri Kumar ... Yours sincerely"; official = "Sir ... I am directed to ... Yours faithfully".

MUST REMEMBER: Who sends what in trade - Buyer → Seller: inquiry, order / purchase order, complaint, claim, debit note (for goods returned). Seller → Buyer: quotation, invoice, adjustment letter, credit note. Issuing bank → Seller (beneficiary): letter of credit. Normal sequence: Inquiry → Quotation → Order → Invoice; if something goes wrong: Complaint / Claim → Adjustment.

MUST REMEMBER: Salutation test - NO salutation and NO close: Office Memorandum, internal memo, government circular, public notice (also Office Order, Order, Notification). "Sir," / "Madam," (official letter) or "Dear Sir/Madam," (other formal letters) + "Yours faithfully,". "Dear Shri Kumar," (Tamil Nadu: "Dear Thiru Kumar,") + "Yours sincerely,": D.O. letter. "Dear Mr. Raman," + "Yours sincerely,": semi-formal letter, and a formal letter to a named person.

16 Practice Section

All questions in this section are PYQ-style: modelled on competitive-exam patterns, not actual previous-year questions.

16.1 Multiple Choice Questions (25)

Choose the correct option. Each answer gives the rule behind it.

MCQ1. Which salutation and complimentary close are correctly paired? (PYQ-style)

  1. Dear Sir/Madam, … Yours sincerely,
  2. Dear Mr. Raman, … Yours sincerely,
  3. Respected Sir, … Yours lovingly,
  4. My dear Kavin, … Yours faithfully,
Answer

Answer: (b) - A salutation that names the person ("Dear Mr./Mrs./Dr. + Surname,") takes "Yours sincerely,"; "Dear Sir/Madam," and "Respected Sir," take "Yours faithfully,", and a friend ("My dear Kavin,") gets an informal close such as "Your loving friend,".

MCQ2. A Tamil Nadu Government officer writes a demi-official (D.O.) letter to a fellow officer, Thiru S. Kumar, to speed up a pending file. Which opening and close are correct? (PYQ-style)

  1. Sir, … Yours faithfully,
  2. Respected Sir, … Yours obediently,
  3. Dear Sir/Madam, … Yours truly,
  4. Dear Thiru Kumar, … Yours sincerely,
Answer

Answer: (d) - A D.O. letter is personal in tone and written in the first person, so it names the officer ("Dear Thiru Kumar,"; Central Government: "Dear Shri Kumar,") and closes "Yours sincerely,"; option (a) is the pattern of an ordinary official letter.

MCQ3. In a formal letter written in full-block format, which is the correct order of the opening parts? (PYQ-style)

  1. Sender's address → Date → Receiver's address → Subject → Salutation
  2. Date → Sender's address → Salutation → Receiver's address → Subject
  3. Sender's address → Receiver's address → Salutation → Date → Subject
  4. Receiver's address → Date → Sender's address → Subject → Salutation
Answer

Answer: (a) - The standard exam layout is sender's address, date, "To" with the receiver's address, subject and then salutation; the traditional British/commerce-textbook layout that puts the subject after the salutation is an accepted variant, but none of the other options is valid in any layout.

MCQ4. Which is a correctly written subject line for a formal letter to the Municipal Commissioner? (PYQ-style)

  1. Subject: I am writing to complain that garbage is not collected regularly in our area.
  2. SUBJECT - GARBAGE PROBLEM!!!
  3. Subject: Irregular garbage collection in K.K. Nagar
  4. Subject: Regarding garbage, kindly do the needful
Answer

Answer: (c) - A subject line is a short noun phrase that begins with "Subject:" and has no full stop; (a) is a full sentence, (b) shouts with capitals and exclamation marks, and (d) is a request that uses an outdated phrase.

MCQ5. Why should an all-numeric date such as 04/05/2026 be avoided in a formal letter? (PYQ-style)

  1. Numbers are not permitted anywhere in a formal letter.
  2. It is ambiguous: it may be read as 4 May 2026 or as April 5, 2026.
  3. It does not show the day of the week.
  4. It does not show the year in full.
Answer

Answer: (b) - Indian and British usage reads day-month but American usage reads month-day, so write "4 May 2026" ("4th May, 2026" is an acceptable traditional Indian style and "May 4, 2026" is the American style).

MCQ6. Consider the following statements about an informal (personal) letter:

1. It has no subject line.

2. It has no receiver's address.

3. It ends with the writer's full name and designation.

Which of the statements given above is/are correct? (PYQ-style)

  1. 1 and 2 only
  2. 2 and 3 only
  3. 1 and 3 only
  4. 1, 2 and 3
Answer

Answer: (a) - An informal letter has a short sender's address, date, salutation, body, close and only the writer's first name; it has no subject line, no receiver's address and no designation.

MCQ7. In traditional British usage, which statement about "enquiry" and "inquiry" is correct? (PYQ-style)

  1. "Enquiry" means a formal investigation; "inquiry" means a request for information.
  2. Both words mean only a formal investigation.
  3. "Enquiry" means a request for information; "inquiry" means a formal investigation.
  4. "Inquiry" is used only in British English, never in American English.
Answer

Answer: (c) - British tradition uses "enquiry" for asking for information and "inquiry" for a formal investigation, while American English uses "inquiry" for both; hence "enquiry letter" and "inquiry letter" are both accepted.

MCQ8. Sri Murugan Stationers asked Kaveri Paper Mills for its rates. Kaveri Paper Mills replied with prices, trade discount and delivery terms, adding: "This offer is valid for 30 days." The reply is a/an: (PYQ-style)

  1. Tender
  2. Estimate
  3. Proforma invoice
  4. Quotation
Answer

Answer: (d) - A quotation is the seller's statement of prices, terms and conditions sent in reply to an inquiry, valid for a stated period (a firm offer); an estimate is only an approximate cost, a tender answers a formal invitation, and a proforma invoice is a preliminary bill sent before supply.

MCQ9. Which document is issued by the BUYER and becomes binding once the seller accepts it? (PYQ-style)

  1. Quotation
  2. Purchase Order
  3. Proforma invoice
  4. Tax invoice
Answer

Answer: (b) - A Purchase Order (PO) is a formal numbered document issued by the buyer and binding once the seller accepts it; the quotation, proforma invoice and tax invoice all come from the seller.

MCQ10. Consider the following statements:

1. A complaint letter reports a problem such as a defect, delay, shortage or wrong goods.

2. A claim letter demands a specific remedy such as a refund, replacement or price reduction.

3. An adjustment letter is written by the buyer to settle his own claim.

Which of the statements given above is/are correct? (PYQ-style)

  1. 1 only
  2. 1 and 2 only
  3. 2 and 3 only
  4. 1, 2 and 3
Answer

Answer: (b) - Statement 3 is wrong: an adjustment letter is the SELLER's reply that settles a complaint or claim by full grant, partial grant or polite refusal with reasons.

MCQ11. Arrange these extracts from a collection series in the order in which they would be sent: (PYQ-style)

1. "Unless the amount is received within seven days, we shall be compelled to take legal action."

2. "This is a reminder that our Invoice No. 214 dated 2 August 2026 is still unpaid; perhaps it has escaped your notice."

3. "We are disappointed that our earlier reminder has not been answered. Please settle the amount without further delay."

  1. 1 → 3 → 2
  2. 3 → 2 → 1
  3. 2 → 1 → 3
  4. 2 → 3 → 1
Answer

Answer: (d) - A collection series moves from a polite first reminder (2) to a firmer second reminder (3) and then to a final notice that warns of legal action (1).

MCQ12. M/s. Kaveri Traders sends one identical letter to all its customers announcing that its office will move to a new address from 1 November 2026. Such a letter is a: (PYQ-style)

  1. Circular letter
  2. Sales letter
  3. Status inquiry
  4. Memorandum
Answer

Answer: (a) - A circular carries the same information to many persons or offices at once (change of address, opening of a branch, a new partner); a sales letter tries to sell, a status inquiry asks referees about a customer's creditworthiness, and a memorandum is an internal note.

MCQ13. In a Letter of Credit (L/C) transaction, the "beneficiary" is: (PYQ-style)

  1. the buyer/importer at whose request the L/C is opened
  2. the seller/exporter who is paid on presenting the specified documents
  3. the bank that issues the L/C
  4. the bank that advises the L/C to the seller
Answer

Answer: (b) - The issuing bank opens the L/C at the request of the buyer/importer (applicant) and undertakes to pay the seller/exporter (beneficiary) when the specified documents are presented; the advising bank informs the seller that the L/C has been opened.

MCQ14. Kaveri Paper Mills (seller) overcharged Sri Murugan Stationers (buyer) by Rs. 2,000 in an invoice. To correct the error, Kaveri Paper Mills should send: (PYQ-style)

  1. a debit note
  2. a proforma invoice
  3. a credit note
  4. a debit advice
Answer

Answer: (c) - A credit note is issued by the seller and reduces the amount the buyer owes (goods returned, overcharge or allowance); a seller's debit note does the opposite, charging an extra amount for an undercharge.

MCQ15. A buyer returns defective goods worth Rs. 5,000 and informs the seller that the seller's account in the buyer's books has been debited by that amount. This intimation is a: (PYQ-style)

  1. Credit note
  2. Delivery challan
  3. Goods Received Note
  4. Debit note
Answer

Answer: (d) - A debit note is issued by the buyer when returning goods or claiming a deduction and tells the seller that his account has been debited (side note: under GST law both credit and debit notes are issued by the supplier).

MCQ16. Which details should a stop-payment letter to a bank contain? (PYQ-style)

  1. Cheque number, date, amount and payee's name
  2. The account holder's ATM PIN, for verification
  3. The OTP received on the registered mobile number
  4. Only the account holder's name and signature
Answer

Answer: (a) - The bank needs the cheque number, date, amount and payee to identify the cheque and stop it; no letter should ever contain a PIN, OTP, CVV or password.

MCQ17. Before supplying goods on credit to a new customer, a firm asks the customer's BANK for a statement about the customer's account and general standing. The bank's reply is a: (PYQ-style)

  1. Credit advice
  2. Trade reference
  3. Bank reference
  4. Letter of Credit
Answer

Answer: (c) - A bank reference is a bank's statement about its customer's account and general standing, given on request; a trade reference comes from other suppliers, and a credit advice only reports that an account has been credited.

MCQ18. Which statement about an Office Memorandum (O.M.) is correct? (PYQ-style)

  1. It is written in the third person and has no salutation or complimentary close.
  2. It begins "Dear Shri ...," and ends "Yours sincerely,".
  3. It is used to publish rules and appointments in the Official Gazette.
  4. It announces important policy decisions to the public.
Answer

Answer: (a) - An O.M. (used mainly between Ministries and Departments) is written in the third person, "The undersigned is directed to ...", with no salutation or close; (b) describes a D.O. letter, (c) a notification and (d) a resolution.

MCQ19. An Additional Secretary wants to draw the personal attention of an officer in another department to a matter pending for several months and to get it expedited. The most suitable form is a/an: (PYQ-style)

  1. Notification
  2. Office Order
  3. Resolution
  4. Demi-official letter
Answer

Answer: (d) - A D.O. letter goes from officer to officer in a personal, friendly tone and is used to draw personal attention, expedite a matter or exchange views and information.

MCQ20. Rules framed under an Act are to be published in the Official Gazette. The form of communication used is a: (PYQ-style)

  1. Resolution
  2. Notification
  3. Office Memorandum
  4. Circular
Answer

Answer: (b) - A notification publishes rules, orders, appointments and promotions in the Official Gazette; a resolution also appears in the Gazette but announces important policy decisions or the appointment of committees and commissions.

MCQ21. Consider the following statements about the word "endorsement":

1. In government correspondence, it is a note at the foot of a communication that forwards copies to others or returns a paper to the sender.

2. In banking, it is a signature on the back of a cheque or other negotiable instrument to transfer it.

3. In business correspondence, it is the seller's reply that settles a buyer's complaint.

Which of the statements given above is/are correct? (PYQ-style)

  1. 1 only
  2. 2 only
  3. 1 and 2 only
  4. 1, 2 and 3
Answer

Answer: (c) - "Endorsement" has both the government meaning and the banking meaning; statement 3 describes an adjustment letter.

MCQ22. The head of an office wants to redistribute work among the assistants of a section and to grant leave to two clerks. The proper form of communication is a/an: (PYQ-style)

  1. Resolution
  2. Office Order
  3. Notification
  4. Demi-official letter
Answer

Answer: (b) - An Office Order carries internal administrative instructions such as distribution of work, grant of leave and postings within the office.

MCQ23. Which is the most appropriate opening sentence for a formal reply? (PYQ-style)

  1. Got your letter - thanks a lot!
  2. Please be informed that your letter has been received and the same is under examination.
  3. As per your letter, kindly do the needful.
  4. With reference to your letter No. 45/2026 dated 12 October 2026, I wish to inform you that the matter is under examination.
Answer

Answer: (d) - A formal reply refers to the earlier letter by number and date and uses plain formal language; (a) is informal with an exclamation mark, while (b) and (c) use outdated phrases ("Please be informed", "the same", "do the needful").

MCQ24. Choose the best modern replacement for "Kindly do the needful." (PYQ-style)

  1. Please take the necessary action.
  2. Kindly revert back on this.
  3. Please do the necessary.
  4. Kindly be informed and act.
Answer

Answer: (a) - "Do the needful" is outdated; the modern formal form is "take the necessary action". "Do the necessary" in (c) is the same outdated idiom, "revert back" in (b) should be "reply", and "Kindly be informed" in (d) should be "This is to inform you".

MCQ25. Which sentence is written in acceptable modern formal style? (PYQ-style)

  1. Please find enclosed herewith a copy of the receipt.
  2. Kindly revert back after verifying the same.
  3. I enclose a copy of the receipt.
  4. Please be informed that the same has been paid.
Answer

Answer: (c) - "I enclose" (or "Please find enclosed") is correct; "enclosed herewith" is redundant, "revert back" should be "reply", "the same" used as a pronoun should be "it", and "Please be informed" should be "This is to inform you".

16.2 Fill in the Blanks (25)

Fill in each blank with the correct word or phrase.

FB1. A formal letter that begins "Dear Sir/Madam," should end with the complimentary close "________". (PYQ-style)

Answer

Answer: Yours faithfully, - "Dear Sir/Madam," addresses an official, an organisation or an unknown person, and such letters close with "Yours faithfully," (capital Y, small f, no apostrophe, comma after).

FB2. An Office Memorandum is written in the third person and usually begins: "The ________ is directed to state that ..." (PYQ-style)

Answer

Answer: undersigned - An O.M. does not use "I"; the signing officer is referred to as "the undersigned", and the O.M. has no salutation or close.

FB3. "________ the orders of the District Collector dated 3 September 2026, a special camp will be held on 10 October 2026." (A formal linking phrase formed from the verb "pursue", meaning "in accordance with".) (PYQ-style)

Answer

Answer: Pursuant to (also "In pursuance of") - it means "in accordance with / following" and links an action to an earlier order, letter or rule.

FB4. A seller's preliminary bill, sent before supply so that the buyer can make an advance payment or complete import formalities, is called a ________. (PYQ-style)

Answer

Answer: proforma invoice - It comes before supply, whereas the tax invoice is the actual bill for goods supplied (quantity, price, taxes, total and due date).

FB5. A formal letter to a named person that begins "Dear Mrs. Raman," should end with "________". (PYQ-style)

Answer

Answer: Yours sincerely, - When the salutation names the person ("Dear Mr./Mrs./Ms./Dr. + Surname,"), the close is "Yours sincerely,".

FB6. "The fee of Rs. 500 was paid ________ Receipt No. 4521 dated 5 October 2026." (A Latin word used to cite a document.) (PYQ-style)

Answer

Answer: vide - Latin for "see"; official writing uses it to cite a document by its number and date (plainer English: "see" or "as shown in").

FB7. An official letter from a Government office to a member of the public usually begins: "I am ________ to inform you that ..." (The word shows that the officer writes on the orders of the Government.) (PYQ-style)

Answer

Answer: directed - The officer writes on behalf of the Government, so an official letter begins "I am directed to ..." and ends "Yours faithfully," with the designation.

FB8. The document that travels with the goods from the seller's godown to the buyer's premises, listing the goods sent (it is not a bill), is called the ________. (PYQ-style)

Answer

Answer: delivery challan (also called a delivery note) - It accompanies the goods; on receiving and checking them, the buyer records their receipt in a Goods Received Note (GRN).

FB9. A letter to "The Manager, Southern Commercial Bank, Salem - 636 001", whose name and gender you do not know, should begin with the salutation "________". (PYQ-style)

Answer

Answer: Dear Sir/Madam, - Use it when the receiver's name and gender are not known; it pairs with "Yours faithfully,".

FB10. A formal letter uses "cannot" and "I am" instead of "can't" and "I'm" because ________ are not used in formal letters. (PYQ-style)

Answer
